 xiv

 Final Report: August 2023 Through August 2025
 DE-SC0022583 SBIR Initial Phase II

I. INTRODUCTION
Chapter I is included to provide a concise summary of project results; there most project activity is presented
in limited detail. The remaining chapters contain full descriptions of all project research and development
activity, results, and products produced during the entire duration, both Phase I and Phase IIA. All chapters
except the current one are arranged according to the United States Department of Energy Office (DOE)
reporting standard [1]; the current chapter is included as a supplement to that format for readers requiring
only a summarizing familiarity. The Project Identification page in the preface is required material of the
DOE reporting standard, ordinarily presented as the first chapter. Two appendices are also included, one
describing the comprehensive testing within the project and the other describing gRPC communications
used by the archive management system.

1. Background
Osprey DCS is developing the Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP), a public-domain, open-source
product to support data science, Machine Learning and Artificial Intelligence applications for large particle
accelerator and experimental physics facilities. Funding for MLDP development was obtained from the
United States Department of Energy (DOE) through a Small Business Innovative Research (SBIR) grant.
The project Phase II activity was completed in July of 2025. The project was formally started in July 2022
with an SBIR Phase I award from the United States DOE, Office of High Energy Physics (HEP) [2]. After
the 6-month Phase I effort Osprey DCS was granted a Phase IIA award for continued MLDP development.
The Phase IIA duration was from July 2023 to July 2025. Reported here is a compendium of the research
and development efforts, accomplishments, and project products for the full 2.5-year project duration
(Phase I and II).

2. Product Description
The Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP) is a product providing full-stack support for data science,
Machine Learning, and Artificial Intelligence (ML/AI) applications at particle accelerators and large
experimental physics facilities. It supports ML/AI applications from front-end, high-speed acquisition of
heterogeneous, time-series data, through data archiving and management, to back-end inspection and
analysis. The MLDP embodies a “data-science ready” platform for data analysis and ML/AI applications
in the diagnosis, modelling, control, and optimization of these facilities. It provides data scientists and
applications a consistent, data-centric interface to archive data standardizing implementation and
deployment of ML/AI algorithms to different operations configurations within the same facility, or between
facilities. Being an open-source, public-domain project, the MLDP is intended for broadest possible impact
by increasing accessibility and minimizing the required expertise for installation and operation.
The MLDP can also be deployed at user facilities for experimental data collection, archiving, and analysis.
It is capable of acquisition and archiving of heterogeneous data from experimental equipment (e.g., images,
arrays, structures, etc.) along with experimental hardware configurations (e.g., scalars, tables), control
system process variables, and any metadata required for archive searches, request building, and data
provenance. Thus, the MLDP can manage experimental data through its entire lifecycle, from acquisition
and archiving, through analysis and investigation, to release and final publication.

 1

A conceptual representation of the MLDP and its function within an accelerator facility is shown in Figure
1. Explicitly indicated are data acquisition, real-time monitoring and processing capabilities, a data archive
with full provenance, and archive search and query via a standardized Applications Programming Interface
(API). Since full data provenance is maintained, any data event can be reconstructed from origin and
correlated to other events of interest. This capability allows data scientists to mine archived data for
anomalies, correlations, and sources. Moreover, post-acquisition user annotations and calculations can be
archived along with facility and/or experimental data allowing data scientists to concurrently search and
query stored data and analysis results. Additionally, the MLDP supports real-time monitoring of data events
and conditions, as well as event annotation post-acquisition. This capability is essential for fast ingestion-
stream processing needed in real-time application. Seen complementing the MLDP are common off-the-
shelf tools for building data science and ML/AI applications, such as the Keras, TensorFlow, PyTorch, and
scikit-learn libraries (e.g., see [3] [4] [5] [6]). The MLDP is intended a “facilitator” of data science
applications complementing these common ML/AI tools.

 Figure 1: conceptual diagram of Machine Learning Data Platform
There are 3 primary functions of the MLDP: 1) high-speed data acquisition, 2) archiving and management
of time-correlated, heterogeneous data, and 3) broad search and query capabilities of archived data by time
correlation, metadata, annotations, and other associations relevant to data science and ML/AI applications.
There are additional MLDP features critical in support of such applications: 4) metadata and data attributes
are also archived and accessible maintaining full data provenance, 5) post-acquisition user annotations
where existing archive data sets can be annotated with comments, associations, and calculations obtained
from the archive data then stored within the archive, 6) a Web Application for remote inspection and
interaction with the MLDP archive including annotation (not shown in Figure 3), and 7) online ingestion
stream monitoring and analysis. Note that the annotation feature creates a “value-added” capacity for client
interactions with the data archive, as annotations are available to all other MLDP users.
The MLDP is composed of the following 3 components (see Figure 2 below):
 1. the Aggregator performing data acquisition, correlation, and staging,
 2. the Data Platform responsible for data archiving, management and analysis, and
 3. the Web Application providing remote archive interaction using a web browser.

 2

The Aggregator is typically deployed within Experimental Physics and Industrial Control System (EPICS)
used at most accelerator facilities [7]. The Data Platform is a standalone system featuring an online
distribution utility with simple deployment; it provides the data science functionality post-acquisition.
Osprey DCS customers stressed independent interest in the Aggregator and Data Platform systems
themselves, emphasizing performance, thus, it was a major development focus. The Aggregator now
supports sampling and acquisition rates 250x the original project goal, while the Data Platform now has
reliable transmission and archiving rates 15x the stated Phase II goal, and current projected rates up to 30x.
The Web Application loads into internet web browsers via a separate server, providing remote access and
interaction with the MLDP archive, including archive annotation, without any programming or scripting
requirements.
Figure 2 is a schematic of the MLDP basic structure, other descriptions are available in previous articles
(e.g., see [8] [9], [10]). The Data Platform main repository site also contains substantial online
documentation, including papers, reports, and presentations [11]. An overview of the MLDP is contained
in this chapter while a comprehensive description of the full system is contained in the following chapter.
Referring to Figure 2, the 3 components of the MLDP are shown in schematic form: 1) the Aggregator
performing front-end data acquisition, correlation, and staging, 2) the Data Platform responsible for data
archiving, management, inspection, and analysis, and 3) the Web Application providing remote archive
inspection and interaction using an internet web browser. The Aggregator [12] is deployed within
Experimental Physics and Industrial Control System (EPICS) [7]. The Data Platform is a standalone system
featuring an online distribution utility and simple deployment [13]; it provides the data science functionality
post-acquisition. The Web Application loads into web browsers via a separate server, providing remote
access and interaction with the MLDP archive without any programming or scripting requirements [14].

 Figure 2: MLDP composite diagram with client relationships and deployment

Figure 2 provides a view the MLDP basic architecture, along with deployment and client relationships. The
Aggregator system is distributed within the EPICS environment containing a Central Aggregator for

 3

coalescing and staging of acquired data. The Data Platform consists of multiple Core Services [15]
interacting through an independent gRPC communication framework [16]. Each service has a gRPC
Application Programming Interface (API) for interaction with clients, the archive, or each other. Data
Platform clients are divided into Data Providers and Data Consumers (these are simply definitions
providing context within Data Platform). Data Providers are arbitrary sources of time-correlated,
heterogeneous data (conforming to the ingestion API) contributing to the MLDP archive; note that the
Aggregator is one such Data Provider. Data Consumers are any parties interested in the MLDP archive,
such as physicists, engineers, data scientists, and applications. Also seen in Figure 2 is a Remote User
interacting with the archive through the Web Application; it is a sub-class of Data Consumer.

3. Project Technical Objectives
The following is a list of the major technical objectives stated in the initial project scope:
 1. Performance: Data transport and archiving rates throughout the MLDP were to support, at a
 minimum, 4,000 scalar signals at a sample rate of 1 kHz. This translates to a data rate of 32 MBps.
 2. Heterogeneous Data Storage: Time-series data of differing types is to be stored and retrieved
 concurrently. This includes all common scalar types as well as complex types such as arrays,
 structures, and images.
 3. Archive Annotations: The MLDP was to support user annotation of archive data in the form of
 comments, data associations, and post-ingestion calculations.
 4. Installation and Deployment: The MLDP was to be deployable at existing particle accelerator
 and user facilities.
 5. Advanced Data Sciences: Phase II contained the addition of “Advanced Data Sciences” use cases,
 including online ingestion datastream access and processing for real-time applications.
Phase I addressed overall feasibility with the creation of a prototype system. The first three objectives were
addressed immediately in the design phase of Phase II. The remaining objectives were viable as project
development continued. The first goal is a straightforward performance goal. For example, for 8-byte
floating point values we must be able to acquire and archive 32 Kbytes every millisecond. More simply,
the data rate must be minimally 32 Mbytes/second (MBps). The data archive must store heterogeneous
data, that is, data of differing types. This includes scalar data of different types such as Booleans, integers,
floats, and character strings as well as complex data types such as numeric arrays, tables, data structures,
and images. These differing data types must be correlated and available within the same result set of a
single query request. Additionally, the MLDP maintains a “metadata archive” containing properties of the
data and providers acquired during sampling, ingestion, and archiving. Metadata is used for archive
searches, query building, query refinement, and to maintain provenance of the archived data.
Users of the MLDP must be able to annotate archived data with notes, data associations, and other artifacts
both during acquisition and post-acquisition. The overall objective is to provide users with the ability to
annotate the archive with information obtained through, inspection, data mining, machine-learning, and
other data processing applications post-acquisition. These post-ingestion results are then available to all
other users of the platform providing a “value added” feature for archive interaction. The wide data query
objective is more general; there a robust archive search and request building capability is the goal. The
ability to request heterogeneous data via time-correlations, metadata, relationships, annotations, and other
data properties, within a single query request must exist. The data requests should not be restricted by the
data type, data source, or time range. Additionally, single requests containing disparate types such as scalars,
arrays, images, and data structures can be queried according to their common properties, such as hardware
system, equipment, alarm status, or user attributes.

 4

Early in Phase I the value of developing the MLDP Core Services (or “Data Platform”) as fully standalone
was recognized. All that is required is that Data Providers conform to the available ingestion APIs. Thus,
any facility can utilize the archiving and search capabilities of the MLDP independent of EPICS; thus, the
Data Platform system itself is versatile having broader applicability. Originally the Core Services and Web
Application server were to be installed manually on a site-by-site basis. However, the new design facilitated
a distribution utility which could be highly automated. The initial distribution system was built simply to
facilitate development within the team. However, its value in distributing project results and products was
immediate; thus, its development goal became formalized.
Finally, ingestion stream processing is the inline, real-time processing of algorithm data within the MLDP
data stream. It provides the most rapid data availability for online machine learning applications. Rather
than querying for datasets from the archive, applications identify datasets and conditions of interest a priori.
The intent is to then provide fast first-level processing of incoming data for specialized clients (applications
not requiring fast processing could simply monitor control system Process Variables).
The ability to access the MLDP archive remotely was also recognized in Phase I. This objective is realized
with the complementary Web Application providing remote archive access using only a standard internet
web browser. The development of the Web Application became a project goal; to provide universal access
to the MLDP archive from remote locations via a public URL without any programming or scripting.

5. Project Summary
This subsection provides a high-level summary of the project research and development activities, the
technical achievements, and the outstanding areas that remain after completion. Those interested in detailed
descriptions of these areas should refer to the next chapter. Details of project status and activities were also
published during project duration in the 2024 IPAC24 conference proceedings [8] [9], the 2025
ICALEPCS2025 conferences proceedings [10], the Data Platform online documentation [11], the fall 2024
EPICS meeting [17], and in previous Performance Reports [18] [19] [20] [21].
 5.1. Project Overview and Evolution
A MLDP prototype was built in Phase 1, completed at the end of 2022 [22]. It was originally intended for
installation at facilities utilizing the EPICS system, as the front end (Aggregator) was EPICS based.
However, the archive management component of the prototype, then termed the Datastore, was built
standalone and universally deployable. The Web Application was initially built as a development tool for
inspection and verification of the data archive without formal API. However, it was found to have
independent merit allowing anyone, developers and data scientists alike, remote access to the data archive
without programming or scripting requirements or skills. Thus, there were 3 independent subcomponents
of the prototype; this basic configuration remains to present day.
The MLDP prototype demonstrated proof of principle and a viable technical approach within the problem
domain, however, it was not full featured and had serious issues regarding performance and reliability. In
Phase I we exploited any available third-party systems and technologies to expedite prototype development.
The approach allowed immediate evaluations identifying areas requiring future attention. Exhaustive
evaluations of the prototype identified a comprehensive set of activities required to meet project technical
objectives [22]. The areas of performance, reliability, and ease of use became a focus of Phase II.

 6

The first operational Data Platform, Release 1.0, achieved an ingestion rate of ~100 MBps, providing a
comfortable overhead of 3x over the target rate. The ingestion and archiving systems were again redesigned
in Release 1.5 adding an additional factor 2 for maximum ingestion rates of 200 MBps. The accompanying
Java API Library (“JAL”, see below) was then redesigned to allow multiple, concurrent data streams for
ingestion, again increasing ingestion performance. Currently, the Data Platform has confirmed data rates
of over 500 MBps over continuous, sustained ingestion yielding over 15x the Phase II goal. We expect
another factor 2x increase in release 1.10, which has undergone preliminary performance testing. The core
code base has seen 3 major revisions, now implementing a Common Core Service Framework (see below).
New release versions are available on the Data Platform main repository site “Release” section [13]. A
standalone demonstration application is included in version 1.11.0 which was released after project
completion; the application demonstrates advanced interaction and collaboration of the Data Platform Core
Services.
 5.2. Technical Approach
The most promising approach to meet the above technical objectives was a complete redesign and rebuild
of the prototype MLDP archiving system. The reduction of 3rd party reliance was also a Phase II technical
objective motivated by the open-source nature of the project and the need for performance improvements.
Additionally, some 3rd party system did not fully support the project requirements for the MLDP.
The initial Phase II effort involved technology evaluations in support of the archive system design [18],
now termed the Data Platform (i.e., rather than the Datastore). Design emphasis was on performance and
a commercial-grade code base. The Data Platform archiving system component is now comprised of the
following systems:
 • Java [23] – The Java programming language offers a reasonable trade-off between performance,
 development effort, and ease of deployment. C++ provided the best performance but at a
 significant development increase and host ecosystem requirements. Java also provides a platform
 independent deployment solution. Java project management is done with Maven [24].
 • gRPC [25] – It was decided to maintain the archive system as a standalone system, independent of
 EPICS. The gRPC library and its accompanying support tools (e.g., Protocol Buffers) provides a
 fast, open-source, platform-independent, and language neutral communication framework that
 supports modularity and extensibility. The latter feature being particularly important for site
 integration, tool building, and Advanced Data Science efforts.
 • MongoDB [26]– The MongoDB database system is now exclusive for all archiving, including
 time-series data, metadata¸ and archive annotations. Previously it was utilized solely for metadata
 storage due to its document-based storage structure. With redesign of the time-series data archive,
 the MongoDB system was found to have exceptional performance. Additionally, it is public
 domain and installs on most platforms.
The modern architecture of the MLDP was shown in Figure 2. It is important to note that a new naming
convention was adopted for the archive management system; Datastore was replaced by Data Platform
(abbreviated “DP”). Since the Datastore prototype and all support was abandoned, it was prudent to identify
the situation as such. The new convention clarifies the revised component from the prototype and identified
the new code repositories. As indicated in the diagram of Figure 2, the new Data Platform design is
composed of independent, collaborating services communicating through gRPC. The new design is
performance-based, modular, and distributed. Additionally, the gRPC communications framework
facilitates the service independence and the distributed, service-based nature of the MLDP archiving system.
The first Data Platform implementation incorporating the design of Figure 2, Release 1.0, was completed
late in 2023. It consisted of an operational Ingestion Service with testing utility; it supported only scalar
time-series data.

 7

Many third-party systems and components were eliminated from the new design, including the InfluxDB
[27] time-series database and the Spring and SpringBoot application frameworks [28]. Although designed
specifically for time-series data management, the InfluxDB database system was functionality inappropriate
for heterogeneous data storage. The Spring and SpringBoot frameworks hindered performance goals and
obfuscated operation of the Data Platform core services. We note that C++ gRPC provided exceptional
performance, unfortunately, it is the most difficult to implement and deploy. Nonetheless, specialized
MLDP components can be built in C++, particularly attractive for ingestion stream interaction and real-
time processing. Additionally, C++ would be the most suitable environment for building an EPICS
Interface (or “pvAccess to gRPC translator”) to the Data Platform, which is discussed in the next chapter.
Thus, Data Platform C++ gRPC communications was well documented [29].
A new approach was also pursued for the Aggregator system. The initial Aggregator relied primarily on
high-performance software for all data collection, alignment and staging. In the new approach front-end
data acquisition and alignment were combined and pushed upstream into the hardware level, rather than
relying on software. The new front-end hardware system, the Osprey 250k, supports of exceptional
sampling rates, up to 250 kHz, 250x that of original project goals. Additionally, it is no longer EPICS
dependent and can be connected directly to hardware; although it is typically deployed within an EPICS
environment for data transport and staging once it has been acquired and correlated.
Aggregator Front End – Osprey 250k
Data acquisition and correlation is now performed at the hardware level within the new Aggregator system.
The new Aggregator front end is designated the Osprey 250k and represents the first hardware product
within the Osprey DCS product line. It is a significant accomplishment within the Phase II project. The
Osprey 250k is fully open-source where documentation, hardware design, and installation and deployment
instructions are publicly available in the repository quarz-daq-250-24 [12]. To promote the project’s open-
source nature the repository also specifies all required hardware components and vendors, assembly
instructions and testing procedures, and includes all necessary firmware for system operation. Here Osprey
DCS is attempting to promote open-source hardware development as well as software development.
Data Platform Communications Framework
As implied in Figure 2, the Data Platform Communications Framework is gRPC-based. It is available in
the Data Platform public repository dp-grpc [16]. The external interfaces and data exchange messages are
defined in the Protocol Buffers meta-language (“Protobuf”) used to create high-level language interfaces to
the gRPC library (see [30] for language description). The dp-grpc repository is configured to automatically
build and locally install the Java language implementation however, Data Platform clients can download
the repository and build the communications framework in the language of choice using the Protocol
Buffers meta-compiler “protoc”. Figure 2 shows the external Protocol Buffers interfaces of the
Communications Framework for each of the Data Platform Corese Services discussed below.
Data Platform High-Level Language API Libraries
Not shown in Figure 2 is the Java API Library (JAL) providing high-level Java programming language
interaction with the Data Platform [31]. The objective is to isolate clients from the low-level Procol Buffers
interfaces within the Data Platform Communications Framework (i.e., dp-grpc). Being Java based, JAL
was used extensively throughout project duration for testing and designing of Data Platform APIs and
services, as well as performance evaluations. The library contains a framework for building JAL
applications and a suite of Data Platform testing applications; see online documentation for details [31]. A
Python API Library (PAL) is intended for future development as many data science libraries are written in
Python.
NOTE: The Data Platform installation contains a suite of benchmarking and validation applications,
primarily for validating and basic tuning of service configurations on the local platform. The JAL library
contains applications for comprehensive performance evaluations and tuning of the Data Platform. As of
this writing the current JAL release is version 1.8 which is compatible with Data Platform release 1.8 (JAL

 8

release 1.10 is in construction). The Data Platform release 1.10 benchmarking applications indicate at least
a doubling of data transport rates from the earlier release (i.e., from 500 MBps to > 1 GBps); however,
these rates have not yet been confirmed with the JAL performance applications.
Data Platform Core Services Architecture
The Data Platform contains the Core Services for managing the MLDP archive. Referring to Figure 2, seen
are the Ingestion Service, the Query Service, the Annotation Service, and the Ingestionstream Service
(abbreviated Ingeststream in the diagram). Each service performs an essential function of the Data
Platform and offers its client a well-defined API for interaction. Note in Figure 2 that the Ingestion Service,
Query Service, and Annotation Service have direct access to the MLDP data archive, while the
Ingestionstream Service connects to the Ingestion Service via the gRPC framework. The code base for the
Core Services is in the dp-service repository [32].
 • Ingestion Service – The Ingestion Service provides the high-speed data ingestion and archiving
 functions with an interface based upon a heterogeneous “data frame.” Any Data Provider
 conforming to the Ingestion Service API can supply data to the Data Platform, creating its
 standalone nature. The Ingestion Service also offers a subscribe/publish service to the live data
 stream, used by the Ingestionstream Service but also available directly to clients.
 • Query Service – The Query Service is the archive interface seen by Data Consumers, such as data
 scientists, accelerator physicists, and applications programmers. For enhanced performance, query
 processing can be distributed between the Query Service and clients. Specifically, as well as tabular
 queries, the Query Service offers streaming “raw” time-series data queries where data reassembly
 occurs on client platforms (e.g., the JAL library). The design off-loads some Query Service
 processing increasing overall performance as the service supports multiple, simultaneous clients.
 • Annotation Service – The archive annotations capability is managed by the Annotation Service
 with an independent API. Clients can annotate archived data with comments, associations between
 archived datasets, and calculations obtained from the archived data. The Annotation Service also
 supports data exporting where archive data and annotations can be exported in various common
 formats such as CSV, HDF5, and Excel workbooks. The export feature also allows clients to
 simultaneously export archived data alongside calculation annotations for further local analysis and
 processing within a common time series.
 • Ingestionstream Service – The Ingestionstream Service provides Data Consumers access to the
 ingestion datastream for real-time applications. Currently the Ingestionstream Service supports
 ingestion stream monitoring of Data Events where clients can specify a Data Condition triggering
 the event and request a data set within the response notification. For example, a client can request
 notification when a Process Variable exceeds a threshold (i.e., the trigger) then receive diagnostic
 data directly from the ingestion stream within a given time window.
As implied in Figure 2 the new Data Platform architecture supports deployment on multiple host servers.
For added performance deployment can utilize horizontal scaling of Data Platform where one or more Core
Services can be deployed on dedicated servers. Likewise, the MongoDB database system can also utilize
multiple servers.
Core Services Common Framework
Each Data Platform Core Service described above shares the same design and implementation framework,
specifically the Core Service Common Framework. The framework is robust, efficient, and relatively
straightforward. There was significant development effort here and we stress its innovation.
The Core Service Common Framework provides a set of base classes and resources for building multi-
threaded service applications based upon the Data Platform Communications Framework. There are two
entry base classes from which all services derive, one is the gRPC library entry point which intercepts and
responds to all gRPC traffic, the other is the service application entry point containing the main() method

 9

by which all Java applications are launched. The latter performs all configuration, startup, and cleanup
operations and is also responsible for managing the former class initialization, configuration, exception
handling, and finalization operations. With the framework each Core Service is responsible for
implementing a “service handler” performing all operations defined in its API. The service handler
performs its API operations through delegation to “service jobs”, one for each operation. The results of a
service job are passed to a dispatcher which relays the data back to the gRPC interface class. Within the
Core Services Common Framework there are base classes and resources for building service handlers,
service jobs, and dispatchers. Details for the Core Services Common Framework are presented in the next
chapter and the Data Platform online documentation [11].
 5.3. Technical Achievements
Most technical achievements were scoped within project requirements. However, others were pursued as
opportunities arose, such as the hardware approach to data correlation, archive data exporting, Data
Platform deployment and ease of use, and particularly, the outstanding performance now seen by the MLDP.
The following is a list major technical achievements and project accomplishments:
 • Osprey 250k: The Osprey 250k is the first hardware product offered by Osprey DCS. It is
 completely open source where design specifications, required hardware components, assembly and
 testing instructions, and all firmware is publicly available [12]. It represents an innovative
 approach where acquisition and correlation are combined upstream at the hardware level.
 • Performance: Performance of the MLDP has greatly exceeded original objectives. The
 Aggregator performance now supports sampling rates up to 250 kHz for 1024 signals, a factor 100x
 improvement. The Osprey 250k has been successfully deployed at NASA’s Artemis II Neil
 Armstrong test facility [33] [34]. Data rates for the Data Platform archive management system are
 now confirmed at 500 MBps on development platforms, a factor 15x beyond original goals. Data
 rates up to 1 GBps are projected with the recent improvements of Release 1.10.
 • Communications Framework: Throughout the project, service interfaces saw repeated
 refactoring to accommodate new features, revised designs, and performance improvements. As of
 current Release 11.0 the Data Platform communications framework is relatively mature and
 supports all features within Phase II scope except Algorithm Plugins (discussed below).
 • Core Service Common Framework: We consider the Core Services Common Framework
 discussed above a technical achievement as it facilitated the Data Platform maintenance, robustness,
 upgradeability, and the extreme performance characteristics.
 • Data Archive: The MLDP robustly supports archiving of heterogeneous time-series data.
 Moreover, the MLDP maintains an archive of metadata in support of advanced query building and
 data provenance.
 • Archive Annotations: Post-ingestion annotation of time-series data is fully supported. Archived
 data can be annotated with user comments, associations between datasets, and calculations (e.g.,
 sourced from archive data). Annotations are then available to all other MLDP users for further
 inspection and analysis. Note that calculations obtained from archive data can be associated with
 the original data, exported alongside source data, and used for additional calculation annotations.
 • Data Exporting: Clients can export archive data, metadata, and annotations to local platforms in
 common formats (CVS, HDF5, Excel) for local analysis and processing.
 • Ingestionstream Monitoring: The Ingestion Service provides direct access to the ingestion
 datastream in the form of a subscribe/publish service for individual Process Variable (PV) values.
 Additionally, the Ingestionstream Service provides access to the ingestion datastream in the form
 of subscribe/publish notifications to user-specific “Data Events”. Whenever a Data Condition is

 10

 seen within the datastream, the client is notified and supplied any requested data within the event
 definition.
 • Installation and Deployment: The Data Platform has a formal installation and deployment system
 available at the main repository site [11]. Deployment consists of downloading a distribution
 archive and unzipping into the install location. The distribution contains scripts for starting and
 stopping services, monitoring service status, and utilities for benchmarking service performance on
 host platforms. Installation instructions and ecosystem requirements are available online.
 • Data Archive Administration: Throughout the project, tools for verification and validation of the
 Data Platform operations and performance were developed in conjunction with each service and
 ship within the distribution. These tools constitute the Administration Library. Although library
 components are available, no formal Archive Administration tool has, of yet, been constructed (see
 below).
 5.4. Outstanding Areas
Outstanding projects areas resulted primarily from an expanded (and unexpected) effort required for archive
annotation implementation, inclusion of data exporting, and the decision to target performance whenever
presented. The first three topics below were scoped within the Initial Phase II Project Narrative. The
remaining topics are included essentially as an activity “wish list” for future pursuit.
  • Full Integration: The Aggregator and Data Platform were developed independently and both
 subsystems have been tested and deployed independently. Currently the Aggregator stages tabulated
 data to HDF5 files, which are then independently read into the Ingestion Service (e.g., see [33] and
 [35] [36]). Full integration of the Aggregator with the Data Platform has yet to be implemented. A
 potential solution is described in Chapter VI, Subsection VI.2.1 (see Figure 63) where an EPICS
 Interface is deployed as an EPICS application [37], perhaps within a Soft IOC if an independent server
 is required [38]. This is a very general solution where any NTTable instance can be passed to the
 interface and would then be ingested by the Data Platform.
  • Python API Library (PAL): A Python language library was intended for back-end Data Platform
 support as there are many public-domain Python libraries supporting the data sciences (e.g., see [3],
 [4], [5], [6]). This activity was deferred until the Data Platform gRPC API was mature. The Java API
 Library (JAL) was used in the design and development of the gRPC API (since the dp-grpc repository
 directory supports Java [16]). Development of the PAL client library is independent and self-
 contained; it can be built at any time, expediated from experience with JAL library implementation.
  • Algorithm Plugins: An intended feature of the Data Platform was an “algorithm plugin” framework
 where exceptional real-time algorithms could be attached to the ingestion datastream. However, it was
 determined that such a framework was beyond the scope of low-level gRPC communications and,
 consequently, the Data Platform Communications Framework. However, the data event monitoring
 feature was successfully implemented in gRPC with the Ingestionstream Service. It is our intent to
 implement an algorithm plugin framework in the JAL library which already has an applications
 framework. Moreover, the Java language is more appropriate for such a framework being high-level
 and having good performance characteristics as a compiled language.
  • Load Testing: Advanced load testing of the Data Platform has yet to be performed. The objective here
 is to determine the maximum archive capacity of the Data Platform in is basic configuration; that is,
 under what conditions it “breaks.” Performance evaluations continued throughout the project but did
 not stress the archive capacity. An advanced Data Simulator capable of producing a variety of ingestion
 scenarios was built during the project [39]. It can be configured to supply heterogeneous data from
 multiple, concurrent Data Providers at differing rates, different data types, executed over extended time
 scales. However, as of this writing, load testing has not been performed. Load testing would determine
 appropriate configurations for the horizonal scaling shown in Figure 2 where multiple servers are

 11

 employed. Note that the MongoDB database system can also be deployed over multiple servers with
 archive “sharding” which increases both capacity and lookup speeds.
  • Archive Administrator Tool: The intent is to create a client GUI application for the entire suite of
 archive administration applications and tools.
 5.5. Project Timeline and Milestones
We provide a brief outline of the project timeline with dates and notable achievements. The outline below
can be compared with the Phase II performance schedule presented in Table 1 and Table 2. A more detailed
project timeline is provided in the next chapter, Subsection II.2.2.
Note: The Data Platform uses “semantic versioning,” where version numbers appear in the format “M.m.p.”
There “M” is the Major version number, “m” is the minor version number, and “p” is the patch number.
The Major version number for the Data Platform is assigned “1” until Phase II completion; thus, all versions
for the Data Platform so far have Major version 1. The major version for all Phase I products was
retroactively assigned “0” indicating a beta system. All releases throughout Phase II are primarily minor
releases with an occasional patch release. Note that, although now public domain and developmentally
mature, any Data Platform release with major version “1” has an API that is subject to change when
warranted.

 5.6. Conclusion
The MLDP is operational supporting data science operations at large particle accelerator and experimental
physics facilities. All project results and products are open-source and available through public-domain
repositories. The system achieved exceptional performance, orders of magnitude beyond original project
goals. The Aggregator system was augmented with specialized front-end hardware combining high-speed
acquisition and correlation, the Osprey 250k. The Data Platform component managing the MLDP data
archive is independent and can be downloaded and installed locally from the main repository site. Hardware
component lists and specifications, assembly and testing instructions, and firmware for the Osprey 250k
are available online. Documentation for the Data Platform is exhaustive and available online. Most topics
within project scope were completed within the 2.5-year duration including significant additions regarding
performance, data exporting, deployment and hardware development. Some outstanding areas remain for
future development as resources allow.

 14

II. ACCOMPLISHMENTS
Summary
A project summary was presented in the previous chapter. However, to make this chapter complete and
self-referential we repeat a portion of the material in this chapter. Specifically, we restate the project goals
(with additional detail), performance schedule, and technical approach used for these goals. We also
provide a brief description of the Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP) and its function within an
accelerator or experimental physics facility. Figure 4 is again included in this chapter since it provides a
convenient reference for the MLDP structure and intended operation.
Project objectives remained consistent through the duration. Performance considerations have guided much
activity since inception and exceed project goals with overall data rates now confirmed at more than 15x
those originally stated with recent improvements showing projected gains of 30x the original objectives.
Additionally, sampling and acquisition rates are now 250x that at project start due to a new hardware
approach within the front-end Aggregator system. Much project activity proceeded according to the
original schedule. A timeline synopsis of project activity appears in Subsection 2.2 with topic details
expanded in the following subsections.
Project accomplishments addressed those identified in the original Phase IIA project scope, with some
additional accomplishments and a few omissions. For example, wherever an unforeseen opportunity to
implement additional performance gains occurred it was exploited, which yielded the exceptional
performance numbers now seen by the MLDP. This is particularly relevant for the development of Osprey
DCS’s first hardware product, the Osprey 250k; the product provides the exceptional sampling, acquisition,
and correlation capabilities now available with the MLDP. Additional features were also included in the
MLDP that were not originally scoped, such as the ability to export archive data to local platforms, a formal
distribution system for local installation and deployment, a Java language client library, and a MLDP
demonstration application. The annotations feature of the MLDP required additional development effort
beyond that originally anticipated; this effort forced the decision to forego the building of a Python language
client library for the MLDP backend. The choice for omission was motivated by the fact that Python library
development is self-contained and could be performed at any future time. Nonetheless, the archive
annotations capability was completed beyond project scope; full archive annotations including comments,
associations, and post-ingestion calculations from archive data are possible along with the concurrent
exporting of calculation annotations with time-series data. The Advanced Data Sciences component of the
project has also been necessarily modified. The Advanced Data Sciences system supports direct monitoring
and processing of ingestion stream data for real-time applications. Direct ingestion stream monitoring and
processing of Data Events is still available; however, it was found that algorithm plugins for ingestion
stream processing is beyond the scope of the low-level communications framework, specifically that of
gRPC. Thus, it has been moved to the companion high-level Java programming language library.
For the sake of chapter completeness, a project overview is again presented in Subsection II.1.3 (i.e., in
additional that in Chapter I. The overview describes the motivation, scope, and approaches used within the
full project (i.e., Phase I and Phase II) including the conceptual diagram of Figure 3. It ends with a
description of the final product, the Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP), in its current state as
represented by Figure 4. These figures provide context for most activity descriptions in the sequel and thus
are referenced throughout the chapter. Readers of Chapter I, or those already familiar with the project can
skip to Section II.2 without loss of continuity, but should be confident with Figure 4. Details of project
status and activities were also published during project duration in the 2024 IPAC24 conference
proceedings [8] [9], the 2025 ICALEPCS2025 conferences proceedings [10], the Data Platform online
documentation [11], the fall 2024 EPICS meeting [17], and in previous Performance Reports [18] [19] [20]
[21].

 15

1. Project Goals
Numerous project objectives were identified in the initial Phase I proposal and in the Phase II Project
Narrative. As the project evolved other objectives were identified and included in the Phase II Year 1
Report [19] and previous Performance Reports [20] [21]. Typically, these were sub-objectives
supplementing the major technical objectives.
 1.1. Project Goals
For completeness, major technical objectives for the Phase II project are again listed below, followed by a
brief synopsis for the objective.
 1. Acquire and archive 4,000 signals at a sample rate of 1 kHz.
 2. Archive and query time-correlated heterogeneous data.
 3. Support for archive metadata and data provenance.
 4. Archive annotations: storage and management of processed data obtained post ingestion.
 5. Broad query capabilities of archive data for data science, that is, request building and data mining.
 6. Develop the archive management system, the Data Platform component, as a fully standalone. It
 should be control-system independent, deployable at any facility, and adaptable to a variety of Data
 Providers. (Applications developed for the MLDP then have broader applicability.)
 7. Remote inspection and interaction with archive (e.g., outside control room, or completely off site).
 8. Formal installation and deployment utility for the MLDP archive management system (Data
 Platform).
 9. Fast ingestion stream access and processing for real-time applications.
1. The first goal is a straightforward performance goal. For example, let the signals be 8-byte float values
then we must be able to acquire and archive 32 Kbytes every millisecond. More simply, the data sampling
and ingestion rates must be minimally 32 Mbytes/second (MBps).
2. The data archive must store heterogeneous data, that is, data of differing types. This includes scalar data
of different types such as Booleans, integers, floats, and character strings as well as complex data types
such as numeric arrays, tables, data structures, and images. These differing data types must be correlated
and available within the same result set of a single query request.
3. The MLDP maintains a primary archive of correlated, time-series data for use by data scientists and
applications. It shall also maintain a “metadata archive” containing attributes and properties of the time-
series data, the acquisition and archiving process, and the sources of archived data. Metadata is then
available for archive inspection, request building, and data source identification. The metadata is also used
to maintain provenance of all archived time-series data.
4. Users of the MLDP must be able to annotate archived data with notes, associations between datasets, and
other artifacts both during acquisition and post-acquisition, including post-ingestion calculations obtained
from the archive data itself. The overall objective is to provide users with the ability to annotate the archive
with information obtained by data mining, machine-learning, and other data processing applications post-
acquisition. These post-ingestion results are then available to all other users of the platform providing a
“value added” feature for archive interaction.
5. The wide data query objective is more general, the ability to request heterogeneous data via time-
correlations, metadata, relationships, annotations, and other data properties. Specifically, the MLDP is to
support general “request building” features that allow clients to perform generalized archive searches.
Moreover, data requests are not restricted by data type, data source, or time range. Finally, single requests

 16

containing disparate types such as scalars, arrays, images, and data structures can be queried according to
their common properties, such as hardware system, equipment, alarm status, or user attributes.
6. Early in Phase I the value of developing the MLDP archive management system (the “Data Platform”)
as fully standalone was recognized. All that is required is that data providers conform to the available
ingestion API. Thus, any facility can utilize the archiving and data management capabilities of the MLDP
independent of EPICS; thus, the Data Platform system itself is versatile having broader applicability.
7. The ability to access the MLDP archive remotely was also recognized in Phase I. This objective is
realized with the complementary Web Application providing remote archive access using only a standard
internet web browser. The development of the Web Application became a project goal; to provide universal
access to the MLDP archive from remote locations via a public URL without any programming or scripting.
8. Originally the Data Platform and Web Application server were to be installed manually on a site-by-site
basis. However, the new design facilitated a distribution utility which could be highly automated. The
initial distribution system was built simply to facilitate development within the team. However, its value
in distributing project results and products was immediate; thus, its development goal became formalized.
9. Ingestion datastream access provides the most rapid data availability. For online machine learning
applications, or facility control applications in general, rapid availability is essential. Rather than querying
for datasets from the MLDP archive, applications identify datasets and conditions within the ingestion
datastream a priori. The intent is to then provide direct access to these datasets and, additionally, fast first-
level processing of incoming data for specialized clients (applications not requiring fast processing could
simply monitor process variables).

 1.3. Project Overview: The Machine Learning Data Platform
For context and self-containment a concise project overview is presented below; readers of Chapter I or
those already familiar with the project can skip to the Section II.2, although inspection of Figure 3 and
Figure 4 is warranted as they are referenced in the sequel. The overview is essentially a brief chronological
summary of project activity. The overview ends with the current architecture and deployment of the
Machine Learning Data Platform as presented in Figure 4.
A conceptual representation of the MLDP and its function within an accelerator facility is shown in Figure
3. Explicitly indicated
are data acquisition, real-
time monitoring and
processing capabilities, a
data archive with full
provenance, and archive
search and query via a
standardized
Applications
Programming Interface
(API). Since full data
provenance is
maintained, any data
event can be
reconstructed from
origin and correlated to
other events of interest. Figure 3: conceptual diagram of Machine Learning Data Platform
This capability allows
data scientists to mine archived data for anomalies, correlations, and sources. Moreover, post-acquisition
user annotations and calculations can be archived along with the facility data allowing data scientists to
concurrently search and query stored data and analysis results. Additionally, the MLDP supports real-time

 18

monitoring of data events and conditions, as well as event annotation post-acquisition. This capability is
essential for fast ingestion-stream processing needed in real-time application. Seen complementing the
MLDP are common off-the-shelf tools for building data science and AI/ML applications, such as the Keras,
TensorFlow, PyTorch, and scikit-learn libraries (e.g., see [3] [4] [5] [6]).
There are 3 primary functions of the MLDP: 1) high-speed data acquisition, 2) archiving and management
of time-correlated, heterogeneous data, and 3) broad search and query capabilities of archived data by time
correlation and other associations relevant to data science and Machine Learning/Artificial Intelligence
(ML/AI) applications. There are additional MLDP features critical in support of such applications: 4)
metadata and data attributes are also archived maintaining full data provenance, 5) post-acquisition user
annotations where existing archive data sets can be annotated with comments, associations, and calculations
obtained from the archive data then stored within the archive, 6) a Web Application for remote inspection
and interaction with the MLDP archive including annotation (not shown in Figure 3), and 7) online
ingestion stream monitoring and analysis. Note that the annotation feature creates a “value-added” capacity
for client interactions with the data archive, as annotations are available to all other MLDP users.
The MLDP is composed of 3 components (see Figure 4 below): 1) the Aggregator performing data
acquisition, alignment, and staging, 2) the Data Platform responsible for data archiving, management and
analysis, and 3) the Web Application providing remote archive interaction using a web browser. The
Aggregator is deployed within Experimental Physics and Industrial Control System (EPICS) used at most
accelerator facilities [7]. The Data Platform is a standalone system featuring an online distribution utility
with simple deployment; it provides the data science functionality post-acquisition. Osprey DCS customers
stressed interest in the Data Platform itself emphasizing performance, thus, it was a major development
focus now with reliable transmission and archiving rates 15x the stated Phase II goal, and current projected
rates up to 30x. The Web Application loads into internet web browsers via a separate server, providing
remote access and interaction with the MLDP archive, including archive annotations, without any
programming or scripting requirements.
A MLDP prototype was completed in Phase 1 while Phase II pursued a redesign and rebuilding of the Data
Platform component with emphasis on performance, reliability, ease of use, and additional use-cases. The
Aggregator was also greatly improved with the development of a hardware-based front-end data acquisition
and alignment system. The Web Application was also rebuilt for robustness and additional features. A
standalone demonstration application was built and included in the last release upon Phase IIA completion.
Technical Objectives
The MLDP prototype demonstrated proof of principle and a viable technical approach within the problem
domain, however, it was not full featured and had serious issues regarding performance and reliability. The
prototype evaluations identified a comprehensive set of activities required for product commercialization
[22]. The following is a list of major technical objectives addressed in Phase II activities:
 • Performance: Data transport and archiving within the archive management system was orders of
 magnitude below project goal (~1 MBps). This was a primary focus for Phase II.
 • Heterogeneous Data: Archiving of complex types (e.g., arrays, structures, etc.) was not reliable;
 data were error prone, missing, and/or inconsistent.
 • Archive Annotations: The prototype had no formal method for user annotation of archive data.
 • Installation and Deployment: The prototype had no formal distribution system and was difficult
 to deploy (it required the cloning and building of 7 separate repositories along with site
 configuration).
 • Advanced Data Sciences: Phase II contains “Advanced Data Sciences” features including
 ingestion stream processing and analysis. This is only possible when the above issues are addressed.

 19

The most promising approach to meet all the above technical objectives was a complete rebuild of the
archive management system, that is, the Datastore prototype. Thus, the Phase II effort involved technology
evaluations and subsequent redesign of the Datastore using a services-based architecture, then rebuilding
the Datastore with emphasis on performance and commercial-grade code base. The reduction of 3rd party
reliance was also a technical objective motivated by the open-source nature of the project, as well as
performance improvement. Henceforth any 3rd party systems were to be public domain and preferably also
open source. The Web Application was also to be rebuilt to a commercial-grade product.
Technical Approach
An extensive evaluation of technologies and techniques was made at the start of Phase II [18]. In particular,
the design studies included extensive evaluations and benchmarking of available technologies and methods.
The focus of these efforts was based upon the following considerations for the Core Services design and
implementation (in order of importance):
 1. Performance
 2. Modularity/dependency
 3. Development effort
 4. Ease of deployment
Following the evaluations the new Datastore design is comprised of the following systems:
 • Java [23] – The Java programming language offers a reasonable tradeoff between performance,
 development effort, and ease of deployment. C++ provided the best performance but at a
 significant increase in development and deployment requirements, particularly so for gRPC. Java
 provides a platform independent deployment solution. We are currently using Java version 21 and
 the Maven utility for Java project management [24].
 • gRPC [25] – It was decided to maintain the Datastore as a standalone system independent of EPICS.
 The gRPC system provides a fast, open-source, platform-independent, and language neutral
 communication framework that offers modularity and extensibility. The latter feature being
 particularly important for Advanced Data Science efforts.
 • MongoDB [40]– The MongoDB database system is now used exclusively for all archiving,
 including time-series data storage, metadata storage, and archive annotations. Previously it was
 utilized solely for metadata archiving due to its document-based storage structure. With redesign
 of the time-series data archive, the MongoDB system was found to have exceptional performance.
 Additionally, it is public-domain freeware and installs on most platforms.
Many third-party systems and components are eliminated from the prototype, including the InfluxDB time-
series database system [27] and the Spring and SpringBoot application frameworks [28]. Although
designed for time-series data, InfluxDB functionality and performance was not appropriate for
heterogeneous data. The Spring and SpringBoot frameworks hindered performance goals and obfuscated
operations. Although the C++ language was not chosen for Data Platform development it was found that
C++ gRPC provides exceptional performance, unfortunately, it is the most difficult to implement and
deploy. Nonetheless, it is possible to create specialized Data Platform components built in C++, which are
particularly attractive for ingestion stream processing in real-time applications requiring exceptional
performance characteristics. Thus, implementation of gRPC communications in C++ was carefully
documented [29].
The new architecture of the MLDP, including the new “Core Services” design replacing the Datastore, is
shown in Figure 4. The diagram includes MLDP subsystems, client relationships, and basic deployment.
It is important to note that a new naming convention is adopted; Datastore is replaced by Data Platform
(DP). Since the Datastore prototype and support was abandoned, it was prudent to identify it as such. The

 20

new convention clarifies the revised component from the prototype and isolates new code repositories. As
indicated in the diagram, the new Data Platform is composed of collaborating services communicating
through gRPC. The new design is modular, performance-based, and distributed. Additionally, the gRPC
communication framework is narrowed and simplified complimenting the distributed, service-based nature.
The Data Platform contains the “Core Services” of the MLDP. Shown in Figure 4 are the Ingestion Service,
the Query Service, the Annotation Service, and the Ingestionstream Service (abbreviated “Ingeststream” in
the diagram). Each service performs an essential function of the Data Platform and offers its intended client
a well-defined Application Programing Interface (API) for interaction. The Ingestion Service provides the
high-speed data ingestion and archiving capabilities providing a narrow interface based upon a
heterogeneous “data frame.” Note that the Ingestion Service is available to Data Providers, one of which
the Aggregator subsystem. However, any Data Provider conforming to the Ingestion Service API can
supply data to the Data Platform, creating its standalone nature. The Query Service is the interface to the
data archive seen by MLDP clients termed Data Consumers, which includes data scientists, accelerator
physicists, control personnel, engineers, and applications programmers. Some query processing is now
distributed between the Query Service and the Java API Library (not shown). Specifically, for enhanced
performance the Query Service offers time-series query operations with minimal data processing (i.e.,
supplying “raw” data) where data processing and data reassembly occurs locally on the client platform (e.g.,
the Java API Library), offloading processing from the Query Service. The archive annotations capability
is now managed by a separate Annotation Service with independent API for Data Consumers. The
Ingestionstream Service provides Data Consumers access to the ingestion datastream for real-time
applications. Note in Figure 4 that the Ingestion Service, Query Service, and Annotation Service have
direct access to the data archive while the Ingestionstream Service connects to the Ingestion Service.

 21

 Figure 4: MLDP composite diagram with client relationships

Implied in Figure 4 is the potential deployment on multiple host servers, supported by the revised Data
Platform architecture. For added performance, Data Platform deployment can utilize horizontal scaling
where one or more Core Services can be deployed on dedicated servers. Likewise, the MongoDB database
system can utilize multiple servers. The Web Application has a separate server which loads the Web
Application into web browsers then marshals all data and communications between the Data Platform and
Remote User. The Aggregator component is typically deployed within the EPICS environment, although
the new front-end hardware system, the Osprey 250k discussed below, can be attached directly to facility
hardware.

2. Project Accomplishments
A summary of project activities was presented in the previous chapter including a concise summary of
project technical accomplishments in Subsection I.5.3. Here we describe each accomplishment in detail.
The intent is to provide a record of project activity and to document the internal architecture, function, and
operation of all MLDP components and subsystems. This is an open-source project, and such
documentation is critical to future open-source development and to the Osprey DCS customer base.
Activity Summary: July to September 2025
Before continuing we present an outline of project activity since the last Progress Report [21]. Although
the SBIR Phase IIA project formally terminated in July 2025, Osprey DCS continued some activities into
September, including the ICALEPCS 2025 conference attendance; we briefly identify this activity here.
Significant gains were made within this short period, and we feel compelled to identify them.
Major activity concerned the Data Platform component of the MLDP, including ingestion datastream
processing and operations evaluations. Additional activity concerned upgrades and improvements to the

 22

companion Java API Library, or “JAL.” The Data Platform and Java API Library are publicly available
(and well documented, see refs. [11] and [31]). As such, both code base and documentation are now version
controlled within the hosting repository as development proceeds.
Regarding the Data Platform, there were 2 minor version releases since May 2025.
 • Release 1.10.0 (July 2025) – Ingestion stream processing of the Ingestionstream Service was
 implemented. The release contained real-time data “event monitoring,” which is part of the
 Advanced Data Sciences portion of the project.
 • Release 1.11.0 (September 2025) – A standalone Demonstration Application was built and included
 with the Data Platform distribution. The application demonstrates the integrated operation of all
 Data Platform Core Services.
Development also continued in the following areas:
 • The Data Platform Main Repository – The Data Platform main repository is used for public-
 domain distribution. The repository has been significantly enhanced with documentation on each
 release, including recent releases and past releases. The repository now contains comprehensive
 documentation on motivation, design, implementation, strategy, and background, complete with
 links to supporting material (e.g., 3rd party components). It also contains a repository sub-branch
 with formal reports, conference proceedings, and presentations.
 • The Java API Library (JAL Release 1.8.0) – The JAL is a companion to the Data Platform
 providing high-level client interaction. Major activity there was a redesign and rebuilding of the
 Query Service API to support timestamp lists and the ability to handle time-domain collisions
 (time-domain collisions occur when multiple Process Variables are sampled at different rates
 during the same interval). Another major addition is the inclusion of an application framework for
 building client applications with the JAL library; it provides common features such as command-
 line parsing, environment access, and exception handling. Many comprehensive testing and
 evaluation applications were built from the framework and now included in the JAL distribution.
 Additional applications continue to be developed.
 • Performance Evaluations - The Java API Library (JAL) was used for comprehensive performance
 and operations testing of the Data Platform operations. Some results are included in Appendix I.
 • ICALEPCS 2025 Attendance – Osprey DCS attended the 2025 International Conference on
 Accelerator and Large Experimental Physics Control Systems (ICALEPCS 2025) in September as
 a corporate sponsor and contributor. The activity was in support of commercialization efforts for
 the MLDP with emphasis on the Osprey 250k data acquisition system and the Data Platform data
 management system.
Additional activities concerned refinements of existing Data Platform features and supplementing
operations of the Java API Library (JAL).

 23

 2.1. Data Actor Definitions
Data management is a fundamental objective of the project and there are common data-related actors used
throughout the sequel. Figure 5 depicts the data actors having context within the MLDP, primarily within
the Data Platform domain. Not shown in the figure are hardware devices, which only appear within the
Aggregator domain.

 Figure 5: MLDP data actor definitions

From the figure we have the following:
 • Data Provider: A Data Provider is any arbitrary source of heterogeneous, time-correlated data. To
 maintain generality Data Providers are assumed to be compliant with the Data Platform Ingestion
 Service API. Specifically, they can deliver their data as (multi-channel) time-correlated data blocks,
 or “data frames” in Data Platform vernacular.
 • Aggregator: The Aggregator is the front-end subsystem of the MLDP performing the actual data
 acquisition, time correlation, and coalescing. From Figure 5 we see that the Aggregator is a specific
 instance of Data Provider, or Data Provider sub-class. Note that the Aggregator coalesces acquired
 data into EPICS pvAccess NTTable instances which are analogous to the data frame of the Data
 Platform.
 • Data Consumer: A Data Consumer is any party interested in the MLDP data archive. This
 includes physicists, engineers, data scientists, programmers, and applications. The MLDP provides
 several services for accessing and interacting with the MLDP archive as shown in Figure 4 and
 discussed in detail in the subsections below. Each service offered to Data Consumer provides
 specialized operations, such as time-series data queries, archive annotations, or ingestion stream
 access. Thus, Data Consumer is a general concept identifying any MLDP back-end user or process.
 • Real-Time Application: As seen in Figure 5 a Real-Time Application (or “Real-Time App”) is a
 sub-class of Data Consumers. Real-Time Applications are processes that perform their operations
 online, such as facility control applications. Thus, they require fast access to the data archive or
 more appropriately, the ingestion datastream.
 • Remote User: A remote user is also a sub-class of Data Consumer. Remote Users are Data
 Consumers accessing and interacting with the MLDP archive from remote locations, say outside a
 facility firewall or outside the facility altogether. For example, the Web Application is designed
 specifically for remote access and for support of Remote Users.
The actors defined above are used through the sequel.
 2.2. Project Timeline and Milestones
Below is timeline synopsis of project activities with major accomplishments, from project inception to
completion and beyond. This subsection is intended to summarize the project accomplishments at the time
of this writing. For completeness we include activities from Phase I providing context for later activities.

 24

For those interested in activity details, the major accomplishments and findings are expanded in the
subsections that follow. Casual readers may wish to skip to the next subsection (Subsection II.3) after this
subsection.
Phase I: The Aggregator Prototype (2022)
The initial Aggregator system was purely software based. It was built upon the Beam Synchronous
Acquisition System (BSAS) developed for collecting and aligning Channel Access and pvAccess signals
within the EPICS environment [35] [36]. The Aggregator collected a set of PV values over a prescribed
time interval, aligned the values according to their EPICS timestamps, then build an EPICS NTTable
instance containing the correlated signal values. Note that the Aggregator system could be distributed
throughout the EPICS environment where “local Aggregators” would collect and align data proximal to
their targeted hardware system (i.e., at the device driver). A “central Aggregator” would then collect all
local data with the pvAccess protocol where it was coalesced into the final NTTable instance. Performance
of the Aggregator prototype was consistent with the original project goals, it could capture ~1,000 signals
at a sampling rate of 1 kHz.
Phase I: The "Datastore" Prototype (2022)
The first implementation of the MDLP archive manager was termed the Datastore. Phase I focused on the
creation of a general API supporting ingestion and query of heterogeneous data types including scalars,
arrays/tables, structures, and images. Service implementations were created using Java for both the
Ingestion and Query Services. Two basic Java client API libraries were also built, one for the Ingestion
Service and one for the Query Service. The prototype technology stack included gRPC for communications
[25] and 2 database systems: 1) InfluxDB database for time series data [27], and 2) MongoDB for metadata
[26]. This prototype successfully demonstrated the use of gRPC APIs for ingestion and retrieval of
heterogeneous data, as well as providing an independent data transport system (e.g., no EPICS protocols
were required). Even more significant, the Datastore demonstrated the feasibility of implementing the
MLDP archive manager as an independent, standalone system. Unfortunately, the Datastore prototype did
not meet the baseline performance requirements; in fact, performance was the major issue.
Phase I: Web Application Prototype (2022)
The concept of remote MLDP archive access was also investigated early; the most promising approach
appeared to be that incorporating a standard internet web browser. Thus, a Web Application prototype was
rapidly developed using JavaScript React and Tailwind libraries. It communicated with the Datastore
through the gRPC interface directly and offered a simple user screens for querying and displaying time-
series data, as well as navigating some metadata. It demonstrated proof of principle for browser-based
remoted MLDP archive interaction and confirmed the use of gRPC Web as a feasibility technology [41].
Phase II Initiated: Design Studies and Evaluations (2023)
The Datastore prototype suffered from serious performance limitations; additionally, it had no formal
archive annotation system. The data ingestion system was completely incapable of keeping up with the
Aggregator system, by almost 2 orders of magnitude. Due to this severe under performance, it was decided
that a redesign and rebuild was necessary. Several design possibilities were developed, along with a plan
to investigate available technologies and techniques for creating the new technology stack.
Technology and Performance Benchmarking (September 2023)
In pursuit of project performance goals, benchmark applications were developed and utilized to evaluate
candidate technologies for use in the new MLDP archive management system implementation. Benchmark
testing focused on gRPC for API communication; InfluxDB, MongoDB and MariaDB for database
archiving; and JSON and HDF5 for direct file archiving to disk. The benchmark results showed that it was
likely we could build service implementations meeting our performance requirements by using gRPC for
communication and only MongoDB for all data archiving. Some benchmark results are documented in the
Appendices (see Appendix I.1).

 25

The Data Platform v1.0.0 (November 2023)
All development on the previous Datastore system was terminated and a complete rebuild of the MLDP
archive management system was initiated after the performance studies. The rebuild was based upon a new
Core Service design and the technology stack described above. To emphasis the Core Services redesign
and rebuild the new system was termed the Data Platform. Version 1.0 of the Data Platform includes an
initial Java implementation of the Ingestion Service providing the new gRPC API and using only MongoDB
for archiving time-series data. The initial implementation included only scalar data and with timestamps
specified using a sampling clock mechanism. The new Ingestion Service is accompanied by a performance
benchmark application that is used at each stage of development to confirm ingestion performance relative
to the project goal. The initial implementation exceeds our goal by a comfortable margin, up to 5-fold on a
development platform. However, performance was a continued focus as the project evolved.
Data Platform Installer (December 2023)
A rudimentary installation and deployment system was built for the Data Platform system to quickly
distribute Data Platform modifications between the development team. The installation system also
facilitated software versioning. The installer distribution repository then became a central location for
developers to monitor development, flag issues, and track progress, as well as store notes, documents,
presentations, and other artifacts. Since its inception the installation and deployment facility has matured
significantly and is now the preferred method of distribution; it was made public in Release 1.7 (January
2025) when the Data Platform API began to stabilize.
Query Service: Data Platform v1.1.0 (January 2024)
Version 1.1 includes the initial implementation of the Query Service using the MongoDB database managed
by the Ingestion Service. A variety of API gRPC methods for querying time-series data are provided to
support clients with varying performance requirements, ranging from streaming response methods that
return raw data results down to simple unary response methods that return tabular data. See Subsection 2.5
for a detailed description of the Query Service API.
Web Application (January 2024)
A rebuild of the Web Application was initiated; a junior developer was hired as lead developer. The new
Web Application communicates with the Core Services using gRPC as before, however using the new API.
The focus of the new Web Application is intuitive user interaction and simplified workflow. It is also used
to confirm the operation of the Query Service and Annotation Service APIs as features are added.
New Communications Framework API: Data Platform v1.2.0 (February 2024)
Version 1.2 saw significant changes to the gRPC API for the Data Platform, with corresponding changes
to the Java Cores Services implementations. Feedback increased as the Data Platform was used by
developers, and the API was modified to be more consistent and conventional. Additionally, the first
version of the Annotation Service API was available in this release; it had a basic gRPC interface only, with
no functionality. In addition, the first metadata queries were added to the Query Service.
Java API Library (JAL): Data Platform API v1.2.0 (March 2024)
The Java language client library was being developed simultaneously with the Data Platform, facilitated
greatly by the availability of the installation utility. (Language client libraries offer users a programming
language API to the Data Platform; providing more straightforward, high-level communications rather than
that from direct gRPC communication.) The Java API library (JAL) operation was first confirmed with
Version 1.2 of the Data Platform gRPC API.
Hardware-Based Acquisition: Aggregator Design Studies (April 2024)
A new design approach to fast sampling and acquisition was initiated. Specifically, sampling and
correlation were to be combined and moved upstream in the technology stack, into hardware. The intent
was to perform data alignment within Field Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) where the sampling of

 26

digitized signals occurred. An open-source FPGA board and supporting environment developed by
Berkeley was available for prototyping [42].
Annotations Service: Data Platform v1.3.0 (April 2024)
Version 1.3 provides an initial implementation of the Annotation Service. The Annotation Services allows
clients to add annotations to archived data post ingestion then query those annotations directly or perform
data queries against those annotations. The focus of the initial Annotation Service implementation was the
data model for associating annotations to archive data. The initial version supports the identification of
archive datasets providing the overall design model for all annotation types. The Annotation Service
provides a significant function within the project; full annotation capability is a stated project achievement.
Thus, this step represented significant progress in that goal.
Data Platform v1.4.0 (May 2024)
Several additional features were added to the Query Service and Annotations Service. Many of these were
