in direct support of Web Application functionality, however, are also of general benefit. As example, the
ability to directly query for tabular data, and regular expressions are now available. The ability to create
basic comment annotations was added to the Annotation Service.
Archive Redesign: Data Platform v1.5.0 (August 2024)
Release 1.5 was a major development achievement for the Data Platform, and the project in general. There
were 3 major areas of development: 1) performance, 2) full heterogeneous data type archiving and retrieval,
and 3) full client-side ingestion streaming support. Regarding performance the archiving system was
redesigned to store time-series data in native (compressed) Protocol Buffers format. This not only reduced
archiving times but also archive size; a performance gain of 2x was achieved in the process. Regarding the
2nd area, all heterogeneous data types were now supported by both the Ingestion Service and the Query
Service according to the original project requirements. Archiving and retrieval of complex types such as
timestamps, image, arrays, and data structures was supported, along with the previously available
heterogeneous scalar types such as Booleans, integers, floats, doubles, and character strings. Finally, direct
unidirectional data streaming to the Ingestion Service was now available to Data Providers. Clients could
now transmit ingestion data to the Data Platform without waiting for confirmation. A summary response
for the entire session is provided to the client upon stream closure.
Data Exporting and Ingestion Archiving Status: Data Platform v1.6.0 (October 2024)
Data Exporting
The major feature of Data Platform Release 1.6 is a data exporting feature added to the Annotation Service.
Recall that annotations are assigned by “dataset,” which is a collection of non-contiguous data blocks within
the archive; thus, the Annotation Service was the natural choice for this feature. It is possible to export data
to external files in the formats of Comma-Separated-Values (CSVs), HDF5, and Excel spreadsheets.
Archiving Status and Provider Registration
The Ingestion Service also saw significant development within Release 1.6. It now includes a lookup utility
for requesting the archiving status of individual ingestion requests (i.e., via client-supplied request
identifier). According to the newly implemented Common Core Services Framework streaming data is
passed to a queue for later processing; rather than blocking on the result of the operation, the operation
result is stored for later access and confirmation by the client. Explicitly, for the Ingestion Service after
receiving an incoming data frame and acknowledging receipt, the data is then immediately passed to
independent archiving threads for further processing; this is an efficient strategy where ingestion streaming
is not interrupted, and archiving can be done with multiple threads. With Release 1.6.0 the status of
ingestion processing and archiving can now be determined post-ingestion with a new Ingestion Service
“query status” request. The status of previously ingested data can be determined using multiple criteria
such as Data Provider name or unique identifier (UID), Process Variable (PV) name, the status itself (e.g.,
“success” or “failure”), the request UID, and time range of the ingestion operation. These criteria can be
combined or requested individually. Every ingestion frame sent to the Ingestion Service has a unique

 27

identifier which can be used to identify the ingested data uniquely (i.e., by request UID). Thus, after
streaming a collection of data to the Ingestion Service a client can now determine the status of the operation
at any time afterwards.
Additionally, Data Providers must now register with the Ingestion Service; a unique name and any
associated attributes and properties are registered with the Data Platform and used as metadata and in
maintaining data provenance.
Process Variable Status
The MLDP archive was also modified to include a status field for all Process Variable values. Specifically,
a “status” field for every archive data value is now recorded and recognized by both the Ingestion Service
and Query Service. The status is used to indicate the condition of the data value or associated
software/hardware at the time of acquisition; it is available in direct support of the EPICS PV status fields.
Data Platform Test Archive
Finally, a new utility, TestDataGenerator, was included with the release distribution; it is launched with
script app-run-test-data-generator. This utility is used to populate the Data Platform archive with a
consistent set of test data (i.e., simulated data) used for testing service operations. We refer to this simulated
dataset as the Data Platform Test Archive. The Test Archive contains simulated time-series data for 4,000
Process Variables sampled at 1 kHz over 1 minute for a total of 240 million samples. The test archive
utility behaves as a simulated Data Provider and the Test Archive is always generated consistently; Process
Variables, timestamps, and sample values are the same for all generated Test Archives. The evaluation
applications included within the Java API Library (JAL) require the Test Archive for their operation.
Web Application Support: Data Platform v1.6.1 (January 2025)
The Release 1.6.1 patch was a special release in support of Web Application development which required
additional features. Most requirements concerned the exporting of time-series data isolated by the Web
Application. The handling of large data sets (say ≥ 100 Mbytes) required specific attention due to the nature
of gRPC, internet web browser behavior, and the supporting Envoy conversion and marshalling system
[43]. Various data corruption issues were corrected for these cases. To identify data corruption issues the
Data Platform now confirms that any exported data file is complete before acknowledging a response. Thus,
any corruption issue is first caught as an exception by the Data Platform and then relayed to clients.
A specialized export mechanism with a configurable size limit was implemented, which also considers the
export format. This feature prevents remote clients from performing overreaching data requests or,
conversely, allowing extreme data requests when warranted; for example, HDF5 files can be left size
unrestricted. Performance was also improved by a factor 5x for data export to Excel files. The Annotation
Service was also modified to allow browsing and querying of client annotations by unique identifier (UID).
Ingestionstream Access: Data Platform v1.7.0 (January 2025)
Ingestionstream Service Prototype
Release 1.7.0 contains the initial framework of the Ingestionstream Service and supporting Ingestion
Service enhancements, a major development effort. The Ingestionstream Service is part of the Advanced
Data Sciences portion of Phase II providing clients direct access to active ingestion streams. The
Ingestionstream Service is intended for real-time control applications supporting facility and/or
experimental operations. A prototype of the Ingestionstream Service appears in Release 1.7.0. The
Ingestionstream service has a rudimentary API for Data Event subscription, but the feature was not
implemented in this release. The prototype did feature the ability to communicate with the Ingestion
Service via a public “subscription service.”
Ingestion Service Data Subscription
In support of the Ingestionstream Service, the Ingestion Service was augmented with a data subscription
subservice, which is available to any Data Platform client. This subservice functions with a standard
“Subscribe/Publish” framework where clients subscribe to a pre-determined dataset within the ingestion

 28

stream and the Ingestion Service publishes the data as it arrives. The client subscription consists of a desired
set of Process Variable (PV) names from which he or she receives the published stream of PV values
according to the subscription parameters (e.g., how often, how many, etc.). The published data is taken
directly from currently active ingestion streams, continuing until the client cancels the subscription. This
mechanism can be viewed as the “twining” of an ingestion stream branch defined by the client.
Note that the Ingestion Service PV Publish/Subscribe mechanism has merit on its own. Clients can
subscribe to a branch of the ingestion stream and monitor collections of the incoming, correlated, time-
series data. However, the primary intent of the subservice is to support the Ingestionstream Service, which
provides additional processing of the raw data.
Calculations Annotations: Data Platform v1.8.0 (March 2025)
Activity within Data Platform Release 1.8.0 returned to Annotation Service development. The original
intent was to complete the service by adding the calculation annotation use case. Specifically, the
Annotation Service requirement is that it support annotating archive data with client calculations obtained
from the Data Platform archive itself, post-ingestion. However, it was found previously during concurrent
development of the Web Application annotation features that the original Annotation Service API was
convoluted. A new Annotation Service API was designed previously; however, the new API was not
implemented until Release 1.8.0. (We felt it imperative to first initiate development of the Ingestionstream
Service then complete the Annotation Service.) In addition to implementing calculations annotation,
refactoring the Annotation Service to support the new API necessitated notable development effort. A
significant, and unexpected, overall development effort has been required to complete the Annotation
Service. However, the annotation capability was a key feature of the Data Platform explicitly stated in
project scope and requested by Osprey DCS Customers. The inclusion of a robust Annotation Service
within the Data Platform Core Services was crucial to project success.
During the calculation annotations development, it was decided that a separate MongoDB archive (i.e.,
documents collection) was needed for client calculations storage. In fact, during the refactoring it was
decided that all annotations should be stored in a separate archive. Although the storage format of
calculation annotations is essentially equivalent to that for time-series data (they share many of the same
resources and constructs), a separate archive for calculations data provided the cleanest and most robust
implementation. Mixing user calculations with time-series data would require specialized programmatic
tools obfuscating database integrity. Now time-series data, archive metadata, and archive annotations all
exist in separate archives (i.e., separate MongoDB documents collections). Annotations query operations
are now more straightforward and more robust. For example, clients can now query annotations by
annotation creator, attribute values, tag values, and regular expressions within the annotations themselves.
Moreover, annotation modification by clients is now more straightforward; that is, if a client wishes to
modify and/or delete an annotation.
The Query Service now supports queries for Data Providers and Data Provider metadata (as well as the
previously supported Process Variable metadata). It is possible to query for contributing Data Providers
along with a variety of Data Provider attributes. Specifically, clients can now determine which Process
Variables are associated with a Data Provider, query attributes and tags associated with Data Providers, and
query basic provider activity profiles. As a use case example, a client can query for a Data Provider by an
attribute value to recover its unique identifier (UID), then use the provider UID to find its associated Process
Variables, along with the first and last times it was active.
Osprey 250k: Aggregator (April 2025)
Osprey DCS’s first hardware product, the Osprey 250k, was formally completed and operational; a major
milestone in the project. The Osprey 250k is a product providing high-speed sampling, data acquisition,
and time-correlation of up to 32 isolated channels per chassis (or 64 common ground signals). Up to 32
chassis can be stacked in parallel (with a common clock) for a combined input of 1,024 analog input signals
and 1,024 correlated digitized output signals with common timestamps supplied as User Datagram Protocol

 29

(UDP) packets fed to high-speed Ethernet networks. The maximum sampling and acquisition rate for a
bank of 32 synchronized chassis is 250 kHz. The Osprey 250k achieves its outstanding performance using
a novel approach to the acquisition and correlation problem; both operations are combined and moved
upstream into the hardware level. Analog signals are first digitized then sampled and correlated with an
Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA).
The Osprey 250k supports switchable sampling rates of 1, 5, 10, 25, and 250 kHz. Thus, the Aggregator
performance has drastically improved, from the original 1 kHz acquisition of ~1,000 signals to the sampling
and acquisition of ~1,000 signals at 250 kHz, a factor 250x improvement. Consistent with project open-
source objectives, the Osprey 250k is a fully open-source hardware product with components, assembly
instructions, and firmware available online [12].
Serialized Data Management: Data Platform v1.9.0 (May 2025)
Client Serialization Option
Performance was again addressed in Release 1.9.0. An opportunity for performance enhancement was
identified by the archive redesign of Release 1.5. Through code profiling it was discovered that significant
resources were expending during serialization within the data streaming process. The new Data Platform
archive architecture enabled elimination of several resource-intensive serialization and deserialization
operations. Explicit client serialization of time-series data was offered as an optional feature for both the
Ingestion Service and the Query Service (i.e., in addition to standard ingestion and query operations). A
factor 2x to 3x speedup was achieved using the new options, as measured by the benchmarking utilities for
each service. Note that these benchmarking utilities ship with the Data Platform distribution but do not
directly measure data transport rates. Maximum data rates for the Ingestion Service had been previously
confirmed at 500 MBps using the performance applications within the Java API Library (JAL), which
measure data transport rates explicitly. Likewise, the maximum data rate for the Query Service (before
Release 1.9.0 upgrade) was confirmed at ~200 MBps with the JAL performance applications. Thus, we
expect ingestion rates of at least 1 GBps and query rates of ~500 MBps but cannot confirm these values
until the JAL Release 1.10.0 which supports the new serialization option. Note that the expected value of
1 GBps is a factor 30x beyond original project goals. We also note that these performance numbers were
achieved on single development platforms. We have not yet considered specialized hardware or horizontal
scaling; thus, there is still significant room for additional performance improvements.
Simultaneous Time-Series and Calculation Annotation Export
Another notable feature of Release 1.9.0 is the ability to export a calculation annotation alongside archived
time-series data. The natural use case for the new feature is to export a client calculation alongside the
originating time-series data. For example, assume the client is collecting raw experimental data from
measurement equipment. There is a calculation that yields a normalized dataset which is then comparable
to other measurements, or to system models. Additionally, there may be other calculations performed by
other clients who wish to use the normalized data. Those calculations can then be stored as annotations
associated with the previously normalized data. It is now possible to recover all data sets concurrently
using the Annotation Service data export feature; for example, if any party wishes to examine the raw data,
the normalized data, and any calculations derived from the normalized data.
Test Archive Enhancement
In Release 1.9.0 the test archive utility TestDataGenerator now supplies Process Variables using both
timestamping methods. Thus, the new Data Platform Test Archive now contains simulated Process
Variable sample values using both a uniform sampling clock and explicit timestamp lists. Note that the use
of explicit timestamp lists is more expensive but handles spurious data and/or non-uniformity in sample
rates. Best practices for the Data Platform discourages the use of timestamp lists as they require
substantially more resources and processing beyond that for a uniform sampling clock. However, support
for timestamp lists is available as a use case extension when required.

 30

Ingestionstream Service Implementation: Data Platform v1.10.0 (July 2025)
Release 1.10.0 includes an operational Ingestionstream Service. Recall that Release 1.7.0 contained a
prototype of the service with API but unimplemented operations (see Heading “Ingestionstream Access:
Data Platform v1.7.0 (January 2025)” of this subsection). The Ingestionstream Service provides access to
the live ingestion stream in the form of Data Event monitoring. This feature is part of the Advanced Data
Sciences effort within the Phase IIA portion of the project and represents a project milestone. (The
Algorithm Plugin feature is also part of the Advanced Data Sciences but has been moved to the JAL library
due to incompatibility with the gRPC communications framework.) Data Events monitoring is performed
using a Subscribe/Publish mechanism like that for the PV value subscription mechanism offered by the
Ingestion Service. It is useful for monitoring data conditions in "real-time" for applications responding to
conditions in the data ingestion stream. The Data Event monitoring mechanism uses the Ingestion Service's
data subscription API to receive data from the ingestion datastream for Data Event Process Variables. The
release includes improvements to the Ingestion Service data subscription handling in support of the new
Data Event subscription API, as well as new Data Platform ecosystem scripts for managing the new
Ingestionstream Service.
A Data Event itself consists of an event trigger, a data event notification, and an optional data event
operation. Currently, an event trigger is composed of a Process Variable, a trigger value, and a trigger
condition such as equals, greater than, less than, etc. A Data Event contains a collection of PV event triggers
where all trigger conditions must be met before the event is recognized. The Data Event notification can
be a simple response indicating that the trigger condition occurred. Or the client can optionally register to
receive a collection of Process Variable values over a given sampling or time window (with optional trigger-
time offset).
Data Platform Demonstration Application: Data Platform v1.11.0 (September 2025)
The Data Platform distribution of Release 1.11.0 includes a desktop Demonstration Application. The intent
is to demonstrate the features of the Data Platform using a single application easily executed on the client
platform. The Demonstration Application runs all Data Platform Core Service internally and only requires
a MongoDB installation for local execution. A Dockers container with the Data Platform MongoDB
database is also available in the Release 1.11.0 installation (i.e., in lieu of a local MongoDB system), further
simplifying Demonstration Application execution.
The Data Platform is a serviced-based system for data archive management; thus, recognition of its full
functionality is difficult unless client applications are built or otherwise available for interaction. The intent
of the Demonstration Application is to provide this client interaction. However, instead of making an
application with limited demonstration capabilities, we decided to build a full-featured tool capable of
navigating the entire MLDP archive, time-series data, all metadata, and annotations. The Demonstration
Application GUI contains screens for searching archive metadata and time-series data, viewing and creating
annotations, request building, and other tools for visualizing and exporting data. Quick links are available
for most functions to expedite toggling between screens and functions. The application includes a simulated
Data Provider users can configure for creation of scalar time-series data, complete with PV names, attributes,
and other metadata for later request building. Once data is ingested it can be inspected and annotated via
query requests; there are screens for plotting time-series data or displaying in tabular format. A request
builder is available for creating the query requests using metadata, time ranges, and data events. Archive
datasets can be exported to the local platform. Calculation annotations are imported to the archive from the
local platform using Excel workbooks. The application uses the Data Platform gRPC API and provides a
useful reference for calling those APIs from a Java client.
The Demonstration Application user interface is built with the JavaFX library [44], whose library
components are built into the installation distribution. Thus, unlike the Web Application, Java and
MongoDB are the only external dependencies. As such we are considering using a similar approach for
remote MLDP archive access (i.e., rather than, or in addition to the Web Application); it is a simple matter
to redirect the application to external Data Platform services via an URL.

 31

 2.3. Aggregator System
The Aggregator system is the front-end of the
MLDP performing sampling, acquisition, time
correlation, and transport staging for the Data
Platform. The operation of the Aggregator is
shown in the use-case diagram of Figure 6. As
seen in the diagram it connects to the hardware
components within a facility, such as
instruments, diagnostics, and experimental
measurement devices. Typically, the
Aggregator is deployed within the EPICS
environment where its final product is NTTable
instances containing aligned sample data from
 Figure 6: Aggregator system use cases
multiple sources. Hardware connection is done
indirectly through the control system (e.g.,
EPICS) or directly in the case of the Osprey 250k (see below).
At project start the Aggregator was purely software based and supported sampling rates of up to 1 kHz for
1,000 input signals. The Aggregator system was greatly enhanced during the project with Osprey DCS’s
first hardware product, the “Osprey 250k” [12]. The Osprey 250k uses the novel approach of performing
both data sampling and time correlation upstream in the acquisition pipeline, specifically at the hardware
level. The product was originally named according to internal components (e.g., boards, chipsets, etc.) and
functionality (e.g., DAQ, 24-bit, 32-input, 250 kHz) but since has been abridged. However, the repository
name, contents, and early drawings still retain this format.
At present the Aggregator is installed and operating at 2 facilities: 1) the SLAC National Accelerator
Laboratory in support of LCLS-II [45] operating at 1 kHz (e.g., see [35], and [36]), and 2) the NASA’s Neil
Armstrong Test Facility in support of Artemis II project in Sandusky, Ohio [46]. At the Artemis II testbed,
the Osprey 250k is sampling 1,024 signals at 250 kHz, requiring 32 synchronized chassis (see Subsection
2.4 below). Arizona State University (ASU) in Tempe, Arizona is considering the Osprey 250k for support
of the CXFEL facility [47] for management of experimental data from their Eiger detector system [48].
    2.4.        Osprey 250k
The Osprey 250k is a hardware chassis providing 32 analog input channels and high-speed Ethernet output
ports providing User Datagram Protocol (UDP) packets containing 24-bit digitized outputs plus the
common timestamp for each of the 32 output signals. Each packet contains digitized data for 4 sample
triggers. It supports sampling rates of 1, 5, 10, 25, and 250 kHz, performing sampling and time correlation
at the hardware level using a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA). It is a direct hardware replacement
for the Aggregator front end which previously relied on software for time correlation of signal data acquired
from EPICS device drivers. The Osprey 250k can be connected directly to hardware outputs such as sensors
and diagnostics.

 32

 Figure 7: Osprey 250k chassis internal schematic

A basic circuit schematic of the Osprey 250k is shown in Figure 7. The figure shows the rudimentary
structure of a single Osprey 250k hardware chassis containing multi-channel, high-speed Analog-to-Digital
Converters (AD/C) followed by a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA) performing sampling and
alignment of the digitized signals. There are 4 AD/C digitizer chips per chassis, each with 8 isolated
channels supporting ±10 Volt differential inputs and 24-bit outputs. Each chassis contains a single FPGA
which samples the 32 incoming digitized signals according to an Event Receiver (EVR) input trigger and
EVent Fan-in (EVF) input signal. The EVF input signal is used to synchronize acquisition, sampling, and
alignment with peripheral Osprey 250k chassis. Accordingly, each Osprey 250k has multiple (six) EVent
Fan-out (EVF) output lines that can be connected to external boxes for simultaneous acquisition. Thus,
chassis can be stacked in parallel with a common input trigger to support coordinated data acquisition; to
date Osprey DCS has successfully deployed 32 chassis operating simultaneously at a 250k sampling rate
for a total of 1024 signals. All EVF input and output lines use fiber optic connections.

 33

A photograph of the Osprey 250k with cover removed is shown in Figure 8. There are 2 primary printed
circuit boards within the chassis: 1) the Quartz boards carrying the front-end digitizers [49], and 2) the
Marble board carrying the FPGA chip [42]. The front of the chassis is at the bottom of the photograph.
The 32 analog inputs use ribbon connectors to the Quartz board and are clearly visible, along with the fiber
optic fan-in and fan-out lines. The master trigger input connector is not visible, hidden beneath the fiber
optic lines, although the 50-Ohm cable can be seen. The power indicator light is seen at the bottom left-
hand side corner of the chassis. The top of the photograph is the back of the Osprey 250k chassis where
the yellow output Ethernet cable is clearly visible. A red and grey fuse housing is seen at the top left-hand
side, mounted to the chassis with a rail bracket. The chassis power supply wires are seen in front of the
fuse housing.

 Figure 8: Osprey 250k with top cover removed

In the center of the photograph are the two printed-circuit boards inside the chassis. The 4 digitizer chips
are seen on the front-end Quartz board, along with 16 amplifier/compressor boards. A black chassis
grounding cable is also connected to the top left-hand side of the board. The FPGA is located at the center
of the Marble board. The back of the Marble board has the connector for the yellow Ethernet cable output
line, and fiber optic couplers for fan-in and fan-out lines. Power supply lines are also connected to the
Marble board.

 34

 Figure 9: rack mounted Osprey 250k chassis (front view)

The front ends of 4 rack-mounted Osprey 250k chassis are shown in Figure 9. The fiber optic connections
on the left-hand side are the EVR and EVF input and output lines. Seen in the top-most chassis is a 50-
Ohm cable with BNC connector; this is the master trigger signal for the entire bank. The clock signal is
then synchronized between boxes with the fan-out and fan-in fiber optic lines. In the photo the analog input
connectors at the center right-hand sides of all the chassis are all left unterminated. Clearly all chassis are
powered up.

 35

 Figure 10: single-chassis Osprey 250k bench test setup

Osprey 250k Installation and Configuration
Figure 10 shows the Osprey 250k bench configuration for a single chassis test setup. A GPS receiver is
used to provide the clock signal to the chassis. The receiver is also connected to the control computer for
comparison with the Osprey 250k outputs, which are routed through an Ethernet switch. A function
generator is used to simulate the input signals for the test chassis. The same signal is split and feed to both
chassis input banks. That is, all 32 input channels receive the same input signal. As indicated in the diagram,
a fiber optic jumper is required between the EVR input and an EVF fan-out output. Specifically, the input
trigger clock signal is captured on the Quartz board then routed to the Marble board using the EVR fiber
optic input line. Thus, of the 6 EVF fan-out lines the “master node” (see below) always requires 1.

 36

 Figure 12: Osprey 250k multi-node installation: up to 6 chassis

When using multiple chassis within an installation, that is for simultaneous acquisition of greater than 32
signals, one chassis serves as a “master node.” The single clock trigger signal is input to the master node
which then fans out to all other chassis in the installation. This configuration provides timing and
synchronization for all peripheral chassis. This situation is represented in Figure 12 for the case of 2 to 6
chassis. Since each chassis has 6 fanout lines the master node can synchronize up to 5 external boxes since
one fanout line must be fed back to the master node. Thus, the dual level fanout (i.e., master/slave) supports
up to 6 chassis with up to 192 channels using the setup of Figure 12.

 Figure 11: Osprey 250k multi-node installation: up to 32 chassis

Whenever more than 6 chassis are required at an installation a tertiary level is required, as shown in Figure
11. The additional nodes (i.e., level 3 nodes) are attached to one or more secondary (level 2) nodes. Here
each level 2 node will connect to itself, and up to 5 other level 3 nodes. This arrangement requires an
alteration to the level 1 master node, which will no longer be looped directly to itself. Instead, the master
node will be connected to itself through one of the level 2 nodes. This maintains an equal propagation time
from the fanouts on the master node (fiber-optic Ports 3 to 8), and the EVR (fiber-optic Port 1) on each
node (including the master).

 37

Osprey 250k Deployment
Figure 13 shows a potential deployment of the Osprey 250k within an EPICS-based facility. In the figure
the Osprey 250k is represented as a circle labelled with name “Quartz 250 kHz DAQ Driver” seen at the
lowest level (in diagram center). Note that it sits directly above the hardware level. Indicated in the diagram
is that the Osprey 250k can output to EPICS IOC Device Drivers, EPICS IOC Database Records (Scan
Tasks), or write acquired data directly to disk. Indicated in the diagram is that the Osprey 250k can function
as a high-speed EPIC Device Driver Read Task; thus, replacing the previous front end of the Aggregator
system.
As shown in Figure 13, when operating with the EPICS environment acquired data enters the control system
via an EPICS IOC servers. It then moves up into the higher levels of the control system where it eventually

 Figure 13: Osprey 250k deployment within an EPICS-based facility

becomes available to clients through the Channel Access (CA) or PV Access (pvAccess) communications
protocol. That is, signal acquisition appears no different than that obtained in typical fashion using
hardware device drivers within EPICS IOCs.

 38

 Figure 14: Osprey 250k deployment at NASA Artemis II test facility

Figure 14 shown a deployment schematic of the Osprey 250k within the NASA Artemis II test facility [33].
The control system is EPICS based where acquisition is done with the Osprey 250k and client interaction
is done primarily through Control System Studio [50] [51]. Within the diagram the Osprey 250k is
represented by the left-hand side greyed box. It contains further details on internal components of the
Osprey 250k (i.e., over that of Figure 7) including the open-source Marble FPGA carrier board [42] and the
Osprey DCS Quartz carrier containing the AD/C chipset [12]. Note that the Osprey 250k is using the User
Datagram Protocol (UDP) for its output channels for increased performance.
 2.5. Data Platform
The Data Platform contains the archive management system for the MLDP as seen in Figure 4. It is
responsible for the high-speed ingestion of sampled, time-correlated, heterogeneous data from Data
Providers, such as the MLDP Aggregator system. It is also the back end of the MLDP providing Data
Consumers access and interaction with the data archive (including the Web Application and the
Demonstration Application). The Data Platform is composed of a collection of collaborating services
communicating using the Data Platform Communications Framework. All services within the Data
Platform are built with a common framework, the Common Core Services Framework. Note that the
Communications Framework is an independent, gRPC-based system that facilitates communication
between services, and with Data Platform clients; it creates the standalone nature of the Data Platform. The
Common Core Services Framework couples with the Data Platform Communications Framework to
provide a common framework for external client interaction and building Core Services. Thus, the Data
Platform features a modular, service-based design pattern that facilities performance, maintenance,
upgradeability, and extensibility.
The Data Platform is a fully independent subsystem of the MLDP. It does not require an EPICS
environment, nor does it specifically require the Aggregator system for archive population; any Data
Provider conforming to the Communications Framework ingestion API can supply data to the Data
Platform. Moreover, the Data Platform has a mature distribution system for installation and deployment
that is publicly available [13]. All source code for the Data Platform Core Services is also publicly available
with the open-source repository [15]. The Data Platform can easily be downloaded, installed, and deployed

 39

on local platforms with minimum configuration and ecosystem requirements (Java, MongoDB, and setting
a single environment variable). Thus, as with the Osprey 250k, the Data Platform represents a significant
addition to the Osprey DCS product line.
The subsections that follow describe the components of the Data Platform, including the Communications
Framework, the Common Core Services Framework, and the distribution system. Also described are the
Web Application and the Demonstration application. Here we provide a description for each Data Platform
Core Service, complete with use case diagrams. The gRPC interfaces for each service are presented in
Subsection 2.6 covering the Data Platform Communications Framework.
Ingestion Service
Figure 15 diagrams the use cases addressed by the Data Platform Ingestion Service, both internal and
external. The primary use case is Data Ingestion; note that the Ingestion Service can concurrently
accommodate multiple Data Provider clients. Data Ingestion includes several internal use-cases including
those shown, Performance, Archiving, and Ingestion Stream Access. Note also that the Ingestion Service
sees the Aggregator system simply as a specialized (sub-classed) Data Provider; that is, it will accept data
from any provider conforming to the API interface (see below). Referring to Figure 15, the Ingestion Status
use case infers that Data Providers can request the status of any ingestion operation post ingestion.

 Figure 15: Ingestion Service internal operation and use cases

The Ingestion Stream Access use case provides clients with direct access to the ingestion datastream; the
use case is supported with the addition of the ingestion stream PV subscription service. The Ingestion
Service also accommodates the Real-Time Processing use for a special class of Data Consumers,
specifically, Real-Time Applications. Real-Time Applications can request access to the live ingestion
stream via a PV value Subscribe/Publish service now available from the Ingestion Service.
As described in Subsection II.2.10 benchmarking utilities are included within the Data Platform distribution.
The ingestion benchmarking utility simulates data ingestion where simulated data tables containing 1,000
Process Variable values are transmitted to the Ingestion Service using its gRPC communications API over
the course of 1 minute. The ingestion benchmarking utility provides an estimate of the number of values
per second transmitted to the Ingestion Service but does not do comprehensive performance evaluations
(i.e., it is a tool for quick benchmarking of Ingestion Service operation on the host platform). The JAL
library contains applications for such evaluations, measuring transmission rates directly. Additionally, we
note that we achieve further performance increases utilizing multiple, concurrent data streams (supported

 40

by the Ingestion Service) implemented with the JAL library. With the JAL Release 1.8.0 evaluation
applications the Ingestion Service had confirmed ingestion and archiving rates of 500 MBps. In Data
Platform Release 1.9.0 an optional “client serialization” feature was added to both the Ingestion Service
and the Query Service. The benchmarking utility indicated a factor 2x to 3x performance improvement
when using this feature, indicating a projected data rate of at least 1 GBps. However, this value has yet to
be confirmed with the JAL applications since, as of this writing, the current library version 1.8.0 does not
support the new feature. JAL Release 1.10.0 is under development and results will be available upon
completion (JAL is moving directly from Release 1.8.0 to 1.10.0). We note that the performance numbers
were achieved on a single development platform (a MacMini with M2 CPU containing 10 cores and 16
GBytes memory) hosting the Data Platform, the JAL client library, and the testing applications; this,
additional performance is expected when using specialized hardware and/or horizontal scaling.
The above performance numbers represent a significant project accomplishment. Osprey DCS has thus
achieved a stated project milestone which implies that our overall performance expectations for data
accessibility are reasonable and achievable. To date, comparable performance numbers are seen with the
Query Service using its benchmarking utility (although performance objectives there are not as clearly
defined).
Query Service
The basic design, structure, and fundamental operations of the Query Service was completed in Release 1.2
where additional features continued to be added in subsequent releases. The performance of the Query
Service is not as critical as that for the Ingestion Service; however, our objective is to have Query Service
performance comparable with the Ingestion Service (data rates of at least 32 MBps). After Release 1.2 the
Ingestion Service was redesigned and rebuilt which required a redesign of the MLDP data archive and
communications framework; consequently, the Query Service required a complete rebuild to conform with
the new archive and gRPC communications framework. The Query Service implementation now complies
with the Common Core Services Framework discussed in Subsection 2.6. A Query Service benchmarking
utility is included in the Data Platform distribution for operational verification and local performance
estimates. Additionally, a data integrity testing utility is included in the Data Platform distribution; the
utility verifies that data ingested by the Ingestion Service is archived and correctly recovered by the Query
Service. Here, we cover the fundamental Query Service use cases.

 Figure 16: Query Service use-case diagram

The Query Service provides MLDP archive access and interaction to end users. To appreciate the end-user
requirements a use case diagram is provided in Figure 16. The most important factors concerning the Query
Service are its features and functionality, that is, the Data Analysis use case. (Performance is a concern but
not as vital as for that for the Ingestion Service.) In the diagram the end-user is a Data Consumer, any party
interested in the data archive, such as data scientists, engineers, operators, and software applications. Also
seen in the figure is the Annotation Service whose specific function is to annotate the data archive but also
supplies metadata associated with archive annotations. Together the Query Service and the Annotation

 41

Service provide archive metadata used in the Request Building use case. Note that the Annotation Service
is a fully independent Core Service and is discussed below. As seen in the figure, the Query Service along
with the Annotation Service supports most general data analysis applications for the MLDP.
The Request Building use is one of the most important components of the Data Analysis use case. Archive
searches guided by metadata is a significant feature of the MLDP. Although data analysis itself is typically
performed on time-series data, identifying the target time-series datasets is greatly simplified using
attributes, properties, and other artifacts attached to the data. The Web Application and Demonstration
Application have request building features built into their user interfaces, essentially making the process
seamless. The gRPC interface for the Query Service (and Annotation Service) contains the metadata query
operations and is covered in detail within Subsection 2.6 concerning the Data Platform Communication
Framework.
From MLDP prototype evaluations in Phase I it was found that advanced data processing within the Query
Service creates significant performance costs for all active clients; note that the Query Service can
simultaneously serve multiple clients. Thus, the new design facilitates distributed data processing between
the Query Service and its clients. Specifically, clients can request “raw” time-series data when expected
result sets are large (say greater than 10 Mbytes). In this case the client is required to re-assemble the raw
data, from its MongoDB storage format, into the final tabular datasets common in data science applications.
Thus, the task of request assembly is off-loaded from the Query Service which then focuses solely on data
location and retrieval. The feature is available within the JAL library and described more fully in
Subsection 2.12.
Currently maximum data rates for large time-series request recovery and assembly are confirmed at 200
MBps for Release 1.8.0. With the addition of the client serialization feature in Data Platform Release 1.9.0
we expect another performance boost, with projected rates in the range 400 to 500 MBps. These are
estimates from the Query Service benchmarking utility and have yet to be confirmed with the performance
measuring applications within the JAL library. These numbers will be available once JAL Release 1.10.0
is completed. Again, all measured data rates were taken from a single development platform hosting the
Data Platform, the JAL library, and the evaluation applications. Additional gains are expected with
specialized hardware and horizontal scaling of the Data Platform installation.
The above performance numbers are significant achievements. Query Service performance is an important
aspect of the MLDP, although not as significant as that for the Ingestion Service. Since query operations
contain lookups and retrievals (clients can perform re-assembly) we expect performance to be below that
for the Ingestion Service. However, our objective was to satisfy some real-time applications and
consequently desired performance to be comparable with the Ingestion Service. The current estimates
suggest that the Query Service data rates are about half that for the Ingestion Service.
Annotation Service
The Annotations Service allows clients (i.e., Data Consumers) to annotate the data archive with comments,
data associates, post-ingestion calculations, and other artifacts (e.g., tags, attributes, names, etc.). These
annotations are then available to all other Data Platform users for inspection, analysis, and creation of
additional annotations. The annotations feature allows Data Consumers to create “value-added” content
through interaction with the data archive, which is then available to all other users. A use case diagram for
the Annotation Service is shown in Figure 17 which includes its association with the Query Service (an
association is also shown in Figure 16).

 42

 Figure 17: Annotation Service use-case diagram

The Annotation Service required a significant development effort, beyond that originally anticipated in the
Phase IIA Project Narrative. The prolonged effort derived primary from the unexpected design phase for
the annotations data model and the decision to implement data exporting within the MLDP, a feature not
originally scoped within the project but one that customers requested. (The design of the annotations data
model is covered below.) The initial Annotation Service implementation was available in Data Platform
Release 1.3.0 in April of 2024. This initial implementation only featured the ability to identify and create
archive datasets, which is a sophisticated process and is described further below. Dataset identification and
creation, being critical to the annotations data model, was confirmed with the first Annotation Service
version. The basic annotations feature did not appear until Release 1.4.0, specifically, only comment
annotations. Due to its convoluted nature, the original Annotation Service API was redesigned in Release
1.5.0; the interface was essentially flattened and simplified to support all annotation types. The data export
feature of the Annotations Service appeared in Release 1.6.0. Note that both data annotation and data
exporting require the ability to identify datasets within the archive, thus, the Annotation Service was the
natural setting for data exporting. The fully featured Annotation Service, including calculations annotations
and the ability to export calculations concurrently with archived data, did not appear until Release 1.8.0 in
March of 2025. Thus, the Annotation Service required over a year of sustained development with
approximately 6 man-months of effort, over 3x that expected at project start. However, the annotations
capability is essential to the MLDP product, requested by Osprey DCS customers before project inception.
Thus, a robust Annotation Service with stable API was critical to project success and represents a project
milestone.
Datasets can be exported in several formats, comma separate values (CSV), HDF5 format, and Excel
workbooks. Clearly there are restrictions on specific data types for export according to the export format.
The data type and export format are checked internally by the Annotation Service; attempting to export
heterogeneous data types in an unsupported export format will result in an exception. Specifically, only
scalar values are allowed for CSV and Excel workbook exports, while HDF5 supports all data types
including complex types such as arrays, images, and data structures.
Referring to Figure 17 we see that Annotation Service use cases offered to Data Consumers all rely on the
Identify Dataset use case. For example, for a Data Consumer to perform an annotation, the Annotate
Dataset use case, it must first identify the dataset for annotation. Likewise for data exporting, the Export
Dataset use case, the exported dataset must first be identified. To query for a specific archive annotation a
Data Consumer follows the Request Building use case offered by an application user interface or the
Annotation API directly. Note that the objective of request building is to identify the dataset containing the

 43

annotation; generally, this involves an archive search using archive metadata. The Web Application and
the Demonstration Application both provide tools for request building. The Web Application supports the
identification of datasets and exporting of datasets, as well as basic comment and association annotations.
The Demonstration Application supports calculation annotations.

 Figure 18: the MLDP data archive query domain

The Annotations Service is a sophisticated component of the MLDP requiring an extended development
effort as described above. Crucial to the Annotation Service was its data model developed in the design
phase early in 2024. The first step in designing the data model was defining an annotation, and exactly that
which is being annotated (i.e., the annotation target). Our approach is shown in the conceptual diagram of
Figure 18. There the entire MDLP time-series data archive is depicted as a quadrant of a 2-dimensional
query domain with the axes of time and data sources (i.e. Process Variables). This representation can also
be viewed as a universal spreadsheet for the MLDP data archive. Regardless, the intent is to define a
collection of well-defined “basis sets” that cover the entire query domain. Such basis sets are depicted as
the Data Blocks within the figure. Each data block is composed of a time interval [ti, ti+1] and a set of data
sources {PVi, PVj, PVk, …}; note that the data sources need not be contiguous, however, the time interval
[ti, ti+1] is assumed continuous. Thus, any data block is described as the set [ti, ti+1]´{PVi, PVj, PVk, …}.
Clearly all data blocks form a basis set which covers the entire query domain, and we can use collections
of data blocks to identify any portion of the archive forming the target for any archive annotation. We call
any such collection of data blocks a dataset. These concepts are formalized in the design of the Annotation
Service, and its API is based upon this model. There are operations within the Annotation Service API that
are specifically offered for the Identify Dataset use case in Figure 17 (see Subsection 2.6 for an API
description).
Archive annotations are maintained in separate archives within the Data Platform (i.e., separate document
collections with the MongoDB database). It was originally planned to store calculation annotations within
the time-series archive since both share the same format and, thus, could be processed using components
that were already implemented for the Ingestion and Query Services. However, it was decided that a much
more robust, and “cleaner”, approach was to create a separate archive for all annotations. The intent was
to isolate any annotations, including calculations, from the original time-series data thus removing any
obfuscation between the two collections. The two archives represent fundamentally different data aspects

 44

supporting different use cases. For example, time-series data is generally ingested then archived
indefinitely, while annotations can be continually modified through their existence (including, possibly,
deletion). Our preference for the separate archive design was motivated by these considerations. The Data
Platform now has separate archives for time-series data, archive metadata, and archive annotations.
Ingestionstream Service
The Ingestionstream Service is part of the Advanced Data Sciences portion of the Phase IIA project. The
concept was to provide direct access to an active ingestion datastream for real-time applications. The
Advance Data Sciences not only included ingestion stream access, but also real-time processing of ingestion
stream data. The original intent was to provide a plugin-framework for exceptional data-science algorithms.
However, such a general framework is difficult to support within the Data Platform Communications
Framework; it is essentially beyond the scope of gRPC communications. Nevertheless, it was found that a
sub-class of algorithms could be supported, those of Data Event monitoring. There we provide clients
notification of user-defined Data Events and Data Conditions seen within the ingestion stream (e.g., trigger
values, statistical correlations, support vector movements, etc.), to which they can respond accordingly.
The Ingestionstream Service required a substantial development effort and was designed and built in stages.
A prototype Ingestionstream Service first appeared in Data Platform Release 1.7.0. It communicated with
the Ingestion Service and had a client API, but client operations were not implemented. The release also
contained an enhanced Ingestion Service which offered a Subscribe/Publish service for PV values,
necessary communication with the Ingestionstream Service. An operational Ingestionstream Service was
first available in Release 1.10.0. The service supports a basic PV value monitoring event. The JAL library
is being considered for the general algorithm plugin framework, since it already has an application
framework and offers high-level support for plugins. However, we are also considering other technologies
such as Emscripten [52] with WebAssembly (Wasm) [53] and the Cling interpreter within the Root
framework [54] for employing inline compilation techniques. Nonetheless, the Ingestionstream Service
represents an important contribution to the Advanced Data Sciences effort, an ambitious task, and, thus, a
significant accomplishment within the project. As described below, an algorithm plugin framework likely
requires Data Event Monitoring offered by the Ingestionstream Service.
Figure 19 shows a use-case diagram for the Ingestionstream Service. There we see the actor Real-Time
Application which is a sub-class of
Data Consumer. The actor requires
fast access to MLDP data,
specifically, fast enough to do real-
time calculations represented by
the Real-Time Processing use case
in the diagram. Online control
applications in facility or
experimental operations are
examples within this use case.
Depending upon real-time
application requirements the,
Query Service may provide
sufficient response, this situation is Figure 19: Ingestionstream Service use-case diagram
also indicated in the diagram. The
Query Service has the broadest
accessibility to the MLDP archive and has superior performance capabilities (in the 100s of MBps). Thus,
the Query Service is the recommended method for general archive access whenever its performance is
sufficient for the intended application. However, there are situations where direct access to the ingestion
datastream is necessary. As seen in Figure 19 both the Ingestion Service and the Ingestionstream Service
are available for the Real-Time Processing use case.

 45

The Real-Time Processing use case implies that data access must be fast enough for online applications,
say control applications adjusting operating parameters in real time. More loosely, real-time processing
enables the control of some aspect of a facility in real time. Providing access to the ingestion datastream
yields the fastest possible data response available from the Data Platform. Note that ingestion data has
already been correlated by the Aggregator, thus, there are advantages to ingestion datastream access rather
than direct monitoring of individual Process Variables within a facility (i.e., directly through the control
system). In Figure 19 we see that the Ingestion Service provides direct client access to the ingestion
datastream in the form of a Subscribe/Publish service for PV values. This feature was originally developed
specifically for the Ingestionstream Service but is now available to all Data Platform clients. The intent of
the Ingestionstream Service is to provide additional real-time processing capabilities of the ingestion stream
data.
The Ingestionstream Service supports the Data Event Monitoring use case shown in Figure 19. The
currently supported Data Event is a basic trigger event: the Data Event consists of an event trigger, an event
window, and a data event notification. An event trigger is composed of a Process Variable, a trigger value,
and a trigger conditional such as equals, greater than, less than, etc. An event trigger can contain a collection
of PV triggers where all trigger conditions must be met before the data event is recognized. The event
notification can be a simple response indicating that the trigger condition(s) occurred. Or the client can
optionally register to receive a collection of Process Variable values within the sample count or time
window (with optional window offset). More sophisticated Data Events can be implemented in the future.

 46

Figure 20 shows the basic control flow required for an application performing a Data Event Request. It
depicts the basic event sequence for this operation and was used to elicit the requirements of the interface
design. The request requires an event definition object (denoted EventDef in the diagram) which includes
a collection of Process Variable names involved in the event (explicitly shown as PVs in the diagram). As
seen in the left-hand side of the diagram the request must first go through a cascade of exception checking,
specifically, is the ingestion stream active, are the required Process Variables within the ingestion stream,

 Figure 20: Ingestionstream Service Data Event control flow and interactions

is the event well-defined. Only when all conditions are valid is Data Event Monitoring enabled. The client
then receives events as a series of published DataEvent objects until either the client terminates the
subscription, or the ingestion stream terminates.
The right-hand side of Figure 20 shows the interactions between the Ingestion Service and the
Ingestionstream Service, along with the required sequence of events. The Data Event Request is initiated
with the operation dataEventRequst(). Note that within the initial Data Event Request sequence two
subservices are created, the Source Monitor and the Event Monitor. The Source Monitor provides a
subscription service to the Process Variables required within the Data Event definition and is created upon
subscription request. The Event Monitor monitors the published Process Variable values provided by the
Source Monitor and is only created if the Process Variable subscription succeeds. Once active the Event
monitor analyzing the Process Variable values for the required data conditions and publishes the events as
DataEvent objects whenever detected (the publishEvent() method). Within Figure 20 the Event Monitor

 47

is created by the Ingestionstream Service. The location for the Source Monitor is the Ingestion Service
which provides the PV value Subscribe/Publish service.
Ingestion datastream access and processing within the Data Platform is a complex process and we present
the basic architectural framework to appreciate its sophistication. The approach was to first implement
ingestion stream access within the Ingestion Service, implement the Ingestionstream Service supporting
Data Events, then consider possibilities for future development of algorithm plugins. Our objective was to
address the Advance Data Sciences effort as much as possible within the project timeline.

 Figure 21: Ingestionstream Service data flow diagram

 First consider the basic data flow required for the Ingestionstream Service, as presented in Figure 21. The
diagram represents the basic processes necessary for the Ingestionstream Service; it also includes the
potential for algorithm plugins at the bottom of the diagram. Implied at the top of the diagram is the
Ingestion Service actively ingesting time-series data from a Data Provider and storing it to the Data Platform
archive. Branching from the Ingestion Service are several abstract processes in cascade, the Data Source
Monitor, the Data Event Monitor, and finally the Data Processor representing a Plugin. The Ingestion
Service offers a PV value subscription service represented as the Data Source Monitor, which isolates
specific data sources (e.g., Process Variables) within the ingestion stream. The Data Source Monitor then
provides values of these data sources to the Data Event Monitor for Data Event determination within the
ingestion datastream. These events are then published by the Event Monitor as a Data Event object; this is
the responsibility of the Ingestionstream Service. The bottom of the diagram indicates that we are exploring
the possibility of implementing the ingestion stream data processing capability as a consumer of Data
Events, represented as Data Processor. That is, algorithmic processing would be based upon Data Events,
which the algorithm defines. The Data Processor subscribes to its desired data events, processes them
according to the Plugin algorithm, then returns the calculation as some form of Data Product (this could be

 48

as simple as delivering a sampling process time slice). This data flow appears to facilitate the plugin
framework we desire.
A more detailed architectural perspective of the Ingestion Service/Ingestionstream Service collaboration is
presented in Figure 22. The top of the diagram provides the situational context for the architecture. We
see two actors, a Data Provider in the process of data ingestion, and an Application engaging with the
Ingestionstream Service. The Ingestion Service and the Ingestionstream Service are coupled (tightly)
through gRPC communications as they necessarily collaborate. The application initiates Data Event
monitoring by supplying the Ingestionstream Service with a DataEventDef object defining the desired event.

 Figure 22: Ingestionstream Service architectural diagram

Within the diagram we see that a DataEventDef object contains a client-supplied unique event identifier
(attribute typeUid), the data sources involved in the event (attribute PVs), and an additional object
DataEventCondition (attribute condition). The latter object is rather sophisticated containing the means

 49

identifying the Data Event, specifically the trigger() method. Also shown is the potential for additional
processing whenever the desired data conditions are recognized, specifically, with the calculate() method.
The figure also indicates the inclusion of a state machine and that the calculation may additionally involve
some type of state vector representing past data events. The current implementation contains a finite-length
data buffer for storage of past and future PV values whenever a trigger condition is met, a basic state
machine. However, as indicated in the diagram, true state processing may be best delegated to algorithmic
processing within the plugin framework.
In Figure 22 we see the components Source Monitor and Event Monitor. These components are tightly
coupled where the Event Monitor receives ingestion stream data from the Source Monitor as a subscription
service. The subscription is represented by the association class SourceSubscription which defines the data
sources and the time window for the values. The Event Monitor uses a DataEventCondition instance to
identify data events within the source subscription. Events are then published as DataEvent objects which
are passed to the client through the IngeststreamAPI interface. The Source Monitor component is in the
Ingestion Service, and the Event Monitor is in the Ingestionstream Service; performance considerations
determined this outcome.
The bottom of Figure 22 depicts a potential architecture for ingestion stream algorithmic processing, which
is currently hypothetical. As discussed above, the location and implementation are still under consideration,
with the JAL library currently being the most suitable candidate. Like with Data Events, algorithmic
processing is initiated with an algorithm definition object, AlgorithmDef, supplied by the client application.
The object contains a unique algorithm type identifier (attribute typeUid), the Data Events that it requires
(attribute events), and the desired algorithm represented by an Algorithm class instance. The processing is
performed by the PluginExecutor component, which receives Data Events from the Event Monitor service.
This component relies upon the (client-supplied) Plugin instance which realizes the processing. As shown
in the diagram the Plugin performs its processing on a collection of DataEvent objects (i.e., method
calculate()) then advances its state with the nextState() method. The PluginExecutor component then
supplies the results of the ingestion stream processing as DataProduct objects. The exact form of the
algorithm framework is under investigation.
A basic outline of the Ingestionstream Service architecture is shown in Figure 23. Note that this diagram
is from the initial design and the current implementation differs somewhat, but the general characteristics
are accurate. Also note that the diagram assumes that all Data Event request validation has occurred and
that the Data Event subscription is now active. At the top of the diagram is the PV subscription service
offered by the Ingestion Service, this is represented by the block SourceSubscription. There we see that
the subscription service monitors the active ingestion datastream selecting only PV values for the collection
of requested PV names (field pvNames). It provides subscription data to the Ingestionstream Service in the
form of DataFrame messages. A separate Ingestionstream Service process, Subscription Worker, manages
the incoming data directing it to the appropriate processing element. It is the first stage of the
Ingestionstream processing pipeline.

 50

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

The encircled collaboration in the middle of the diagram (i.e., the collaboration containing the
RawDataCorrelator and RawCorrelatedData classes) is included for cases where the subscription data
requires real-time alignment (i.e., by timestamp). In most situations this additional alignment process

 Figure 23: Ingestionstream Service basic architecture

would not be required as the ingestion stream data has already been aligned by the Aggregator system and
enters the pipeline as correlated data. However, there are use cases with multiple, concurrent ingestion
streams where the incoming subscription streams would then be potentially misaligned (e.g., PV values

 51

sourced from differing sample clocks); it is important to consider this situation and provide for it if
necessary. Thus, the Ingestion Service should provide metadata concerning the current ingestion
datastream and resulting subscription data to avoid this intermediate processing step whenever possible
(e.g., does the subscription contain samples from separate ingestion streams?). We note that the additional
correlator collaboration has already been built as part of the Java API Library (JAL) Query Service API
and is available for the Ingestionstream Service implementation.
The active process Buffer Manager receives the subscription data once it is fully aligned. It is the
responsibility of this process to maintain the data window buffer, RawDataBuffer, and ensure that it is current
with the data subscription. The RawDataBuffer is an active class implementing a finite length data buffer
for PV samples. Its structure and operation are defined by the DataEventWindow message fields. The buffer
manager supplies the data window buffer with Process Variable samples. Samples outside the data window
fall out of the buffer at regular intervals governed by the buffer length (be it time duration or sample count).
Note that the data buffer can contain future sample values as well as past values, this determination being
made by its length, size or duration field, and the offset field in the DataEventWindow message.
The data condition trigger is represented by the active process Event Trigger, which monitors the aligned
subscription data. When a data condition trigger is detected the Event Trigger notifies the RawDataBuffer
to begin the window fill operation, blocking until complete. The buffer releases once the data window is
complete, then its contents sent to the client within a Data Event response.
We note that it is possible to receive data event notifications without data or operations. That is, the
Ingestionstream Service supports the use case where clients only receive notification of a requested data
condition. The client would then perform their own custom operations related to that data condition (e.g.,
a query operation, dataset annotation, etc.). That is, the data condition trigger mechanism and its availability
have merit on its own right.
 2.6. Data Platform Communication Framework
The design and development of a robust and stable API is a primary factor in most all software products;
the Data Platform is no exception considering its size and sophistication. The API for large software
systems necessitate considerable forethought as they cannot be easily modified once established. The Data
Platform Communication Framework required a significant development effort; it was redesigned three
times and saw continued refinement throughout the project. Thus, the framework itself is an important
project accomplishment representing a milestone in development.
The Data Platform Communications Framework is required for all Core Services communications and low-
level client interactions. The framework is based upon Google’s gRPC library [25] utilizing Protocol
Buffers interface definition language [30]. The framework is contained in the Github repository
https://github.com/osprey-dcs/dp-grpc (i.e., ref. [16]). The repository contains five Protocol Buffers files
(“proto” meta-language files) each defining a service interface and the data messages required by the
interface. The repository is configured to generate and locally install the Data Platform Communications
Framework in the Java programming language, which is the implementation language of the Data Platform.
However, Data Platform users can download and build the communications framework in the language of
their choice using the Protocol Buffers compiler “protoc”. Note that Protocol Buffers supports most all
common high-level programming languages. Thus, although the Data Platform is built in Java, clients can
communicate directly with the Core Services in any programming language. Appendix II is available for
those who wish to create Data Platform interfaces in other languages, specifically C++ and Python.
The interfaces and data messages of the Data Platform Communication Framework are presented here in
the Unified Modeling Language (UML) format, rather than choosing a programming language. Although
the Data Platform builds the framework in Java, having a language-neutral description of the APIs is more
beneficial for a general audience, specifically for clients who wish to communicate in other languages. The
structure of the high-level language interfaces and data messages generated by Protocol Buffers can vary
greatly depending upon the programming language used.

 52

 Figure 24: overview of Data Platform Communications Framework

Figure 24 presents an overview of the Data Platform Communications Framework along with its
relationships to Protocol Buffers, gRPC, and the Common Core Services Framework described in
Subsection 2.7 below. The communications framework is used both for internal Data Platform
communication, and for external client communications with Data Platform Core Services. The framework
is defined completely by the following five files (not shown in Figure 24 is file ingestion_stream.proto):
 1. common.proto – contains the common messages and types exchanged by all Core Services.
 2. ingestion.proto – contains the Ingestion Service interface and messages specific to that service.
 3. query.proto – contains the Query Service interface and messages specific to that service.
 4. annotation.proto – contains the Annotation Service interface and its specific messages.
 5. ingestion_stream.proto – contains the Ingestionstream Service interface and its specific messages.
Note from the figure that the Protocol Buffers meta-language compiler, protoc, generates a unique service
interface class from each “proto” file defining a Core Service interface. The file common,proto contains
data messages common to all service interfaces and is imported into all other “proto” files. The generated
service interface classes are DpIngestionServiceGrpc, DpQueryServiceGrpc, DpAnnotationServiceGrpc, and
IngestionStreamService for the Ingestion Service, Query Service, Annotation Service, and Ingestionstream
Service respectively. These classes provide both the client-side and service-side interfaces and act as front
ends to the gRPC library. The interface-defining proto files also contain data messages specific to the
interfaces they define. Note from Figure 24 that clients specifically require gRPC library Channel instances
for service communications, while services rely on gRPC Server instances for client communications. The

 53

gRPC library is utilized for data transport but interaction is greatly simplified by the Protocol Buffers
interfaces which hide most gRPC implementation details (i.e., other than Channel and Server objects).
Figure 24 also includes the basic relationship between the Data Platform Communication Framework and
the Data Platform Common Core Service Framework covered next in Subsection 2.7. The two frameworks
are coupled via the Protocol Buffers generated service interfaces. As seen in the diagram the Common
Core Service Framework provides an active class for managing the gRPC Server instance required for any
service. As we shall see it also provides base classes for common service functions where child classes
perform operations specific to the service.
We cover in detail the Data Platform Communication Framework at the time of this writing, specifically,
Release 1.11.0. The presentation also serves to document the Data Platform itself and to emphasis the
robustness of the current design. The discussions are not comprehensive; however, the diagrams do contain
most aspects of each interface. The essential features necessary to address the service API objectives are
also covered in the narrative.
Common Messages
Figure 25 contains some important common messages within the common.proto file, along with their
relationships. The diagram is not exhaustive but contains the data messages necessary for essential Data

 Figure 25: Data Platform Communication Framework common messages

 54

Platform operations such as ingestion, archiving, and time-series data request. In particular, the diagram
demonstrates how the issue of heterogeneous data transport and storage is addressed. There we see that a
data value, represented by the message DataValue in the lower right-hand side, consists of a union of various
data types, including scalars, arrays, structures, and images. That is, the DataValue message consists of a
single value whose type is identified by the union field. Note that the complex data types of Array,
Structure, and Image are also defined in common.proto, they are shown in Figure 26 below. Protocol Buffers
automatically serializes the value according to its type and once serialized the value can be recovered by
name or identified with a Protocol Buffers generated type enumeration (not shown). Note that all DataValue
messages contain a valueStatus field (of type ValueStatus). This field contains any status information
concerning the data value and is analogous to the alarm and status fields of EPICS Process Variables. As
seen in the diagram the valueStatus field contains condition information of the data value that can arise
from acquisition, through transport, to final storage and archiving.
Note further in Figure 25 that the ValueStatus message is defined as an enclosed class of the DataValue
message; that is, enclosed message definitions are supported by the Protocol Buffers meta-language. This
configuration is typically applied within the communication framework whenever an internal message field
type only appears in the scope of the parent message. The practice reduces namespace clutter and clarifies
the message context.
A sampling process is represented by the DataColumn message, which is simply an ordered aggregation of
DataValue messages along with a name field (presumably the Process Variable name). The timestamps for
a sampling process are contained in a separate DataTimeStamps message. The sampling process and its
timestamps are then combined in the DataBucket message forming the entire time-series, whose format and
name reflects the Data Platform internal storage structure for time-series data. Note that the DataBucket
message contains a variety of metadata fields that can be associated with any sampling process. Note
further that a sampling process can be of any heterogeneous type supported by a DataValue message.
The SerializedDataColumn message is the primary component of the client serialization option feature
included in Data Platform Release 1.9.0. Here clients can perform the serialization of DataColumn messages
themselves, rather than allowing the automatic serialization by Protocol Buffers. Implied in the Figure 25
is that SerializedDataColumn messages are realized with the toByteString() operation of the target
DataColumn message (i.e., once the DataColumn message is populated). A DataBucket message can then
contain either a DataColumn instance or a SerializedDataColumn instance, the attribute being a field union.
Client serialization reduces the number of serialization/de-serialization operations performed by Protocol
Buffers during transport and by the Data Platform during archiving and recovery (since sampling process
data is stored in its serialized form). The client serialization option increases data transport and archiving
performance by at least a factor 2x.
The timestamps for a sampling process are specified with the DataTimestamps message. It contains either
a SamplingClock message or a TimestampList message, the attribute being a field union. Specifically, the
timestamps can be specified explicitly with an ordered list of Timestamp messages, TimestampList, or
implicitly via a uniform sampling clock, SamplingClock, where sample times are specified by start time,
period, and duration (i.e., field “count”). Support for TimestampList messages was introduced in Release
1.6. This capability is available for situations where spurious data is expected, or sampling is otherwise
nonuniform. Best practice is to avoid the use of timestamp lists whenever possible, since they require
significantly more overhead and resources than sampling clocks. However, the timestamp list feature is
available whenever required.
The above design represents a robust solution to the requirement of heterogeneous data ingestion and
archiving. It also presents further advantages in query operations as data can be returned as DataColumn
messages without modification (or SerializedDataColumn messages). This advantage is also prevalent
within the Annotation Service where datasets can be similarly returned.

 55

 Figure 26: Data Platform Communications Framework complex data types

The complex data types Array, Image, and Structure are shown in Figure 26. The Image type is quite basic;
it contains the raw image data as a byte string and an enumeration specifying the image format. The Array
type is simply a vector of DataValue instances. Recall, however, that the DataValue type can contain an
Array instance; thus, multi-dimensional arrays are formed by nesting Array instances. For example, an
MxN matrix is formed by first populating the M matrix rows as N-length Array instances, then assigning
them as elements of the parent Array instance (this forms a row-packed matrix, a column-packed matrix is
formed by first populating the N M-length columns). Currently it is the responsibility of the client to assure
proper formatting of multi-dimensional arrays. A data structure, type Structure, exploits the same
