mechanism. Seen in Figure 26 is that a Structure is a collection of (name, value) pairs represented by the
enclosed Field message. The value attribute of Field is again a DataValue type which can contain
additional (nested) Structure instances. Thus, data structures can be built as wide and as deep as the client
desires. Note further, that complex data types can be cross populated, for example we can have an array of
data structures, an array of images, or a data structure of arrays. Finally, the message CalculationSpec is
included in common.proto; this message is used in the data exporting of calculation annotations alongside
archive datasets.
Ingestion Service API
The Ingestion Service was the first Data Platform Core Service to be addressed in the project, the first
version appearing in Release 1.0. The Ingestion Service is depicted in Figure 4 within the context of all
Data Platform services. Its operation was described previously in Subsection 2.5 containing the use-case
diagram of Figure 15. Here we describe the fundamentals of the API shown in Figure 4. Again, the
narrative is not exhaustive, we cover the major features necessary to perform Ingestion Service operations.
However, all operations and data messages within the ingestion.proto file defining the Ingestion Service
communications do appear in the following figures.

 56

 Figure 27: Ingestion Service communication framework interface and data messages

The gRPC interface (i.e., the API) to the Ingestion Service, DpIngestionService, defined in file
ingestion.proto is shown at the top of Figure 27. In Figure 27 (which shows both interface and data
messages) we see that the interface is narrow and relatively simple. The interface has 6 operations
supporting 4 functions:
 1. Data Provider Registration – providers first obtain their UID for subsequent data ingestion,
 2. Data Ingestion – multiple operations are offered for time-series data ingestion,
 3. Ingestion Data Archiving Status – determination of data archiving status post ingestion,
 4. Ingestion Datastream Access – subscription to specific ingestion stream time-series data.
It is necessary for any Data Provider client to first register with the Ingestion Service using operation
registerProvider()before data can be transmitted; the registration messages RegisterProviderRequest and
RegisterProviderResponse are shown within the box in the lower right-hand side of Figure 27. Provider

 57

registration is necessary to maintain data provenance and the integrity of the MLDP data archive. The Data
Platform maintains a registry of all contributing Data Providers to which it assigns a unique provider
identifier (i.e., the provider UID). Data Providers can supply metadata for self-identification during
registration such as descriptions, tags, and attributes; this information is useful when performing archive
searches for request building. Note from Figure 27 that Data Providers can also attach metadata to the
ingested data in the form of attribute pairs (e.g., equipment identifiers, location identifiers, experiment
identifiers, etc.) and event metadata (e.g., snapshot identifier, trigger time and event, etc.).
Data Ingestion Messages
All data ingestion is performed using the common IngestDataRequest message, regardless of the specific
ingestion operation used, of which there are 3. Specifically, in all ingestion operations clients supply data
using IngestDataRequest messages. These request messages contain an IngestionDataFrame instance, or
“data frame”, the standard unit of ingestion. The IngestionDataFrame message (i.e., the “data frame”) is
essentially an aggregation of sampling processes represented by the DataColumn messages and/or
SerialializedDataColumn messages, along with their collective timestamps represented by the
DataTimestamps message. Note that each IngestDataRequest must contain both the Data Provider unique
identifier (UID) (supplied by the Ingestion Service upon provider registration) and a client request unique
identifier supplied by the client. The client request UID is necessary for post-ingestion status lookups
discussed below. Provider registration was discussed above. Finally, the client can also supply optional
metadata for each ingestion request.
There are 3 operations supporting data ingestion:
 1. ingestData() – unary blocking, synchronous exchange of single request and response.
 2. ingestDataStream() – unidirectional, asynchronous data streaming with single response.
 3. ingestDataBidiStream() – bidirectional, asynchronous data streaming with acknowledge responses.
The ingestData() operation is a standard unary RPC function call while the remaining operations are data
streaming operations supported by the gRPC library. Unary ingestion is a blocking, synchronous operation
where the client sends a single request and then receives a single response when the operation is fully
complete. Unlike unary gRPC ingestion, the latter cases maintain an open datastream between client and
service where messages are transmitted as fast as possible. Operation ingestDataStream() was introduced
in Release 1.5; there a forward, unidirectional data stream from client to Ingestion Service is established.
The client sends all its ingestion data over the stream then receives a single confirmation response when
the stream is terminated. This is contrary to the bidirectional streaming operation ingestDataBidiStream()
where the client receives confirmation responses for each ingestion request. It was hoped that unidirectional
streaming would yield significant performance gains but, so far, only marginal gains in ingestion
performance has been observed. However, unidirectional ingestion is generally better.
The ingestion responses, either IngestDataResponse or IngestDataStreamResponse messages, contain either
acknowledgments or exception notifications. The IngestDataResponse message applies to both the unary
and bidirectional streaming operations, as it is a response to a single ingestion request. The
IngestDataStreamResponse is unique as it contains a summary of all the streaming activity. Thus, it contains
a collection of all client request UIDs received during streaming, along with a list of all those UIDs rejected
by the Ingestion Service.
Ingestion Status Messages
The operation queryRequestStatus() within the Ingestion Service API allows clients to determine the
archiving status of an ingestion request post-ingestion. For performance considerations the Ingestion
Service queues archiving requests for later processing, rather than fully processing all incoming requests
before releasing. Although transport and streaming exceptions are reported immediately, an archiving
operation may eventually fail; it is possible to determine the integrity of all ingested data post-ingestion.

 58

 Figure 28: Ingestion Service ingestion request status messages

The messages required for operation queryRequestStatus() are shown in Figure 28. As seen in the diagram
the process is rather sophisticated supporting a variety of status criteria. (We see in the sequel that this type
of multi-criteria request support is common in the Data Platform Communication Framework.) Referring
to Figure 28, the message QueryRequestStatusRequest defining the status request is a composite of request
criteria all defined by enclosed message QueryRequestStatusCriterion. The latter message is a field union
where ingestion status can be requested by the provider UID, provider name, request UID, the status itself,
or the time range of ingestion. Each criterion is defined by an enclosed message type within the parent type
QueryRequestStatusCriterion, respectively, ProviderIdCriterion, ProviderNameCriterion,
RequestIdCriterion, StatusCriterion, and TimeRangeCriterion. Note again, that the enclosed message
definitions prevent namespace collisions, as other service interfaces use similar multi-criteria naming
conventions. All criteria within a status request are combined as filters, that is, with an ‘AND’ operation.
For example, the client can request all its ingestion operations that failed within a specified time range (e.g.,
a day) by supplying 3 QueryRequestStatusCriterion instances: 1) a ProviderIdCriterion set with its UID,
a 2) StatusCriterion set to INGESTION_REJECTED, and 3) a TimeRangeCriterion set with the desired time

 59

interval. The client is returned an QueryRequestStatusResponse message which is an aggregation of all
status results matching the request.

 Figure 29: Ingestion Service datastream access subscribe/publish messages

Ingestion Datastream Access Messages
Ingestion datastream access is provided with the subscribeData() operation of the DpIngestionService
interface. Shown in Figure 29 is SubscribeDataRequest message which clients provide to initiate the
subscription. If successful, the client subsequently receives the published data as SubscribeDataResponse
messages in return. Although this is a bidirectional streaming operation, only 2 SubscribeDataRequest
messages are permitted from the client: 1) the first message initiating the subscription, and 2) the second
message canceling the subscription. If this protocol is violated the Ingestion Service sends an exception,
then terminates the connection.
To accommodate the above protocol the SubscribeDataRequest message contains a field union containing
either a NewSubscription message or a CancelSubscription message; both message types are enclosed
within the SubscribeDataRequest parent message. The NewSubscription message contains the collection
of Process Variable names forming the subscription request while the CancelSubscription message is
empty, simply signaling that the client is canceling the subscription. Referring again to Figure 29 the
SubscribeDataResponse is also a field union, in additional with a timestamp indicating the time instant of
the Ingestion Service response (most Data Platform response messages contain such a timestamp). Upon
initiating the data subscription, the client receives either an acknowledgment, type AckResult, or an
exception, type ExceptionalResult. The acknowledgment signifies that the subscription request was
accepted (i.e., the requested PV values are all available within a live ingestion datastream) and subscription
data is henceforth to follow. The exceptional result signifies a rejected subscription request, for example,
there is no active ingestion datastream, at least one PV value was not present within all active streams, or a
general error occurred (see exception message). If the subscription request is rejected the Ingestion Service
closes the data stream after sending the exception. If the subscription request is accepted, the client then
receives a stream of SubscribeDataResult messages containing the subscription data. Note that the
SubscribeDataResult message has the same format as our ingestion data frames. The subscription data
continues to stream until the client either sends a CancelSubcription message or closes the connection.

 60

Query Service API
The basic design, structure, and fundamental operations of the Query Service were completed early in the
project, in Data Platform Release 1.2. Additional features and operations have been added in subsequent
releases. The most important factor concerning the Query Service is its features and functionality, as this
is the aspect of the seen by MLDP end users. Specifically, in addition to time-series data queries, metadata
support is particularly important for archive searches and request building for time-series dataset
identification. Performance is a also concern, but not as vital as for that for the Ingestion Service. The
functionality of the Query Service was discussed in Subsection 2.5, where the use-case diagram of Figure
16 was presented. The Query Service implementation now complies with the Common Core Services
Framework discussed in Subsection 2.7. Here, we cover the basic Query Service use cases and API
structure for their support.

 Figure 30: Query Service communications framework interface

The communications interface for the Query Service, DpQueryService, is in file query.proto and
represented in Figure 30. All required functionality of the Query Service is supported by a relatively narrow
interface creating a robust design. However, the messages needed to support the interface can have the
sophisticated structure seen before in Figure 28 for ingestion status requests, particularly for the metadata
requests. There are 3 classes of queries supported by the Query Service:
 1. Time-Series Data Queries: There are 2 types of operations: 1) raw data queries, and 2) table data
 queries. Raw data operations are prefixed with queryData…() and require a QueryDataRequest
 message to initiate. They return heterogeneous, correlated, time-series data in “raw” format. The
 queryTable() operation returns time-series data in tabular format.

 2. Data Provider Queries: This is the single operation queryProviders(). It provides information
 about all Data Providers that have contributed to the MLDP archive, including names, UIDs,
 associated Process Variables, and times when they were active.
 3. Metadata Queries: These operations are suffixed with ...Metadata() (i.e., queryPvMetadata() and
 queryProviderMetadata()). Metadata queries return information about the MLDP data archive
 itself. Typically, metadata query results are used for archive searches, and time-series data and
 annotations request building, that is, request refinement. Currently the Query Service supports
 metadata concerning Data Providers and the Process Variables. Eventually all metadata
 information acquired during the ingestion process will also be available.

 61

The different forms of the time-series data queries (i.e., operations prefixed with queryData…()) are
available for either convenience or performance. Clearly the queryDataTable() operation is the most
convenient, returning results in tabular form through a unary, blocking RPC operation. However, it is not
fast, it is size limited, and it does not support all the features of the Query Service. Clients desiring
maximum performance are provided the option of streaming raw time-series query results with operations
queryDataStream() and queryDataBidiStream(); these are the fastest possible data transfer options.
However, the burden of data reconstruction is then left to the client, a design decision enabling distributed
data processing. Best performance dictated that some data processing be offloaded to clients. Otherwise,
data processing wait times for a single client are shared among all clients of a Query Service instance. The
Java API library (JAL) performs the time-series data reconstruction on the client side, thus, unburdening
the Query Service of these processing requirements.

 Figure 31: Query Service communications framework data messages

Time-Series Data Messages
Figure 31 contains a list of the time-series data messages used by the Query Service interface
DpQueryService shown in Figure 30. In Figure 31 messages are categorized by data format, either “raw”
format or tabular format. The top-level request messages used by the interface are QueryDataRequest and
QueryTableRequest, respectively. Note that the QueryDataRequest message contains sub-messages needed
for different query types, it being used for unary, unidirectional streaming, and bidirectional streaming
operations. Both queries contain a time-interval for the request and a list of Process Variable names, either

 62

directly with the QueryTableRequest, or within the QuerySpec enclosed message for the QueryDataRequest.
The QuerySpec message also contains a Boolean flag, if set, requests that time-series data be returned as
SerializedDataColumn message rather than un-serialized DataColumn messages, thus increasing
performance. The QueryTableRequest request message contains an additional table format enumeration
specifying that the table be returned in column-packed format, or row-packed format. It also has the option
of identifying the requested Process Variable values using a PV name regular expression (i.e., rather than
an explicit list of PV names).
The time-series data responses are given by the QueryDataResponse and QueryTableResponse message. The
QueryDataResponse is relatively simple, it contains all requested data in the raw DataBucket message format
native to the MLDP archive structure; thus, this response requires a minimum of processing by the Query
Service. If a bidirectional streaming operation is used, with queryDataBidiStream() and the initial
QueryDataRequest message identifying the requested data, the client must then always reply with subsequent
QueryDataRequest messages with CursorOperation field set to receive the next QueryDataResponse message.
For unidirectional streaming with the queryDataStream() operation the Query Service simply transmits all
requested data back to client until exhausted. The QueryTableResponse contains the enclosed message
TableResult, which is either a column-packed table, message ColumnTable, or a row-packed table, message
RowMapTable. The table format is already presented, but at the cost of added processing by the Query Service
and gRPC-limited table size (it is a single unary response).
With raw data queries using the QueryDataRequest message, the client (e.g., a client API library) must
assemble the raw time-series data into the desired final format (e.g., a data table). Note that each DataBucket
message is essentially a single sampling process along with its timestamps and any associated attributes
and/or metadata. The collection of sampling process values must be collected from each DataBucket
message and aligned according to the timestamps within the buckets. With the streaming operations
queryDataStream() and queryDataBidiStream() there is no guarantee that data is returned in order, or that
sampling process values originate from the same sampling clock. Thus, client reassembly can be complex.
It is important to note that since gRPC data streams are used to transport the raw time-series data with the
queryDataStream() and queryDataBidiStream() operations, the responses are essentially unlimited in size.
Specifically, result sets are not subject to gRPC message size limitations (default is 4 Mbytes). This
contrasts with the queryData() and queryDataTable() unary operation which can return only a single
QueryDataResponse or QueryTableResponse message restricted to the gRPC message size limit. In principle,
a single, streaming time-series data request could return the entire MLDP data archive back to the client.
For additional performance clients can established multiple concurrent data streams to recover a time-series
data request; thus, there data transport performance is essentially determined by the host network capability.
Finally, from Figure 31 note that all Query Service response messages contain either the requested data or
an exception message; the determination being made the field populated within a union. This is the general
mechanism for exception handling within the Data Platform Communication Framework. Clients must
always check for exceptions in their request responses; the details of any request errors are contained in the
exception message.

 63

 Figure 32: Query Service communication framework Data Provider messages

Data Provider Messages
The messages used in the Query Service queryProviders() operation are shown in Figure 32. The operation
is used to determine information about all Data Providers that have contributed time-series data and/or
annotations to the MLDP data archive. In the Data Provider request message QueryProvidersRequest we
again see a similar multi-criteria format as supported by the Ingestion Service ingestion status request
shown in Figure 28. Data Providers can be queried by UID, associated text (i.e., a description), tag values,
and attribute values. Each criterion is aggregated in the request, and the response is a collection of
information on all Data Providers matching the criteria. That is, the request criteria are inclusive (i.e., an
OR operation) and information for any Data Provider matching a criterion is returned.
The response to a queryProviders() operation is the QueryProvidersResponse message shown in Figure 32.
Again, the response is either an exception or a ProvidersResult message, which is simply a composite of
enclosed message type ProviderInfo instances. The ProviderInfo message contains the provider UID,
name, description, and any tags and attributes associated with the provider. Note that the field metadata is
also included; it is of message type ProviderMetadata shown in Figure 33 below and contains the provider’s
associated Process Variables and activity times.

 64

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

 Figure 33: Query Service communication framework metadata messages

Metadata Messages
The messages used for metadata requests are shown in Figure 33, which includes that for both Process
Variable metadata and Data Provider metadata. From the diagram we see that both requests
QueryPvMetadataRequest and QueryProviderMetadataRequest, respectively, are relatively simple. The
former is either a collection of Process Variable names or a regular expression for requested Process
Variable names, and the latter is a single Data Provider UID. Both responses, QueryPvMetadataResponse
and QueryProviderMetadataResponse, respectively, contain the results within a similarly named
MetadataResult message (or an exception). Namespace containment is enforced using enclosed message
type definitions supported by Protocol Buffers as shown in Figure 33. For Process Variable metadata the
MetadataResult message is a collection of PvInfo messages, each of which contains numerous properties
for all Process Variables matching the request. These properties include the PV name, the first time it was
active, the last time it was active, the last source of timestamps (i.e., either clock or explicit list), the number
of data buckets supplied by the PV, and the Data Provider last associated with the PV. For the Data Provider
metadata, the MetadataResult message is a collection of ProviderMetadata messages, of which typically
there is one, that for the single Data Provider identified in the request. The ProviderMetadata message
contains the provider UID, the Process Variable names associated with the Data Provider, the first and last
times the provider was active, and the total number of data buckets supplied by the Data Provider. Note

 65

that only a single Data Provider can be queried at a time whereas multiple Process Variables are supported
in a single metadata request.
Annotation Service API
The Annotation Service allows clients (i.e., Data Consumers) to annotate the data archive with comments,
data associates, post-ingestion calculations, and other artifacts such as metadata. These annotations are
then available to all other Data Platform users to inspect, analyze, and contribute additional annotations to
existing ones. The annotations feature allows Data Consumers to create “value-added” content through
interaction with the data archive, which is then available to all other users. The Annotation Service
functionality was described in Subsection 2.5 complete with the use-case diagram in Figure 17 and
discussions of the data model used by the Annotation Service.
As discussed in Subsection 2.5 the Annotation Service required a significant development effort, beyond
that originally anticipated. The first implementation was part of Data Platform Release 1.3.0 in Aprill 2024,
while the final, fully featured Annotation Service did not appear until Release 1.8.0 in March of 2025. Thus,
the Annotation Service required over a year of development time, considering that the data model design
started early in 2024. The data model, based upon the notation of a dataset composed of constituent data
blocks, was an important innovation facilitating robust operation of the Annotation Service; this model was
presented in Subsection 2.5. As we see in the Annotation Service interface and messages below, dataset
creation and management are crucial to archive annotations. The exporting of datasets is also provided by
the Annotation Service. The Web Application and Demonstration Application both support the
identification, creation, and exporting of datasets, as well as some form of archive annotation.

 Figure 34: Annotation Service communication framework interface

A schematic of the Annotation Service interface contained in annotation.proto, DpAnnotationService, is
shown in Figure 34. Notes that the interface is narrow, containing only five operations; two are for creation
and querying of datasets, two are for creation and querying of annotations, and one is for data exporting.
Thus, there are 3 categories of operations:
 1. Dataset Management – operations saveDataSet() and queryDataSets().
 2. Annotation Management – operations saveAnnotation() and queryAnnotations().
 3. Data Exporting – operation exportData().
Note that all operations within the DpAnnotationService interface are unary gRPC operations, there are no
streaming operations. All interaction with the Annotation Service is strictly single request/single response.

 66

The creation of an archive annotation is a 2-step process:
 1. First, the targeted archive domain (dataset) is identified with operation saveDataSet(), which
 returns a unique dataset identifier in the response (see artifact DataSetId in Figure 35 below).
 2. Second, the annotation itself is created with operation saveAnnotation() using the generated dataset
 identifier.
Note that when creating an annotation with a high-level API library (or with the Web Application) this two-
step process is generally hidden from clients. The saveDataSet() and saveAnnotation() operations are so
named as they also allow modification and updates of existing datasets and annotations, respectively (i.e.,
rather than the previously named createDataSet() and createAnnotation() operations of earlier
Annotation Service interfaces).

 67

 Figure 35: Annotation Service communication framework dataset messages

Dataset Messages
The messages used for Annotation Service dataset management are shown in Figure 35. An archive
annotation applies to any dataset in the query domain and, thus, dataset management is a critical to operation.
The definition of datasets is essential for annotation, and dataset creation has the explicit operation
saveDataSet()within the interface. The messages necessary for dataset creation are shown in the left-hand
side of the diagram, where the dataset itself is represented by message DataSet. The dataset creation request
and response are given by the SaveDataSetRequest and SaveDataSetResponse messages, respectively. As
described in Subsection 2.5, a dataset is a collection of data blocks where each block is composed of a time

 68

interval and a collection of Process Variable names (note that a data block is essentially equivalent to a
time-series data query). A data block is represented by the DataBlock message.
To create an archive annotation, clients must first create the dataset to be annotated; this action requires the
population of a DataSet message. The DataSet message is populated with all DataBlock messages defining
the dataset, along with a dataset UID, dataset name, the client (owner) UID, and an optional description.
The dataset UID is only required if an existing dataset is being modified (e.g., data blocks are being added
or removed), otherwise it is ignored. Once the dataset is defined, it is used to populate a
SaveDataSetRequest message then transmitted to the Annotation Service via the saveDataSet() operation.
The Annotation Service responds with a SavedDataSetReponse message containing the unique identifier
(UID) of the new (or modified) dataset which is subsequently used for all archive annotation creations and
queries. The significance of the dataset UID is specifically highlighted in Figure 35 with the DataSetId
artifact.
Archive datasets are queried using the queryDataSets() operation with a QueryDataSetsRequest message.
As shown in Figure 35 the QueryDataSetsRequest message uses a multi-criteria mechanism as seen before
in the Ingestion Service ingestion status requests and Query Service metadata requests. Datasets can be
requested directly by their UID, indirectly by their owner UID, by any text description or comment, and by
any Process Variable name contained in their defining query domain. These criteria are inclusively
combined (i.e., in an OR fashion). The corresponding response to queryDataSets(), a
QueryDataSetsResponse message, is the collection of all datasets matching any request criterion. Note that
the datasets themselves are returned within a DataSetsResults message, a composite of DataSet messages
matching the request.
There is additional value to the explicit definition and creation of datasets, specifically the support for data
exporting with the exportData() operation. By its nature a dataset defines a region of the query domain
and, thus, is a time-series data request. This property is instrumental in the data export feature whose
required messages are shown in the right-hand side of Figure 35. The desired dataset is identified within
the ExportDataRequest message, along with the export format, and an optional calculation annotation using
the CalculationSpec message defined in common.proto. Note the desired dataset is requested by its UID.
The response to the exportData() operation, the ExportDataResponse message, contains the full path of the
exported file if exported to the local platform (typically to the client temp directory) and/or a URL to the
exported file if the Annotation Service is configured for export to a web server.

 69

 Figure 36: Annotation Service communication framework annotation messages

Annotation Messages
The messages required for annotation management are shown in Figure 36. Shown in the diagram are all
the messages used by the two operations saveAnnotation() and queryAnnotations(), which is a substantive
requirement. Note that the SaveAnnotationRequest message at the top left of the diagram is essentially
identical in field complement to that of the Annotation message at the bottom center. That is, the
SaveAnnotationRequest message contains all the information for creating an archive annotation, of any type.
It contains several required fields including the annotation name, the UID of the annotation creator, and all
the dataset UIDs being annotated. Optional metadata can be included in the request, such as tag values,
attribute pairs, and event metadata. If an existing annotation is being modified its UID is assigned,
otherwise the id field is left null to create a new annotation. The annotation itself is represented by the
optional fields comment, annotationIds, and calculations, representing a comment annotation, an
association annotation, or a calculation annotation, respectively. Thus, any annotation type can be created
by the single SaveAnnotationRequest message.

 70

The response to a saveAnnotation() operation is the SaveAnnotationResponse message. It contains either
an exception if the creation failed, or a SaveAnnotationResult message if the annotation was successfully
created. The contents of the SaveAnnotationResult message is simply the UID of the annotation created or
modified.
Previously each annotation type was represented by a separate message type, which convoluted the already
complex Annotation Service message set. In the new design only calculation annotations have a separate
message type, Calculations. Referring to the bottom right-hand side of Figure 36, the Calculations
message is simply a collection of CalculationDataFrame messages. The latter message has essentially the
same format as an IngestionDataFrame message seen in Figure 27, with the addition of a name field and the
exclusion of serialized data columns support. The format signifies the similarity between ingested time-
series data and calculation annotations, thus, the ability to export them concurrently. It is worth noting that
the Calculations message can be compared to an Excel workbook, where each contained
CalculationDataFrame message represents a named spreadsheet within the workbook.

Multiple criteria  options are available when querying for annotations, as seen in the
QueryAnnotationsRequest message. Archive annotations can be retrieved directly by UID, or indirectly by
owner UID (i.e., the user creating the annotation), enclosed dataset identifier, any associated annotations,
and by any metadata or comment string associated with the annotation. The result of the annotation query,
contained in the QueryAnnotationsResponse message, is the collection of all annotations matching the query
criterion. Again, the annotation is represented by the Annotation message, which is essentially content
equivalent to the SaveAnnotationRequest message.
Ingestionstream Service API
The Ingestionstream Service is part of the Advanced Data Sciences effort of the Phase IIA project. Its
functionality and operation were described in great length within Subsection 2.5. Noted there was the
substantial development effort required for the service and multiple architecture and operational diagrams
were presented, as the Ingestionstream Service was developed in stages. The first Ingestionstream Service
prototype appeared in Data Platform Release 1.7.0; however, the operational version did not appear until
late in the project, Release 1.10.0. Ingestion stream access needed by the Ingestionstream Service required
collaboration with the Ingestion Service, which then required development of the PV subscription service.
Currently the Ingestionstream Service supports Data Events with Process Variable trigger events. As
described below, although seemingly rudimentary this is a significant accomplishment as the framework
for more elaborate Data Events has been established.
During development of the Ingestionstream Service prototype it became apparent that a generalized
algorithm plugin framework was beyond the scope of the Data Platform Communications Framework. The
plugin framework gRPC interface became convoluted and messages were complex and numerous; the low-
level nature of gRPC was simply overwhelmed by the requirements for a generalized plugin framework.
Our current strategy is to move the algorithm plugin framework to the Java API Library (JAL) offering
high-level programming support; however, other options are available, as was discussed in Subsection 2.5
(WASM [53] and Cling [54]). We recognize that this is a significant project deviation, but the Java API
Library is a more natural setting for an algorithm plugin framework. The library contains an application
framework for rapid development of JAL applications, and the plugin framework is a natural extension of
this application framework. The JAL can provide high-level connection to the Ingestionstream Service,
direct support for state machines, generalized calculations on event data, and a well-defined interface for
client algorithms (i.e., “plugins”).
Data Event Monitoring
Our current objective for the Ingestionstream Service is to provide general “Data Event” monitoring
capabilities within a Subscribe/Publish framework. There, clients subscribe to a well-defined Data Event
and receive notifications of the event, potentially along with a collection of processed data defined by a
“Data Event operation”. The current Ingestionstream Service is designed to handle “conditional events.”

 71

This is a basic Data Event use-case but one requiring all the design elements for a general Data Event. The
notion of a conditional event is based upon a comparison of Process Variable values. For example, the
condition trigger could be a threshold event where a selected PV value exceeds a given limit, say L.
Although conceptually simple, there are subtle issues that must be addressed within the framework.
Consider the following situations:
 • The data type of the Process Variable. The Data Platform supports heterogeneous data, and Process
 Variables may have complex data types such as arrays, images, or data structures, as well as non-
 numeric scalar types such as strings and Booleans. Additionally, the Data Platform supports
 timestamps as heterogeneous data values.
 • Concerning the above, we must consider if the Process Variable is comparable to value L in any
 meaningful sense? For example, numeric arrays have various scalar-valued norms which could be
 compared to a scalar valued L. Likewise, an image may have a client-defined, scalar intensity that
 could be compared to a scalar valued L. Or, on the other hand, L may be a numeric array or image
 array which is to be compared on an element-by-element basis.
 • Does the greater than comparison make sense within the above context? For example, when the
 Process Variable is Boolean or string valued how is the > operation interpreted? This point likewise
 applies to other conditionals such as equals and less than.
The examples of the above are edge conditions requiring significant developmental support. In the current
implementation we limited ourselves to scalar-valued Process Variables only. Even then it must still be
determined if the conditional operation is valid. For example, the greater than operation is valid for two
timestamps but not for a timestamp and a double.
Ingestionstream Interface and Messages
A diagram depicting the Ingestionstream Service interface and its required messages is shown in Figure 37.
The top of the diagram contains the Ingestionstream Service interface DpIngestionstreamService contained
in file ingestion_stream.proto. The bottom top of the diagram contains the set of Protocol Buffers
messages necessary to support a basic Data Event as described above. The Ingestionstream interface
contains only a single operation, subscribeDataEvent(). The operation supports a Subscribe/Publish
service where clients subscribe to a Data Event, and the service publishes a stream of Data Event responses.
Explicitly shown in the diagram is the dependency of the Ingestionstream Service to the Ingestion Service
API, specifically, the subscribeData() operation. The dependency is needed to obtain PV values from the
ingestion datastream, that is, those values defined within the Data Event, discussed more below. The
Ingestion Service PV value subscription operation is performed within a Data Event subscription operation
of the Ingestionstream Service.
Like the Ingestion Service subscribeData() operation, the Ingestionstream Service subscribeDataEvent()
operation opens a bidirectional gRPC data stream but accepts no more than two messages from the client.
The client initiates the subscription by sending a NewSubscription message for which it receives an empty
AckResult message if the subscription succeeds, or an ExceptionalResult message if the subscription fails.
If successful, the Ingestionstream service then sends subscription data back to the client until either the
subscription is cancelled, or an error occurs. Clients cancel the subscription by either closing the stream or
sending a CancelSubscription message. If the subscribe/cancel protocol is violated an exception occurs
and the Ingestionstream Service terminates the connection.

 72

 From Figure 37 note that a new Data Event request, defined by the NewSubscription message within the
initiating SubscribeDataEventRequest message, consists of a collection of triggering events, or
PvConditionTrigger messages, and a Data Event processing operation, represented by the
DataEventOperation message. Here, a data event trigger contains the name of the Process Variable
triggering the event (pvName), a trigger conditional (condition), and the comparison DataValue instance
(value). Note that the trigger conditional is of type PvCondition, the enumeration of all the conditional
comparison supported by the Ingestionstream Service. It is important to note that the comparison value

 Figure 37: Ingestionstream Service interface and required message

field value is of message type DataValue. This is, the data type defined within the Data Platform
communications framework that can represent all supported heterogeneous data types. Thus, the
comparison value can be any heterogeneous data type; this design provides the most general applicability
and upgradeability essentially addressing all issues discussed above. However, it also requires significant
development to properly enforce valid comparisons. Thus, the explicit inclusion of the Request Validation
boundary component within Figure 37; the component performs validation of all PvConditionTrigger
instances defined in the Data Event request.
To emphasize the nontrivial value comparison requirements for a PV condition trigger, the boundary
element “Request Validation” is included at the top of Figure 37, required by the Ingestionstream API
DpIngestionstreamService. Before a Data Event request subscription is initiated it must first be validated.
The Request Validation component performs validation of all PvConditionTrigger instances defined in the
Data Event request. First, a live ingestion datastream must be active and the Process Variables contained
in the Data Event must all be available within the ingestion datastream; that is, the Ingestion Service
subscribeData() operation must succeed. Then the trigger condition Process Variable types and

 73

comparison types must be checked for acceptable comparisons. (This process was outlined previously in
Figure 20.) The Ingestionstream Service then has a well-defined mechanism for performing value
comparisons which are then performed, in real time, on the live ingestion stream data.
The other field of the NewSubscription subscription request message, operation, defines the optional Data
Event operation and is of type DataEventOperation. The Data Event operation consists of a data window,
window (of type DataEventWindow), and a collection of targeted Process Variables targetPvs. (No further
processing operations are yet available for the Data Event.) The data window is important as it specifies
either a time window or sample count window for PV value collection, these buffered values are returned
to the client whenever the Data Event occurs. Thus, all PV values for the Data Event require active
buffering to maintain the window. Additionally, if the window specifies future times or future sample
counts the buffering must continue until the window is filled as defined. For example, consider the case of
a triggering event that monitors a PV governing machine protection; the data window might require the
collection of PV values occurring 5,000 milliseconds before the triggering event and 100 milliseconds
afterwards. Conversely, a data window for an experimental measurement Process Variable might have a
window starting 10 microseconds before a shot and 1,000 microseconds afterwards.
As seen in Figure 37 the response to a Data Event is a SubscribeDataEventResponse message. Subscription
data is returned as either an Event message or an EventData message, depending upon whether the optional
DataEventOperation message was included in the Data Event subscription. The Event message contains
the original PvConditionTrigger message identifying the event, along with the Process Variable value that
triggered the event. If a DataEventOperation was included in the Data Event subscription the client receives
an EventData message for each triggered Data Event. This message includes both the Event message
defining the Data Event and all the ingestion stream data requested in the event operation subscription. The
event data is returned in the form of raw DataBucket messages forming the contents of the data window
buffer described above. Recall that the DataBucket message is essentially a container for a sampling
process; it contains the Process Variable values along with its timestamps and any metadata.
Additional processing within a Data Event is possible in future development. Potential operations include
sample compression or reduction, filtering, and “data aggregation” (i.e., statistical reduction) which can be
efficiently implemented. Note that for many data processing operations the results can still be returned
within a DataBucket message.
 2.7. Data Platform Common Core Services Framework
Each of the Core Services within the Data Platform share the same basic design and implementation
framework. The design has proven to be robust, streamlined, and amenable to upgrades. The Common
Core Services Framework is used throughout the Core Services code base and publicly available in the dp-
service repository [15]. This subsection is somewhat technical but is included to demonstrate the novel
techniques originally developed for improved performance in data transport, processing, and storage, but
now demonstrating overall robustness and reliability. There was significant effort within the design phase
of the project (resulting in desired performance gains), and we attempt to highlight its innovation in
application across all Data Platform Core Services. The Data Platform Common Core Services Framework
is a noteworthy accomplishment.

 74

 Figure 38: Common Core Services base implementation, entry points, and gRPC relationship

Figure 38 shows the relationship of the Common Core Services Framework with the Data Platform
Communications Framework, Protocol Buffers, and the gRPC library; it also establishes the notation used
throughout this subsection. Depicted in the diagram is the situation for an arbitrary Data Platform service
which we denote DpService; for example, this could be the Ingestion Service, Query Service, Annotation
Service, etc. Within the Communication Framework box (labelled DP Communications) the file
dpservice.proto defines the interface DpService and its required messages for data transmission. For
example, dpservice.proto would represent ingestion.proto for the Ingestion Service, query.proto for the
Query Service, annotation.proto for the Annotation Service, etc. The interface DpService contains several
operations provided by the service, labelled operation1(), operation2(), …, etc. We carry this notation
throughout the presentation.
The Protocol Buffers compiler, protoc, generates the class DpServiceGrpc from the interface definition
DpService in dpservice.proto. This class contains the enclosed classes DpServiceStub and
DpServiceImplBase. The former class is used for client-side communications and is referred to as the
“communication stub” in Protocol Buffer vernacular. The latter class is an abstract base class used for
service-side communications, our point of interest. It contains abstract methods as “hooks” into the service
operations; child classes implement these abstract methods to receive client requests and provide responses.
The base class perform most interactions with the gRPC library isolating the service from gRPC library
details. Thus, through class derivation the abstract class DpServiceImplBase provides the entry from the

 75

Data Platform Communications Framework into the Common Core Services Framework. This situation is
shown explicitly in Figure 38 with the child class DpServiceImp which derives from DpServiceImplBase.
Seen in Figure 38 is the abstract base class DpGrpcServerBase from which all Common Core Service
Framework applications are derived. It is responsible for starting the service on a gRPC server instance
and performing any cleanup operations upon service termination. In our example case the DpService
application is implemented with the derived class DpServiceGrpcServer; it contains the main() method by
which all Java applications are launched. It is also responsible for managing the Communication
Framework entry point class DpServiceImpl, such as performing any initialization, configuration, exception
handling, and finalization operations.
Any Common Core Services Framework application requires implementation of a “service handler.” The
service handler performs all operations defined in the service interface, that is, the operations operation1(),
operation2(), etc., of Figure 38. As shown in Figure 38, the DpServiceImpl subclass attaches to the service
handler via the interface DpServiceHandlerAPI; the interface exposes methods to perform all DpService
operations, plus initialization and finalization operations. Thus, each Core Service must implement a
service handler with a well-defined service handler interface, in our example case DpServiceHandlerAPI.
Service handlers typically delegate each service operation to specific “service jobs” defined for the
operation, which is covered below. As shown in Figure 38 the DpServiceImpl subclass performs all
DpService operations through this interface.

 Figure 39: Common Core Services Framework service handler and service jobs

Figure 39 shows the architecture and operation of a Common Core Services Framework service handler
implementation, for our example case DpServiceHandler. Recall that in our case, client requests are
intercepted by DpServiceImpl then passed to service handler DpServiceHandler through the interface
DpServiceHandlerAPI, which is the entry point into Figure 39. The framework provides the abstract base
class QueueHandlerBase for all service handler implementations. The base class manages a queue of all

 76

service jobs and maintains a thread pool (i.e., ExecutorService) of queue workers that run the service jobs
as they arrive. It is the responsibility of the service handler class to create the appropriate service job for
the operation requested by the client. Referring to the bottom right-hand side of the diagram, service jobs
all inherit from the common framework base class HandlerJob. Shown in Figure 39 are the example service
job classes DpServiceJob1, DpServiceJob2, DpServiceJob…, etc., performing the service tasks for
operation1(), operation2(), operation…(), respectively. To be recognized by active queue worker
instances each service job class must implement the execute() abstract method which performs its assigned
operation task. Queue workers are provided by the framework as instances of the QueueWorker class, the
number of queue workers for each service being a framework configuration parameter. Note that the setup
is that of a Producer/Consumer design pattern where the service handler produces service jobs, and the
queue workers consume them (i.e., by executing them).

 Figure 40: Common Core Service Framework job results dispatching

 The results of a service job are returned to the client through a dispatcher service as shown in Figure 40.
As seen in the figure each Core Service dispatcher derives from the framework base class Dispatcher, in
our case DpServiceResponseDispatcher. Each service job maintains a reference to the dispatcher where it
returns the results of its operation. The dispatcher, in turn, maintains a reference to the Communications
Framework entry class DpServiceImpl which returns the service operation results back to the client (i.e., as
a response message). It is the responsibility of the dispatcher to convert the job results from its native
format (typically Java objects) to the required format for transmission back to the client (typically Protocol
Buffers messages). For example, a service job could produce a collection of time-series data which the
dispatcher then converts to a collection of Communication Framework DataBucket messages ready for
transmission within the current data stream. Figure 40 depicts the circuit of job initiation through the
DpServiceHandlerAPI interface; it is first converted to a service job by the service handler in Figure 39, the

 77

service job is then executed by a queue worker returning its results to the dispatcher, which then converts
the results to transmission format and passes it to the service class for transport back to the client. The
figure also includes some details regarding Protocol Buffers messages and gRPC data streaming, which is
managed by the service class DpServiceImpl. Service jobs are typically aware of client requests in the form
of Protocol Buffers messages, for example classes Message1, Message2, …, etc. in the diagram. Data streams
are represented by the gRPC StreamObserver interface which has a template parameter bound to the
message type which it is streaming. Thus, when gRPC data streaming is invoked, the service class
DpServiceImpl communicates with clients via StreamObserver interfaces with appropriately bound message
types.
 2.8. Data Platform Administration Library
Throughout the project tools for verification and validation of the Core Services operations and
performance were developed in conjunction with each service. These complementary tools and utilities are
essentially that originally described for the Data Platform Administration Library in the original project
narrative. Thus, many of the library components are now available within the Data Platform distribution,
although no formal Administration Library API has, of yet, been developed.
The Administration API library provides a collection of tools for managing MLDP deployments,
specifically the Data Platform component. Its intent is to provide tools and utilities for the following tasks:
 1. Testing availability and connectivity of all Core Services in all possible configurations.
 2. Performance testing and benchmarking of Core Services.
 a. Ingestion Service verification and performance benchmarking.
 b. Query Service verification and performance benchmarking.
 c. Annotation Service verification and validation.
 3. Managing the MDLP data archive.
 a. Data validation.
 b. Corruption detection.
 c. Data throughput verification and integrity.
 d. Compacting, purging, and/or cold storage of legacy data.
To add some specificity, consider the following situations:
 • At project start, it was crucial to have a performance benchmarking utility for the Ingestion Service
 under the new design and implementation. The performance of the Data Platform was critical to
 project success and the ability to rapidly determine the outcome of the new designs, techniques,
 and technologies was necessary. This was the motivation for the Ingestion Service benchmarking
 utility which now ships with the Data Platform distribution.
 • When developing the new Query Service a focused effort was made to create a verification and
 benchmarking system for the query results. Consequently, a generalized verification and
 benchmarking system was developed for the Query Service.
 • Additionally, within the Query Service development process, it was recognized that a formal
 system for validating full data throughput was needed; specifically, we required a tool for verifying
 that any data stored in the archive via the Ingestion Service is exactly that recovered via the Query
 Service. Thus, a formal tool for data throughput validation was also built.
 • As the project matured it became apparent that a standardized MLDP test archive was invaluable
 in performance and operations testing of the Data Platform Core Services. Thus, a tool was built

 78

 that would populate the MLDP data archive with a standardized set of simulated test data, that is,
 the Data Platform Test Archive.
Although creation of the above tools required significant development effort, the task greatly reduced
overall development efforts. With the Data Platform installation and deployment system in place, whenever
a new release was available, all team members could download it, install it, and immediately begin
evaluation and development. The above tools were available for verification and validation of the new
releases on local platforms. Data integrity tests are available as unit tests within the installation which can
immediately verify coordinated operation of both the Ingestion Service and the Query Service on local
platforms. Additionally, the benchmarking applications are available for comparisons and tuning of local
installations (note that the Common Core Services Framework has configuration parameters that can be
tuned for specific platforms).
During the project duration, multiple upgrades and modifications were made to the Data Platform
Communication Framework (i.e., the Data Platform API). We wish to acknowledge that availability of the
above tools was instrumental in support of this process. The design of the gRPC interfaces and data
messages is critical to the Data Platform operations and client experience, and extensive effort has been
devoted to ensuring the robustness and flexibility of this communication system. The ability to make
changes and/or upgrades then immediately verify proper operations was essential.
Currently the above tools are distributed within the unit testing libraries and as independent applications
(utilities) that ship with the Data Platform installation. The intent is to eventually create a client front-end
application with graphical user interface (GUI) for the entire suite of administration applications and tools,
which we denote the Data Platform Administration API. The format of the Administration API is still
under consideration (e.g., standalone JavaFX application or internet web browser application).
 2.9. Data Platform Demonstration Application
A standalone desktop application demonstrating Data Platform operations and functionality was built and
now included in the Data Platform Release 1.11.0 installation [11] [13] [55]. Since the Data Platform is a
collection of collaborating services, it is difficult to demonstrate its operation in a conventional setting.
This situation, along with the need to disseminate project results for commercialization, was motivation for
the Demonstration Application development. The application runs standalone on a local desktop and can
demonstrate most features of the Data Platform Core Services, including time-series data request, metadata
requests, annotation creation, data events, and data exporting. Most important, it has a graphical user
interface (GUI) capable of advanced request building based upon metadata associated with the data archive.
Finally, the application features a basic data simulator that users can configure to produce a variety of
simulated time-series data complete with metadata from Data Provides, Process Variables, and Data Events.
Detailed instructions for running the Demonstration Application are available at its public repository [55];
here we cover the outstanding features of the application. Note that although the Demonstration
Application code base is in a separate directory, the built application is included in the Data Platform
distribution releases [13]. This is the same situation as for the Data Platform Core Services code base
repository [15].
The Demonstration Application is easily launched on local platforms with the installed script app-run-
desktop-app. The only ecosystem requirement for the Demonstration Application is that a MongoDB
database system is deployed locally with the standard Data Platform configuration (see the Data Platform
online documentation [11]). The application does not require any Core Service availability; all Core
Services are enclosed within the application itself. Additionally, for further convenience a Dockers
container is also available within the Release 1.11.0 distribution, which contains the required MongoDB
database system pre-configured. The Demonstration Application uses a separate archive within the
MongoDB database thus any existing data within the MLDP archive is undisturbed.

 79

 Figure 41: Data Platform Demonstration Application startup page

Figure 41 shows the startup page for the Data Platform Demonstration Application. The Demonstration
Application starts up with a fresh MongoDB archive upon launching, thus, the first task after each startup
is to populate its archive with sample data. The archive can be populated either by importing values from
a pre-existing Excel spreadsheet (e.g., generated by a previous session) or by generating new values using
the simulated Data Provider contained in the application. In the case of an Excel spreadsheet, the columns
represent the Process Variable sampling values. Process Variable names are provided by column headers
while the timestamps are contained within the first 2 columns: the 1st column containing the timestamp
epoch second, and the 2nd column containing the nanosecond offset. The Demonstration Application data
generation page is shown in Figure 42. It is available through the menu item selection Ingest/Generate or
by clicking on the Ingest->Generate link within the startup page.

 80

 Figure 42: Demonstration Application data generation page (top)

Figure 42 shows the top of the data generation page for a simulated Data Provider. The Provider Details
box contains the property fields defining the simulated Data Provider populating the data archive. Fields
marked with an asterisk (*) are required, all others are optional. Note, however, that the optional fields
constitute metadata associated with the Data Provider which can then be used in archive searches and
request building. As seen in Figure 42, the user has supplied both tag values and (key, value) attribute pairs
for the simulated Data Provider (named Eiger_Detector).

 81

 Figure 43: Demonstration Application data generation page (bottom)

The bottom of the data generation page is shown in Figure 43. There we see that two Process Variables
have been assigned to the Data Provider, one PV sampling data at 10 Hz (Trigger) and the other sampling
data at 100 Hz (PhotonDensity). The sampling duration has been set to 5 seconds for both Process Variables.
A Data Event subscription has also been assigned for the latter PV to be triggered whenever its value is
greater than a threshold value. At the bottom right-hand side of the figure the Generate button is visible.
The user presses this button to generate the simulated data whenever the Data Provider is fully configured.
Note that, once generated, the simulated data can be stored locally in an Excel spreadsheet for later import.

 82

 Figure 44: Demonstration Application query building using Data Provider metadata

As an example of query building using metadata archive searches, we consider the case for our example
Data Provider Eiger_Detector. Figure 44 shows the Demonstration Application Data Provider metadata
query page. There we see that the user has identified the target Data Provider using only partial text
(“Beamline 2”) from its description field. The metadata query result contains provider name, description,
metadata, and the Process Variable names associated with the Data Provider. The application automatically
creates a time-series data request containing all the Process Variable names associated with the Data
Provider, as seen in the left-hand side of the figure. Process Variable names can be removed from the auto-
generated query by clicking the trashcan icon. Alternately, by clicking on the Process Variable names in
the metadata query result, a time-series data query can be constructed. The time-series data query is
performed by moving to the Query Editor screen using the Edit Query button in the right-hand side box, or
menu item selection Explore/Data.

 83

 Figure 45: Demonstration Application Data Exporer page post time-series data query

Figure 45 shows the Data Explorer page of the Demonstration Application after performing the above time-
series data request. At the top of the page, we see the actual request, the Process Variable names and time
interval defining the query request; the Submit Query button is used to perform the request. The bottom of
the page shows the results of the time-series data query in both table format and chart format. Within the
top box we also see tabs for building datasets, and annotations assigned to datasets. Recall that a time-
series data query essentially defines a data block. Thus, using the Add to Dataset button in the top box,
the current time-series data query can be used to create a new dataset or added to an existing dataset under
construction. The Demonstration Application has additional pages for archive annotation searches, dataset
search, and Data Event creation and display. It also has provisions for exporting annotation data and
creating calculation annotations associated with datasets.
The Demonstration Application is a powerful tool in the dissemination of project results. Thus, it can
produce scaled demonstrations of most Data Platform functionality as a standalone desktop application
requiring a minimum of local ecosystem support. Moreover, it ships directly within the Data Platform
distribution along with the Core Services and other tools and utilities.
 2.10. Data Platform Distribution System
The Data Platform repository is public domain, open source, and freely available at the main repository site
[11]. The Data Platform has a mature distribution system which is accessible within the Releases section
of the main repository (i.e., see [13]). Consequently, installation, deployment, and configuration of the
Data Platform are straightforward, and the process is fully documented on the main repository site. The
distribution system is a major accomplishment and represents a project milestone. Rapid installation and
deployment are especially warranted considering the standalone nature of the Data Platform.

 84

Data Platform distributions are available as release versions, using semantic versioning in the format ‘M.m.p’
discussed in Subsection 2.2 (i.e., Major ‘M’, minor ‘m’, patch ‘p’). As development continued new features
and upgrades were made available as new Data Platform release versions. Early in the project not all
versions were backward compatible since the service APIs evolved. Once the APIs began to stabilize the
main repository was made public (Release 1.7.0), however, previous versions are still available for
inspection. Detailed release notes are included in the repository with each release version, including any
new features and “breaking changes.” The Breaking Changes section of the Release Notes contains all the
backward compatibility issues with the previous version. Although the APIs are relatively stable, public
access is available under the caveat that APIs are still subject to change in new releases, specifically,
anything under major release version 1. However, as mentioned, comprehensive release notes are included
identifying any backward incompatibility introduced in a new release.
Note that all subsystems of the Data Platform are also open source and publicly available, such as the Core
Services code base [15], the Data Platform Communication Framework [16], and the Demonstration
Application [55]. The repository containing the Data Platform ecosystem support is also publicly available
[56]; this repository contains the scripts, configuration files, and formal documents produced during the
project. There are also links to these repositories within the main repository site. Note that each repository
contains additional documentation on its contents and function within the Data Platform.
Data Platform Main Repository
Described here is the process for installation and deployment of the Data Platform from the main repository
[11], that is, the Releases section [13]. Once deployed, all that is required to launch any Data Platform Core
Service is the execution of a shell script, all of which ship within the distribution. Likewise, termination of
any service is done with a complementary script. Scripts for initializing and starting the MongoDB database
service are also available, as well as utilities for installation and deployment of the Web Application service.
A script is also available for starting the Demonstration Application. The installation also ships with a suite
of utilities for testing, benchmarking, and creating the Data Platform Test Archive. There are additional
scripts for supporting the MongoDB database as Dockers containers.

 85

The Data Platform system installer is available as a zipped archive. The archive is available for download
at the Github repository URL https://github.com/osprey-dcs/data-platform (see ref. [11]) within the
“Releases” section on the main page (see ref [13]). Once installed, Data Platform deployment has also been
greatly simplified; the ecosystem for a local deployment is reduced to Java (version ≥ 21), MongoDB
Community Edition (version ≥ 7), and an environment variable DP_HOME which points to the local installation
location. A screen capture of the Data Platform main repository web page is provided in Figure 46. All
available releases of the Data Platform installer are available for download by following the links in the
“Releases” section of the web page seen in the lower right-hand corner of the figure. Note that at the time
of the writing there were 14 releases in total with Release 1.11.0 being the latest version available.

 Figure 46: Data Platform main repository page

The latest Data Platform distribution web page at the time of this writing is shown in Figure 47. The
distribution web page is accessible by clicking the “Releases” hyperlink shown in the main page of Figure
46 then selecting for the desired release version. Note that Figure 47 contains 2 screenshots: 1) an excerpt
for the top of the web page starting with the release notes, and 2) a screenshot of the page bottom after
scrolling. The bottom section contains the active links for downloading the Data Platform installer archive
and/or source code, specifically, in the “Assets” box. As shown in Figure 47, there are detailed release
notes describing the major changes including new behavior, new additions, and any breaking changes for
the release.
The installer archive is named data-platform-installer-1.x.x.tar.gz, where 1.x.x indicates the release
version (i.e., for Major release versions 1). The distribution products and artifacts retain their names for
each version (e.g., Core Service names), so new versions may appear identical except for API changes,
service additions, and utility additions. Thus, it is recommended that clients install new releases in
directories marked with release version numbers. The installation of the unzipped archive is described
below.

Data Platform Installation
After downloading the desired release installer archive using the above link, the installation and
deployment process for the Data Platform consists of the following steps:

 87

 1. Install Java (version 21 or greater) locally.
 2. Install MongoDB 7.0 or 8.0 (free Community Edition) and create administrative account.
 3. Unzip the Data Platform installation archive into the desired local install location.
 4. Set the DP_HOME environment variable to the local installation directory, say “/install/location”.
 a. Alternatively, a file .dp.env is created in the client startup directory with line
 export DP_HOME=/install/location

 5. Set the DP_JAL_HOME environment variable to the JAL installation directory (i.e., if used).
 a. Alternatively, include export DP_JAL_HOME=/jal_install/location in file .dp.env.
 6. Start the desired Data Platform Core Service using corresponding script (see below).
The process is well documented. There are detailed instructions for each step included within the Data
Platform main repository page of Figure 46. The main page also contains extended documentation
concerning the Data Platform and its operation, developer notes, and a repository of formal documents and
presentations concerning the Data Platform and full MLDP.
The Data Platform Deployment
Figure 48 depicts the host platform deployment configuration once the Data Platform has been installed,
that is, by unzipping the installer archive data-platform-installer-1.x.x.tar.gz within the desired
directory, installing supporting systems (e.g., Java and MongoDB), and setting of environment variables.
(Note that the release version is included in the installer name.) For convenience the installation contains
a package of executable shell scripts within the bin subdirectory. These scripts are used to start and stop
the various services, including the MongoDB database system and the Envoy proxy server (for the Web
Application). Testing utilities are included in the installation, started as scripts, for operation verification
and performance benchmarking the Ingestion Service and Query Service on the local platform. A utility
for populating the Data Platform Test Archive is also included. (Data validation utilities are included in
the services unit tests.)
The Core Services are all contained within the single Java archive (i.e., “jar” file) dp-service.jar. This is
a shaded archive (i.e., “fat jar”) where all service dependencies are included, that is, other than MongoDB.
Each service script locates the desired service application within the jar archive and executes the service
within the Java environment. Note that services have configuration parameters within the file dp.yml which
is accessible by dp-service.jar via the environment variable DP_HOME. Once launched, a service exposes
its designated gRPC interface on the host platform (e.g., DpIngestionServiceAPI, DpQueryServiceAPI,
DpAnnotationServiceAPI, etc.). The interface is available through a host port address assigned in the
configuration file (default values were shown within the figures of Subsection 2.6). Note further that each
service maintains a log file within the log subdirectory. Logs are available for diagnosing service operations
and capturing any exceptional events. Differing logging levels are available as a configuration parameter
within dp.yml; note that excessive logging can be useful in determining proper service operation, however,
it also can create performance degradation.

 88

The environment variable DP_HOME within the host platform must be assigned to the top-level installation
directory of the Data Platform. The environment variable is used by the shell scripts to locate the
installation location on the platform. Assignment can be done either directly with a shell command (e.g.,
export DP_HOME=/installations/data-platform) or via the file .dp.env placed within a platform startup
directory (e.g., a user home directory); the file contains the single line DP_HOME=/install/location executed
within the shell.

 Figure 48: Data Platform installation and deployment

The distribution system has become a powerful tool for installing and deploying the Data Platform product.
As of Release 1.7.0 Osprey DCS opened repository access to the public under the caveat that the API is
still subject to change, although the API is now mature and relatively stable. Nevertheless, comprehensive
release notes are included with each release version in case any “breaking changes” are introduced in new
releases. The installation and deployment system gives customers the ability to easily download, install,
and evaluate the Data Platform. It also allows open-source development within the entire community where
developers can fork repositories adding additional features and capabilities.
 2.11. Web Application
The Web Application provides remote access and interaction with the MLDP data archive [14], specifically
the Remote User actors within Figure 4. It allows remote users general access to the Data Platform archive
using a standard internet web browser and a public URL. The Web Application is an independent system

 89

and can be hosted on an independent Web Server platform as shown in the MLDP deployment within Figure
4. It communicates with the Data Platform using gRPC through a gRPC proxy service described below.
Stated in the Phase II narrative are the following requirements for the Web Application:
1. Data exporting of archived time-series data and metadata: Isolate and export archive data in common
 formats (CSV, Excel, NumPy, etc.). Data sets of interest are identified remotely then locally
 downloaded for analysis and processing.
2. Provide common data science functions: Data visualization in the form of tables and charts. The ability
 to analyze time-series data using standard data science techniques such as averaging, fitting,
 correlations, support vectors, etc. Note that extensive development in this area is unwarranted as
 specialized analysis is available through data exporting, common spreadsheet applications, and
 available data science tools and libraries.
3. Post-ingestion archive annotations (i.e., a viable subset): Features of interest are the ability to annotate
 archived data with user notes and comments, and to provide data associations within the archive.
Thus, the objective of the Web Application is to provide a Graphical User Interface (GUI) to the Data
Platform having maximal functionality while providing intuitive controls and options in support of user
workflow. Thus, a dedicated effort was made on designing the user interface with focus on comprehensive
functionality, and with particular attention to the “look and feel.” Significant effort focused on improved
user workflow, that is, the sequence of operations required for specific tasks. Many intermediate workflow
stages were eliminated or short-cuts provided. For example, users can now perform a time-series data
request then directly create annotations for the result set, rather than being tasked with the creation of data
blocks and datasets prior to annotation creation. Likewise, a time-series data request can be directly
exported without intermediate stages. Moreover, the Web Application supports all data exporting features
of the Data Platform.
The Web Application also supports request building using metadata searches. Process Variable metadata
browsing is available either directly or using “mouse-over” browsing of PV metadata from displayed time-
series data requests; here abridged metadata records are displayed within a “hints” window. The ability to
browse annotations using metadata, that is, by attributes, creator, and regular expressions is also available
in the Web Application. These features were added as they became available within the Annotation Service.
Data Provider metadata browsing has not yet been implemented.
Direct user operations are also available within the Web Application. For example, the creation of datasets
needed for archive annotation can still be performed using a sequence of popup windows for directly
selecting Process Variables and time ranges. Direct Process Variable metadata requests are also available
in a similar fashion.
The notable feature not yet available within the Web Application is visualization, specifically charting; as
of this writing, only tabular displays of time-series data and metadata are available. However, the data
interoperability framework with the Data Platform has been established. Although not completed to
original project specifications, the Web Application represents a project milestone.
Note that the Data Platform Demonstration Application does include visualization. In fact, with a minimum
of modification the standalone desktop application can access remote MLDP archives. Thus, we essentially
have 2 avenues to pursue remote access: 1) threw a Web Browser using the Web Application and 2) via a
standalone desktop application such as the Demonstration application. We are considering both options.
Future development of the Web Application can proceed at any time as it is principally an independent task.

 90

 Figure 49: Web Application user interface screenshots (time-series tables)

The prototype Web Application had an elementary user interface, functional but not complete, nor intuitive.
Thus, a dedicated effort was made on designing the new user interface with focus on comprehensive
