functionality, and with particular attention to the “look and feel.” Several screenshots of the Web
Application GUI are shown in Figure 49. As seen in these examples, the user is presented an intuitive
tabular view of the data archive. The interface allows the user to interact with data directly by highlighting
and selecting various regions of interest for further processing. Users can drill down recursively within
larger regions to isolate data blocks of interest. Multiple data blocks can be concurrently selected to build
datasets, the targets for annotations, exports, and/or further query requests. (The notion of datasets was
covered when discussing the Annotation Service within Subsection 2.5.) Additional properties for selected
data (e.g., metadata) are also available using mouse over windowing and mouse right clicking.

 91

 Figure 50: Web Application user interface screenshots (annotation creation)

Figure 50 includes a few screen shots of the Web Application in the process of creating a user annotation.
The top of the figure includes screen shots where the user first creates a dataset from a time-series data
obtained from a time-series data request. Note that the user can create datasets from a collection of data
blocks taken from the requested time-series data by highlighting the data regions (not shown). In the bottom
of Figure 50 the user has created a comment annotation within the Data Platform archive. In the last screen
shot one can see the Annotation ID, the owner ID, the Data Set ID, and the annotation comment. The ability
to export data from the Data Platform archive is also possible within the Web Application. The workflow
follows a similar pattern as that for annotation where a dataset is first identified (e.g., using the same
controls as that for annotations) then the dataset is exported in the desire format.

 92

 Figure 51: Web Application and gRPC Web framework (taken from ref. [43])

For connection to the Data Platform Communication Framework, the Web Application utilizes the “gRPC-
Web” open-source library [14]. The gRPC-Web framework is a JavaScript client library enabling browser-
based applications to interact with gRPC-based services using an Envoy proxy server [43]. The situation is
illustrated in Figure 51. The necessity for this general setup (i.e., a gRPC proxy server) is due to web
browser applications reliance on the HTTP transport protocol while gRPC is based upon the HTTP2
protocol; consequently, some “translation” is always required. The choice of Web-gRPC/Envoy was made
for its relative convenience, open-source distribution, and mature technology (it is part of the gRPC
framework under continued support).
The communications system for the Web Application has been designed and built, and the Envoy services
are included with the Data Platform distribution. Successful interaction between the Web Application, the
Query Service, and the Annotation Service has been established. As mentioned above, Web Application
development can continue at any time as it is essentially an independent task.
 2.12. Java API Library
The Data Platform Java API Library (JAL) is contained in Github repository https://github.com/osprey-
dcs/dp-jal [31]. The objective of the Java API Library is to provide a high-level, performance-based
framework for client interactions with the Data Platform, thus isolating them from the details of the Data
Platform Communication Framework, Protocol Buffers, and gRPC. At the time of this writing the library
contains Java language APIs for the Ingestion Service and the Query Service, and partial support for the
Annotation Service (Release 1.8.0). The release versioning of the JAL generally follows that for the Data
Platform. Specifically, releases with equal version number are compatible but not all features of the Data
Platform version may be supported. As expected, the support within JAL lags that of the Data Platform as
development there can only proceed when Data Platform features are available. The currently available
version is JAL 1.8.0 while JAL 1.10.0 is due for release later in 2025. JAL version 1.9.0 was skipped since
most Data Platform features new to JAL appear in release 1.10.0. Most of the development described here
is for JAL 1.8.0, which was a major development effort beyond 1.7.0.
From a data science perspective, a Java language API to the Data Platform is not as useful as a Python
language library, since most popular machine learning, artificial intelligence, and general data science
libraries are implemented in Python (e.g., see [3] [4] [5] [6]). There are, however, public-domain, high-
performance Java language ML/AI libraries available (e.g., see [57] [58].) The JAL library is, however, an
important contribution to the project and, from a development perspective, a necessary one. The JAL
library was used extensively in the design of Data Platform APIs, operations testing, and performance
evaluations. The JAL library is covered in detail to emphasize the accomplishment, and to document the
process necessary to achieve high-performance interaction with the Data Platform in other languages.

 93

The Java language was purposefully selected as the first high-level Data Platform API support. The primary
reason is that the Data Platform itself is implemented in the Java language, this allowed for direct
evaluations of design and performance (i.e., the two systems should compare similarly in operation and
performance). Second, the repository containing the Data Platform Communication Framework is
configured to build and locally install in the Java language (see ref. [16]); thus, it was a straightforward task
to update to the new gRPC interfaces as new version releases were available. Additionally, software
components could be shared between systems. Finally, it should be noted that development of the JAL
library was a significant effort, one that would be made more difficult in languages other than that of the
Data Platform implementation language. Additionally, the experience and knowledge gained in the JAL
development can now be used to build high-level API libraries in other languages.
An overview of the Java API Library (JAL) is
shown in Figure 52. We see there that the JAL is
composed of 4 API subsystems, each supporting a
Data Platform Core Services, plus an Applications
Framework for rapid JAL application development,
a JAL Tools Suite, and a JAL Applications Suite for
Data Platform testing. Within Release 1.8.0 the
Annotation API only partially supports the Data
Platform Annotation Service and the
Ingestionstream API has yet be implemented.
Support for these subsystems is being addressed in
JAL Release 1.10.0. Finally, the JAL relies on the
file jal-config.yml to provide default values for
configuration parameters, of which there are many.
Specifically, each API subsystem is typically
supplied with numerous configurations parameter
allowing for optional performance on client
platforms. The proliferation of allowable Figure 52: Java API Library component structure
configurations was also a development choice used
to determine maximum performance and capabilities of the Data Platform. Except for the Ingestionstream
API which is undeveloped, we cover each of these subsystems below.
Ingestion Service API
The JAL Ingestion API is a sophisticated system that exploits most all Data Platform Ingestion Service
capabilities. For example, the Ingestion API implements significant concurrent processing through multi-
threading and the use of multiple, concurrent gRPC ingestion datastreams supported by the Ingestion
Service. With JAL Release 1.8.0 the Data Platform ingestion performance is confirmed at 500 Mbps using
the performance evaluation applications included in the library. With the client serialized data column
option within Data Platform Release 1.9.0 we expect data rates of 1 GBps although this has yet to be directly
verified. We are currently augmenting the JAL Ingestion API and Application Suite to include serialized
data column ingestion testing which will appear in JAL Release 1.10.0.

 94

The basic JAL Ingestion API seen by clients is depicted in Figure 53, along with its relationship to the Data
Platform Communications Framework which it conceals. There we see that two interfaces are available,
IIngestionService for unary data ingestion, and IIngestionStream for streaming data ingestion. Both
interfaces are available from the “connection factory” utility class DpIngestionApiFactory. The connection
factory supplies clients an implementation for each interface through its various connect(…) static methods.

 Figure 53: Java Language Library Ingestion API

(To save space method names are abbreviated in the diagram; methods connectService(…) and
connectStream(…) are used to distinguish between interfaces IIngestionService and IIngestionStream,
respectively.) The arguments for the connect(…) methods specify various connection options available to
client, such as a specific service address, use of Transport Layer Security (TLS) protocol, compression, etc.

 95

Using a connect() method with no argument yields the default connection as specified in the configuration
file jal-config.yml. Note that both interfaces require Data Provider registration before ingestion can begin
(for streaming ingestion opening the stream and registration are equivalent). Finally, any ingestion interface
instance should be shut down when no longer needed (i.e., with the shutdown() method). This operation
releases all resources used by the interface, preserving overall performance. We note that the JAL Query
Service API uses the same basic format for connection factories and interfaces.
All interaction with the Data Platform Ingestion Service is done through interfaces IIngestionService and
IIngestionStream. As seem in Figure 53 these interfaces are narrow exposing a minimum of operations.
The IIngestionService interface supports unary ingestion where single data frames are transmitted
synchronously, whereas with streaming ingestion supported by the IIngestionStream interface an open
datastream is maintained with the Ingestion Service and clients transmit multiple data frames
asynchronously until the stream is closed. The separate implementations for unary and streaming
operations was a practical decision; the ingestion procedure for each case was sufficiently different to
warrant separate interfaces emphasizing the differing protocols. The result of any ingestion operation is
returned as an IngestionResult record instance (for both unary and streaming ingestion). The
IngestionResult record contains a list of transmitted request IDs, a list of ingestion requests rejected by the
Ingestion Service (by request ID), a list of any exceptions occurring during ingestion, and other parameters
not shown.
Note that all data ingestion is performed using instances of class IngestionFrame, the JAL unit of data
ingestion. The IngestionFrame class is essentially an abstraction and generalization of the
IngestionDataFrame Protocol Buffers message of the Data Platform Communication Framework used in
ingestion requests (shown in Figure 27 and as indicated in Figure 53). An IngestionFrame instance is an
active aggregation of IDataColumn interfaces containing sampling process values, along with the process
timestamps. The timestamps can be supplied either explicitly as a list of Java Instant objects or implicitly
as a UniformSamplingClock instance (implying uniform sampling). There are multiple techniques for
creating data columns, thus the generic treatment with interface IDataColumn. Note that data columns must
supply a name, a data type, methods for accessing values, and a method for computing its memory allocation.
Column memory allocation is used for “frame decomposition” described below. (Frame decomposition is
an option available for ingestion frames too large to transmit due to gRPC message size limitations.) The
IngestionFrame class also provides methods for computing memory allocations for the same purpose.

The IIngestionService interface provides straightforward unary RPC communications with the Ingestion
Service where a single IngestionFrame instance is transmitted and a single IngestionResult is returned.
This is a synchronous, blocking operation which does not return until all data is transmitted to the Ingestion
Service. It is the safest and most reliable ingestion operation, although it is also the slowest. This interface
is appropriate whenever performance requirements are not a limiting issue. Note that to maintain data
provenance clients must first register with the Ingestion Service using the registerProvider() operation
before invoking the ingest() method, otherwise an exception is thrown. Thus, the process for ingestion
consists of the following steps:
 1. Ingestion Service Registration – invoke the registerProvider() operation supplying provider
 name and any optional metadata with a ProviderRegistrar record.
 2. Data Ingestion – invoke the ingest() method for each independent data frame IngestionFrame
 produced by the provider.
 3. Interface Shutdown – invoke the shutdown() method to close the connect with the Ingestion Service
 and release all gRPC resources.
The IIngestionStream interface provides gRPC data streaming of ingestion data with the Ingestion Service,
concealing the details of the streaming process. Clients are required to first open the data stream with the
openStream() operation, which also registers the Data Provider with the Ingestion Service. Henceforth,

 96

clients can supply an arbitrary number of ingestion frame either with a single call to the ingest() operation,
or with unlimited multiple invocations. The Ingestion API can be configured to enforce a finite capacity
ingestion buffer where ingestion data is queued for transmission (otherwise the ingestion buffer has infinite
capacity). If the buffer reaches capacity (i.e., due to a backlog by the Ingestion Service) the ingest()
method will block thus throttling any further ingestion by the interface. In this manner Data Providers can
experience “back pressure” from the Ingestion Service whenever attempting to supply data beyond its
ingestion limits. Note that there is an awaitQueueReady() operation available to Data Providers which
allows them to block until the ingestion buffer is available for additional data. This operation allows clients
to monitor the ingestion process and perform their own performance tuning in real time. Once all ingestion
is completed the Data Provider must close the data stream with the closeStream() operation where it
receives the results of all streaming operations in an IngestionResult record. Finally, the interface must
also shutdown with the shutdown() operation to close the Ingestion Service connection and release all gRPC
resources. Thus, streaming with the IIngestionStream consists of the following steps:
 1. Open Datastream – invoke the openStream() method simultaneously registering the Data Provider
 with the Ingestion Service for all incoming data.
 2. Data Ingestion – invoke the ingest() operation as often as necessary to asynchronously transmit
 all data frames supplied by the provider.
 3. Optionally Block – invoke the awaitQueryReady() operation to block after large data transmission
 to coordinate with the Ingestion Service.
 4. Close Datastream – invoke the closeStream() method to close the ingestion datastream once data
 transmission is complete.
 5. Interface Shutdown – invoke the shutdown() method to close the connect with the Ingestion Service
 and release all gRPC resources.

 97

 Figure 54: Java Library Ingestion API internal architecture

Figure 54 shows the basic internal architecture of the JAL Ingestion API. There we see 2 implementation
classes:
 1) DpIngestionServiceImpl exposing the IIngestionService unary interface, and
 2) DpIngestionStreamImpl exposing the IIngestionStream streaming interface.
We see that DpIngestionService is relatively simple; it maintains two ingestion frame processing class
instances, FrameDecomposer and FrameConverter (discussed below), and a single gRPC Channel instance
which is created and provided by the connection factory DpIngestionApiFactory. The
DpIngestionServiceImpl class communicates directly with the Data Platform Ingestion Service via the
gRPC channel through the IngestionAPI gRPC interface. Unary gRPC operations are relatively
straightforward thus direct communication here is efficient.
Regarding the ingestion frame processing classes, the FrameDecomposer class checks memory allocation of
each ingestion frame for compliance with the gRPC message size limit, decomposing it into smaller
composite frames meeting this limit if the condition is violated. The class has been designed to optimize
this process, however, if it is known a priori that all ingestion frames meet gRPC size limits, frame
decomposition is a configuration parameter that can be switched off providing additional performance. The
FrameConverter class converts IngestionFrame instances of the Java API Library into IngestDataRequest
messages required by the Data Platform Communications Framework for data transmission. It creates a
new IngestDataRequest message for each ingestion frame, extracting the appropriate data, metadata, and
any parameters then populates the message.

 98

From Figure 54 we see that streaming interface implementation class, DpIngestionStreamImpl, is
significantly more complex. It relies primarily on 2 independent component systems:
 1) IngestionFrameProcessor – converting IngestionFrame instances into IngestDataRequest
 messages, decomposing them if necessary, and
 2) IngestionChannel – transmitting IngestDataRequest messages to the Ingestion Service using
 multiple, concurrent gRPC datastreams.
These components produced significant performance gains (> 2x) over the previous implementations. Note
that the two components are connected through an IngestionDataBuffer instance which exposes the
interfaces IMessageConsumer and IMessageSupplier. This class acts a buffer for IngestDataRequest
messages, isolating the IngestionChannel from any ingestion spikes from the Data Provider. The
IngestionDataBuffer class also has a finite-capacity option where it blocks whenever capacity is reached;
it also provides notification of this condition so that DpIngestionStreamImpl can refuse any further ingestion
attempts while at capacity. The IIngestionStream interface awaitQueueReady() operation relies on this
buffer. The IMessageConsumer and IMessageSupplier interfaces are significant as it is possible to swap
ingestion buffer implementations. In the Release 1.8.0 implementation the buffer capacity is determined
by total memory allocation, rather than number of messages contained; this situation is more reflective of
operating conditions as messages sizes can vary.
Upon opening a datastream (i.e., with the openStream() interface operation) class DpIngestionStreamImpl
activates both components then submits IngestionFrame instances to the IngestionFrameProcessor
component as they arrive from Data Producers (i.e., via the interface ingest() operation). Once started the
process is essentially autonomous. When a Data Producer closes the stream (i.e., with the closeStream()
interface operation) class DpIngestionStreamImpl shuts down both components, collects the streaming
results from the IngestionChannel component, coalesces them into an IngestionResult record, then return
it to the client.
Note from Figure 54 that the IngestionChannel component can manage multiple, concurrent gRPC data
streams, abstracted by the class IngestionStream. It maintains a single gRPC Channel instance which it
receives from the connection factory DpIngestionServiceApiFactory. A gRPC channel can support
multiple datastreams thus no additional gRPC resources are required. The class IngestionStream also
allows for either unidirectional (forward) data streaming or bidirectional data streaming as both possibilities
are available from the Ingestion Service. The stream type is determined at creation, and the details are
handled internally by the class, which performs all data transmission to the Data Platform Ingestion Service
through the gRPC interface IngestionAPI. Finally, the IngestionFrameProcessor component can maintain
multiple instances of processing classes FrameDecomposer and FrameConverter. These instances can be
spawned within thread pools and execute concurrently; this situation yielded the best performance.
Query Service API
The Java API Library Query Service API offers clients straightforward Java interactions with the Data
Platform Query Service. As with the JAL Ingestion API, a connection factory is used to supply clients with
a Query Service interface using various connection options. The interface is wider here since both time-
series requests and metadata requests are supported. Internally, the Query API system is significantly more
complicated than the JAL Ingestion API. This condition is due to the necessary processing within the Query
API when raw, time-series data requests are performed. However, with the client-side processing of raw,
time-series data, overall data rates for large query requests have been confirmed at over 200 MBps for
Release 1.8.0, before availability of the Data Platform serialized data column option. We expect data rates
approaching 500 MBps in Release 1.10.0 when using client serialized data columns.
The JAL Query API is designed for maximum performance in time-series data recovery and assembly. It
can utilize multiple gRPC data streams for time-series data request recovery and has various configurations
for post processing and assembly of the requested data. Together, the Query Service and the JAL Query

 99

API are designed for maximum overall performance by distributing time-series data request processing
between the client and server; requested time-series data is supplied in raw format, then reconstructed (e.g.,
tabulated) on the client platform. Thus, the task of aligning, assembling, and tabulating request data is
offloaded from the Query Service.
Once all data has been recovered and processed from a time-series data request, it is packaged into a data
table containing the heterogeneous data. The Query API interface IQueryService returns these tables as the
interface IDataTable. Multiple implementations of IDataTable are available (e.g., static and dynamic
tables), which are chosen by the library according to recovery conditions. Use of the IDataTable interface
also avoids commitment to a single implementation. Static and dynamic data tables are described in further
detail below.
Metadata requests are returned as Java Record lists, containing records for all metadata matching the request.
Metadata requests are supported by interface operations for specific metadata types. There are separate
record types for each metadata type. For example, ProviderMetaRecord records contain Data Provider
metadata while PvMetaRecord records contain Process Variable metadata.
We cover the JAL Query API in substantial detail, specifically, for the time-series data request processing.
Client-side processing of raw, time-series data is a complex process requiring data recovery, correlation,
assembly, and table creation. Developing a performance-based implementation for the process was a
significant development effort. We wish to document the necessary steps and the solutions for future
development of other high-level language APIs for the Data Platform (e.g., a Python library). Additionally,
being an open-source project, careful documentation of implementation details aids in community
development.
Most client resources for the Query API are in the com.ospreydcs.dp.api.query package. Default
configuration parameters are in the library configuration file jal-config.yml (under the query: section)
within the project config directory. Resources required by the library for query operations and processing
are contained in subpackages. Located in the main package is a single interface to the Query Service,
IQueryService. It is available through the connection factory QueryApiFactory in the main package. The
interface provides operations for both time-series data requests and metadata requests.
Basic Architecture
The general architecture of the JAL Query API is shown in Figure 55. There we see that a single interface,
IQueryService, is available for client interaction. (Not shown in the diagram is the connection factory
QueryServiceApiFactory utility class supplying these interfaces.) The class DpQueryServiceImpl provides
the latest implementation for the interface (there are earlier versions). The implementation class
DpQueryServiceImpl maintains a QueryRequestProcesssor component instance for all raw, time-series
request recovery and correlation processing. We see also that a request decomposer and a data correlator
are parts of the QueryRequestProcessor component. Once the raw request data is available from the
processor as a sorted set of correlated query data blocks, RawCorrelatedData instances, the implementation
class then assembles the resulting IDataTable instance from the fully processed dataset and returns it to the
client through the interface. As seen in the diagram, this is a two-step process where an intermediate object,
a SampledAggregate instance, is first created from the correlated blocks. This class supplies timestamps and
fills in any data source sample values that may not be a part of all data sources within the full result set (i.e.,
as null values). The correlation process is significantly more complex than the basic setup shown in Figure
55. Briefly, after raw request data is correlated into RawCorrelatedData blocks it is then decomposed into
“super domains” explained below (i.e., if necessary). Then the super domain data is paged into
SampledBlock instances which create JAL formatted request data upon demand. The sampled blocks are
aggregated into SampledAggregate which perform the actual table creation. We expand upon the process in
further detail below.

 100

 Figure 55: JAL Query API basic architecture diagram

 Shown in Figure 55 are two implementations for the IDataTable interface which contain requested time-
series data. The first is a static data table, StaticDataTable, where are all timestamps and data values are
created upon construction and available for immediate query. This table can be resource intensive for large
result sets but is appropriate for smaller requests. The second is a dynamic data table,
SampledAggregateTable, where timestamps and data values are created upon demand (i.e., if necessary).
This implementation is equivalent to a “sparse matrix” implementation common in numerical libraries.
Note that the general architecture of the Query API is somewhat different than that for the JAL Ingestion
API (compare with Figure 54). The Ingestion API does not employ a separate “ingestion request processor”
component. Instead, most of the high-level ingestion operations are performed by subcomponents under
direct management of the Ingestion API interfaces. The ingestion situation is more conducive to this
implementation because ingestion is essentially a one-way process, data flows from the client to the Data
Platform Ingestion Service. For time-series query operations, data must be processed both at the initial
location (request decomposition) and at the terminal end of data flow (request data assembly). In the query

 101

operations, The IQueryService interface implementation performs initial processing of the request itself,
performs the request, then manages the processing of request results. Additionally, any request filtering
operations must have access to the original data request, the DpDataRequest instance discussed below, to
obtain the filtering parameters.
Data Requests
The JAL Query API provides a “request builder” class, DpDataRequest, for creating time-series data
requests; it is available directly in the main package and indirectly through the IQueryService interface.
The intent of DpDataRequest is to isolate clients from the QueryDataRequest Protocol Buffers message
required by the Data Platform Communications Query API for such requests. The class contains various
“select” methods for choosing the Process Variables within the request and “range” method for specifying
the time range of the query. There are also various “filter” methods for filtering the query result sets
according to PV and metadata properties. Once the client has formulated the desired request using the
above methods, the DpDataRequest instance is offered to the IQueryService interface for time-series data
request recovery.
Direct metadata requests are created using the DpMetadataRequest class, which is also a request builder.
Again, “select” methods are offered for Process Variable and Data Provider names. There are “filter”
methods for identifying metadata tags and attributes.
Time-Series Request Decomposition
The ability to utilize multiple gRPC data stream for request recovery is facilitated by a request
decomposition mechanism within the JAL Query API. Specifically, the library contains a class
DataRequestDecomposer which creates a collection of composite time-series data requests from a single,
larger request. Request decomposition is typically performed according to a preferred decomposition
strategy specified in the library configuration file; however, clients can specify their own decomposition
strategy. Data requests can be decomposed by data source (“horizontally”), time range (“vertically”), both
data source and time range (“gridded”), or not at all. Once decomposed, each composite request can be
recovered using a separate gRPC datastream. These strategies are enumerated in the enumeration class
RequestDecompType.

gRPC Data Streaming
Within the Java API Library a time-series data request can be recovered using multiple gRPC datastreams,
where a collection of independent gRPC streams is managed by the QueryChannel component class (so
named since all streams share the same gRPC Channel object). This multi-streaming data transport
mechanism is based upon thread pooling where each independent thread task maintains a separate gRPC
datastream for request data recovery. Each thread task operates concurrently accepting QueryData messages
from the Query Service then queuing them in a common message buffer for post processing. (Note that
from Figure 31 QueryData messages contain raw data in “DataBucket” format native to the MongoDB
database.) When the Query Service signals that a datastream is complete, the stream thread terminates itself
while notifying the QueryChannel owner.
Additionally, there are 2 types of gRPC data streams available for recovering raw, time-series data from
the Query Service: 1) a unidirectional (backward) data stream from the Query Service to the Java API
Library and 2) a bidirectional data stream between the Query Service and the Java API Library. In the first
case the client JAL initiates the data stream after which request data is continuously streamed back until all
data is sent, upon which the Query Service terminates the stream (with a “half-closed” event). In the latter
case the client JAL again initiates the datastream, and the Query Service sends single data messages until
receiving an acknowledgment message from the client between transmissions. The Java API Library can
utilize either stream type in any streaming operation (including multiple streams). The default choice is set
in the JAL configuration file.

 102

Data Correlation
Support for raw, time-series data containing either uniform sampling clocks or explicit timestamp lists
necessitated a significant development effort within the JAL Query Service API. This is due to the nature
of time-series data recovery from the Data Platform Query Service. To expedite performance time-series
data request processing is distributed between the Data Platform Query Service and its clients.
Consequently, when streaming time-series data from the Query Service to a client it is recovered as raw
“data buckets,” which is essentially the raw format within the MongoDB archive. These buckets contain
data values for a sampling process, and its timestamps, for a finite time interval. However, the buckets can
arrive at the client in any order, not necessarily the ingestion order. Moreover, the timestamps can appear
implicitly as sampling clock parameters, or explicitly as timestamp lists. Thus, they must be aligned, or
correlated, before their data can be tabulated. This alignment process is the responsibility of the client JAL
Query Service API, thus reducing the processing requirements of the Data Platform Query Service (which
may be simultaneously servicing multiple clients).
The Query Service supplies request data in raw format, that of the “data bucket” native to the MongoDB
storage archive. The Java API Library then aligns, or “correlates”, the raw data on the client platform,
eventually assembling it into tabular format. A RawDataCorrelator object is used to correlate the raw
request data, that is, aligning all the recovered sampled data in time. This object polls the common message
recovery buffer (described above) for available response data and aligns it according to the explicit
timestamps or sampling clock provided in the data bucket. This is a dynamic process where correlated data
blocks, RawCorrelatedData objects, are created according to any new time ranges encountered. Specifically,
Process Variable samples are added to existing blocks with common timestamps, or a new block is created
whenever new timestamps are encountered. This process continues indefinitely, and the set of correlated
blocks is available at any time. Thus, it is imperative that any RawDataCorrelator object be reset before
processing any new data request (i.e., using the reset() method). Note from Figure 55 that the raw data
alignment and RawCorrelatedData block creation is multi-threaded, done by spawning RawDataInsertTask
instances which are independent thread tasks.
Recall from Figure 30 that the Query Service can supply time-series data in tabular format, however, this
request is available only as a unary RPC operation. Thus, the query result set is size limited to the current
maximum gRPC message size limit. Moreover, this is an expensive operation appropriating significant
Query Service resources and should be avoided whenever possible. It is available primarily for the Web
Application where raw data assembling is impractical.

 103

 Figure 56: JAL Query API raw, time-series data correlation

 The implementation of the JAL Query API data correlation component RawDataCorrelator is depicted in
Figure 56. The entry point for requested time-series data is the QueryMessageBuffer class at the top left of
the diagram. This is an active buffer that accepts incoming data as QueryDataResponse.QueryData Protocol
Buffers messages then offers them as they become available. As indicated in the diagram, this is the
common collection point for all concurrent gRPC datastreams employed to recover the request data. (Recall
that the JAL Query API supports the use of multiple, concurrent gRPC data streams, which greatly improves
data recovery performance.) From the diagram we see that a separate process, MessageTransferTask, moves
the QueryData messages from the buffer to the processing class RawDataCorrelator. An independent
message transfer thread is needed because both the QueryDataBuffer and the RawDataCorrelator objects are
active classes that block when in intermediate states; the MessageTransferTask thread coordinates the
message transfers.
The function of the RawDataCorrelator class is to align the raw data within the incoming QueryData
messages. The incoming QueryData messages are an aggregation of raw “data buckets” (the MongoDB
database format) containing a PV sample vector and its timestamps. The alignment is done by identifying
common timestamps within the incoming data buckets. The RawDataCorrelator processes the incoming
messages into sorted sets of RawCorrelatedData instances, ordered by starting timestamp (see below). As

 104

inferred from Figure 56, internally the RawDataCorrelator maintains a pool of independent
RawDataInsertTask threads that perform the alignment, thus utilizing concurrency and improving
performance.
A processed RawCorrelatedData instance contains all the PV samples within the recovered data that share
a timestamp collection. When a QueryData message arrives, the RawDataCorrelator object inspects all
current RawCorrelatedData instances for an equivalent timestamp collection; if found the data is assigned
to that instance, if not another instance is created and added to the managed collection. The recovered data
within a RawCorrelatedData instance is “raw” in the sense that it is still in the form of Protocol Buffers
messages used by the Data Platform Communications Framework. The Process Variable samples are still
contained in DataColumn messages used by the framework and must be deserialized and converted into Java
type in the downstream processing. The timestamps shared by all PV data columns within a
RawCorrelatedData instance are assigned either a single SampleClock message or single TimestampList
message.
Within the Data Platform archive, timestamps for time-series data can be specified using either a uniform
sampling clock or an explicit list of timestamp instances, that is, a timestamp list. Support for timestamp
lists was added to the Data Platform to handle spurious data, and irregular or intermittent sampling. Thus,
data buckets can contain timestamps in either form. Note that sampling clocks require only a period, start
time, and duration whereas timestamp lists contain the full, ordered vector of timestamp instances, they are
significantly more expensive to process and archive but nonetheless available if required. Specifically,
when using a timestamp list the timestamp alignment process compares all timestamps instances, whereas
a sample clock requires only the comparison of clock parameters.
The ability to support both uniform sampling clocks and timestamp lists in raw data correlated was
implemented using inheritance. Figure 56 depicts the inheritance structure where we see that
RawCorrelatedData is an abstract base class from which 2 subclasses are derived: 1) RawClockedData
supporting uniform sampling clocks, and 2) RawTmsListData supporting timestamp lists. This design allows
the processed data to be treated in a consistent manner while supporting the alignment of both timestamp
cases. Specifically, the RawDataInsertTask class recognizes both subclasses while handling either
timestamp case during alignment.

 105

Time-Domain Collisions
The JAL Query API supports the management of “time-domain collisions” in requested time-series data.
A time-domain collision occurs when multiple Process Variables are sampled during the same time interval,
but with differing timestamps. For example, a client could request time-series data for Process Variables
that were sampled with clocks running at different sampling rates during the same time interval. The above
alignment process would then produce a consistent set of RawCorrelatedData instances, but some instances
would contain overlapping timestamp intervals (i.e., intersections) with differing timestamps, and thus
timestamp ordering would be inconsistent. We refer to this situation as a time-domain collision.

 Figure 57: JAL Query API time-domain collision processing

The solution realized by the JAL Query API is depicted in Figure 57. There an intermediate processing
step is added to the request recovery pipeline, the creation of “super domains.” A super domain is more
accurately described as a “super time-domain” for a raw, correlated data set. Within the super domain
processing any collection of RawCorrelatedData instances having intersecting timestamp domains is
identified then aggregated into a single RawSuperDomData instance. Thus, all the timestamps within a
RawSuperDomData instance are contained within an extended time interval defined as the super domain. The

 106

timestamps within a RawSuperDomData instance are not guaranteed to align, but all are contained within a
well-defined time interval. Clearly a RawSuperDomData instance requires additional processing beyond that
of a RawCorrelatedData instance. Explicitly noted in Figure 57 is that RawSuperDomData does not inherit
from RawCorrelatedData, rather, it is an aggregation of RawSuperDomData instances.
From Figure 57 we see that super domain processing and creation is performed by class
SuperDomainProcessor. This class accepts a sorted set of RawCorrelatedData instances, then identifies and
creates a list of all RawSuperDomData instances contained in the original set (there may be none). The
remainder, that is all RawCorrelatedData not within any super domain, is then also available. The processing
result is a list of RawSuperDomData instances and a list of RawCorrelatedData instances, both ordered by
initial timestamp, where all elements within both lists have disjoint time domains. These collections of raw
time-series data are then presented to the next stage in the time-series data recovery pipeline.
Data Table Creation
The final step in the assembly of a time-series data request is data table creation. Specifically, request data
is tabulated into multiple columns each containing the Process Variable samples and where each row
corresponds to a timestamp. Each column contains the sample data for a single Process variable; each row
contains all the Process Variable samples for a single timestamp. All the timestamps are also available as
a single, unique table column. Note that if time-domain collisions are present in the recovered data then
not all row entries are populated for any given Process Variable (i.e., they contain null values). Note also
that the table may contain heterogeneous data and non-numeric data, however, all the data within a single
data column does have the same data type. The data type for a table column is available from the
IDataColumn interface accessible via the IDataTable interface.

Data table creation within the JAL Query API is performed using another intermediate step in the time-
series data recovery pipeline, the creation of a SampledAggregate instance. This process is depicted in the
diagram of Figure 58. A SampledAggregate instance contains all the recovered data of a time-series data
request in the form of an ordered collection of SampledBlock instances, discussed below. The
SampledAggregate can produce 2 types of data table implementations, both exposing the IDataTable
interface passed to clients: 1) a static data table StaticDataTable where all request data is created at
construction, and 2) a dynamic data table SampledAggregateTable where request data is recovered upon
demand. A static data table has faster data access times but at the cost of added heap storage, where a
dynamic data table has slower access times but reduced heap storage. Static data tables are appropriate for
smaller request sizes and dynamic data tables are needed for extreme request sizes. Dynamic data tables
may be thought of as a “sparse matrix” implementation for data tables. The JAL Query API determines
which table type is appropriate according to the request size using configuration parameters in JAL
configuration file.

 107

 Figure 58: JAL Query Service API time-series data table creation

The SampledBlock class contains JAL formatted data as extracted from a collection of raw, correlated, time-
series data. It represents a finite-time collection of correlated Process Variable samples for a given interval
of common timestamps. Accordingly, from Figure 58 we see that a SampledBlock instance can be created
from either a RawCorrelatedData instance or a RawSuperDomData instance (i.e., using the static from()
methods). This action is facilitated with inheritance where three subclasses are used, SampledBlockClocked,
SampledBlockTmsList, and SampledBlockSuperDom. (The SampledBlock class is abstract and cannot be

 108

instantiated.) Each subclass is required to produce its corresponding collection of timestamps and sample
values, specifically with abstract methods createTimestamps() and createTimeSeries(), respectively. For
static data table creation these methods are called upon construction whereas with dynamic data tables (i.e.,
SampledAggregateTable) these methods are called on demand (i.e., when the specific table data is accessed).
Note that the SampledBlock class exposes the IDataTable interface, thus, each SampledBlock instance is
essentially a table page within a larger aggregate table. This is an important aspect of dynamic data table
implementation. The internal access of the (dynamic) SampledAggregateTable class functions as a paged
table where lookups are done according to the page(s) containing accessed data. The timestamps and
sample values within the page are then created on demand and stored for any future access, as described
above.
Use Case Extensions
As with super domains, timestamp lists are use case extensions that require additional processing and
resources if utilized. Specifically, the alignment of raw data by timestamp list requires comparison of all
timestamps of each data bucket. It is “best practices” policy to avoid the use of timestamp lists whenever
possible, but they are available whenever needed. Likewise, processing of time-domain collisions is
available, but can be switched off in the JAL configuration file.
Consider the situation for timestamp lists. The Data Platform Test Archive includes a series of Process
Variables with timestamp lists. There is also a JAL tool in the Application Suite that can supplement the
test archive with timestamp list Process Variables. JAL evaluation tools have been built for evaluation of
the timestamp list case and the case of mixed clocked and timestamp list (which is possible with the above
architecture). Surprisingly, it has been found that timestamp list processing is not significantly more
expensive than that for uniform sampling clocks, at least for the test cases evaluated.
Within the current Data Platform test archive there are no super domains, that is, all Process Variables
within the archive are sampled at the same rate. However, a JAL tool is available which supplements the
test archive with simulated Process Variables having time-series data sampled at arbitrary rates. Tools have
been built for the evaluation of JAL super domain processing components. The additional processing
required for super domains has been found to subvert the performance gains for the upgraded Query Service,
sometime significantly. However, when no super domains are present within a request then performance
results are identical to those previously measured. That is, the super domain processing supports additional
use cases, but at the cost of additional processing when present. Thus, it is possible to configure the JAL
library to reject requests when time-domain collisions are detected.
Annotations API
Minimal support for the Data Platform Annotation Service is available in JAL Release 1.8.0. The basic
framework of JAL Annotation API was constructed where the Annotation Service connection mechanism
was built and tested using the standard “connection factory” framework of the Java API Library. An
interface IAnnotationService was designed and there exists a basic implementation supporting dataset
creation and query. Complete support for Data Platform archive annotations is planned for release 1.10.0.
At the time of JAL Release 1.8.0 the Annotation API development was postponed due to the rebuilding of
the Data Platform Annotation Service. As described in Subsection 2.6, the Annotation Service API was
completely redesigned during that timeframe. The JAL efforts in 1.8.0 were then directed toward the super
domain processing with the Query API as described above. The intent is to complete the JAL Annotation
API in Release 1.10.0.
Application Framework
A framework for rapid JAL application development was created and is now included within the library
distribution, the JAL Application Framework. This addition was not part of the original project scope but
was motivated by the need for rapid development of comprehensive testing tools for the Java API systems.
A framework was constructed to support tools for advanced testing of the JAL components (i.e., beyond
that of unit testing), particularly for the Query Service API components. This framework was then found

 109

to be particularly useful for rapid development of any application based upon the Java API Library. Thus,
the framework was generalized for all JAL clients and is now included in the JAL distribution. Moreover,
with this framework a suite of JAL applications has been built for comprehensive testing of the Data
Platform Core Services; these applications ship with the JAL distribution.
Previous experience demonstrated that test coverage for Query API components was beyond the scope of
standard unit testing. For example, running Query API unit testing became extremely time consuming and
required the availability of the Data Platform Test Archive, conditions discouraging users from performing
full JAL library builds (i.e., with unit testing). Thus, during development of the advanced Query API
components, standalone applications were built for comprehensive testing. The ability to simultaneously
query the Data Platform Test Archive, manage JAL configurations, analyze performance, and inspect
recovered data is best handled by specialized applications. It was then decided to build a framework for
rapid development of these specialized applications; that is, rather than create a suite of “one offs.” This in
turn lead to the development of a generalized application framework for all JAL clients with only modest
additional effort. Moreover, the specialized testing applications are now ship and build with the JAL
distribution. As in a similar fashion to Data Platform Core Services deployment, tools may be launched
with shell scripts included in the JAL distribution.

 110

 Figure 59: JAL Application Framework basic architecture

The basic architecture of the JAL Application Framework is shown in Figure 59. The framework is based
upon inheritance hierarchy. At the top of the hierarchy (i.e., most general) is the abstract base class
JalApplicationBase. It provides operations common to most Java applications, particularly for command-
line applications. Thus, there are numerous static methods for parsing application arguments for commands,
switches, variables, and properties, and outputs (not all are shown in the diagram). There are also static
methods for terminating application execution for various exceptional situations. Note that all these
methods are static so that they can be executed from the application entry point, the static main() method.
The JalApplicationBase class also contains methods for creating and opening output files, event logging,
execution timing, and building output report headers (again, not all operations are shown in the diagram).
The public method close() should always be invoked when exiting the application normally. This method
releases any application resources and performs any cleanup operations; it also aware of the state variables

 111

bolRun and bolCompleted, although child classes should be the primary managers of application states.
Specifically, sub-classes of JalApplicationBase should implement a run() method for normal application
execution as seen in the application class LowLevelQueryJalApp.
Note that application framework base class JalApplicationBase requires the template parameter T. As
indicated in Figure 59, the type of T must be derived from JalApplicationBase. This condition provides
JalApplicationBase awareness of the application class type (i.e., the final child class) maintained in
attribute clsApp.
The derived class JalQueryAppBase is an extension of JalApplicationBase. It is a convenience class
available primarily for testing of the JAL Query API components. Upon construction it automatically
connects to the Data Platform Query Service and creates the low-level Query API components
DpQueryConnection, QueryMessageBuffer, and QueryChannel. These components can then be used by child
application classes for operations and performance testing, or any other application specific function. Note
that the recoverRequest() operation is available for basic recovery of low-level QueryData Protocol Buffers
messages from a time-series data request. There are other methods for low-level interaction with the Data
Platform Query Service not shown. In normal termination, the shutdown() method should always be called
to release all the above Query API components and close the base application.
JAL Tool Suite
The JAL library contains a collection of common resources used in the JAL API subsystems; this includes
metadata record implementation, support for images, various enumerations of supported Data Platform
operations and types, data columns and tables, and time abstractions. In addition to these common resources,
the JAL library contains a separate suite of tools in support of application building, the JAL Tool Suite.
Most of these tools focus on the processing of performance evaluations results and are used by the JAL
Application Suite. However, they are publicly available to all JAL clients.

 112

 Figure 60: JAL Tool Suite partial contents

The partial contents of the JAL Tool Suite are shown in Figure 60; seen are the most-used resources of the
JAL Application Suite. At the top left-hand side of the diagram is the enumeration TestArchiveRequest.
This enumeration contains constants identifying various types of time-series data requests specific to the
Data Platform Test Archive. It contains collects of requests for Process Variables utilizing sampling clocks
only, requests for timestamp lists only, and requests using both sampling clocks and timestamp lists.
Moreover, the enumeration provides a variety of request sizes for each request type. Each enumeration
constant has a method create() for creating the actual DpDataRequest instance used by the Query API,
method getDataSourceCount() returning the number of Process Variables in the request, method
getRequestDuration() returning the request duration, and method estimateRequestSize() estimating the
request allocation size (in bytes).
The two abstract base classes at the top right-hand side of the diagram, ConfigScorerBase and
ConfigScoreBase, are used for processing of evaluation results. Within the Application Suite, test

 113

configurations and corresponding test results for the configuration are both contained within Java Record
instances. Thus, both Tool Suite classes contain the template parameters Config and Result which are the
Java Record types for the supported test configuration and the test result, respectively. Note that Java Record
types are immutable; they cannot be modified once created and may be considered stateless objects. Thus,
test configurations and test results are both immutable objects. The class ConfigScorerBase has the
additional template parameter Score, derived from ConfigScoreBase, which is the score type given to a test
result.
Each ConfigScoreBase represents the “score” for a specific test configuration, as determined from a
collection of test results having the same configuration (discussed next). The constructor for the class
requires a Config record instance identifying the targeted test configuration. Note that in most evaluation
operations the Config records have equivalence classes where a subset of fields is used for the actual test
configuration, this is the “configuration” recognized by the ConfigScoreBase-derived score class. The
remaining Config record fields are “free parameters” within the scoring. Thus, the potential for multiple
Result records applicable to one ConfigScoreBase-derived class, and the context for a configuration “score.”
Thus, Config records may also be considered “test case” records where both configuration parameters and
free parameters are included, however, the ConfigScoreBase class is solely interested in configuration fields.
Child classes of ConfigScoreBase are required to implement abstract methods extractDataRequest() and
extractDataRate() to extract the originating data request and the data rate (basic score) from a Result
record; these methods will throw exceptions if given Result records with incompatible configuration. Sub-
classes can add additional methods for custom scoring criteria; however, the data rate criterion is always
available from the base class. The addIn() method is used to add Result records to the current configuration
score; this method will also throw an exception if provided a Result record with incompatible configuration.
Finally, note that there are static methods createRateOrdering() and createHitOrdering() which provide
Java Comparator instances that order ConfigScoreBase-derived classes by the configuration data rate and
number of Result records with the given configuration, respectively. These are used by the
ConfigScorerBase class to order scores within Java container instances.

The ConfigScorerBase base class is used to process and manage a collection of Result record scores
produced from a test evaluation. Sub-classes are required to implement the abstract methods
extractConfiguration() and newScore() which, respectively, extracts the Config record from a Result
record, and creates a new Score instance from a given Config record (e.g., using the ConfigScoreBase
constructor). These operations are necessary since Config, Record, and Score are all generic types and the
base class has no a priori knowledge of their field and attribute structure. Once constructed, the
ConfigScorerBase class is populated with Result records using the score() methods. Note that the
ConfigScorerBase class can also be fully populated at construction using ConfigScorerBase(Result[*]).
After population the results of the processing are available via methods recoverScoresByRates() and
recoverScoresByHits(), both returning ordered collections of evaluation scores. There are other methods
for recovering evaluation results not shown, and various “printOut()” methods for printing results directly
to output streams. Clients can also implement additional methods for handling custom scoring criteria.
The last utility shown in Figure 60 is the DataRateLister class. It has the single template parameter Result
identifying the Java Record type containing results for a particular test evaluation. Instances of this class
are used for producing line printouts of ordered collections of Result records to output streams. Shown in
the diagram are the 4) enclosed interfaces: 1) Index, 2) RequestId, 3) DataRate, and 4) Allocation. Each
interface also requires the template parameter Result, and each interface exposes the single operation
extract(). It is thus assumed that Result records have fields identifying the test result index, the originating
data request ID, the resulting data rate, and the resulting memory allocation. With this implementation
instances of DataRateLister can be created using Lambda functions for the interfaces supplied to the
constructor. For example, we could employ
DataRateLister rateLister = new DataRateLister(r->r.index(), r->r.id(), r->r.rate(), r->r.alloc());

 114

Or, more concisely,
DataRateLister rateLister = new DataRateLister(Result::index, Result::id, Result::rate, Result::alloc);

Once constructed, the above parameters are printed to an output stream, line-by-line, for sorted collections
of Result records using the printOut() method.
JAL Applications Suite
As mentioned previously, within the Java API Library a comprehensive test suite for Data Platform
evaluation has been built, and continues to be extended, the JAL Application Suite. The evaluation
applications within the suite use the new JAL Application Framework and the JAL Tool Suite. Unlike the
benchmarking utilities included with the Data Platform distribution, the Application Suite applications
measure exact data rates and other performance metrics over a wide variety of configurations and conditions
chosen by users. The applications provide numerous command-line options for JAL component
configuration, as well as default configurations. They are designed to identify JAL API system and system
component configurations for optimal performance, as well as test Data Platform operation and
performance. Note that we also have at hand the Data Simulator for Data Platform testing; however, it is
designed primarily for load testing.
Previously such testing was performed in a “brute force” manner which became awkward and cumbersome,
especially considering the continued upgrades and modifications to the Data Platform and Java API Library.
Test suite applications are built as applications that perform a battery of tests for a specific API system or
component under a set of user-defined configurations, parameters, and loading conditions. Thus, the test
applications can be run to evaluate any Data Platform or Java API Library upgrades where results are
immediately available. These applications are also intended to identify operational issues within the Data
Platform, measure performance characteristics, and to identify optimal configuration parameters for the
JAL library and the Data Platform.

 115

The typical architecture of an evaluation application within the JAL Application Suite is shown in Figure
61. The example application, named TestApplication in the diagram, is designed to evaluate the operation
and performance of an arbitrary JAL Query API component, TestComponent, under various configurations
and load conditions. Referring to the top left-hand side of the diagram, being Query API based application

 Figure 61: JAL Application Suite example application basic architecture

TestApplication inherits from JAL Application Framework base class JalQueryAppBase binding the
template parameter T to its own class type. The class TestApplication maintains a reference to the
component under test, a TestComponent instance, shown in the top center of the diagram. By deriving from
JalQueryAppBase the application inherits a complement of common components used in Query API
operations; these components can either aid in the evaluations of TestComponent or be the actual test
component under evaluation. A single test component instance is generally used for all evaluations. Note
that TestApplication contains the static main() method used to execute any Java application; it accepts all
the command-line arguments to the application..
Referring to the bottom of Figure 61 the parameters for each TestComponent evaluation are contained in
records of type TestCase, while the results of an evaluation are contained in TestResult records. Most
important within TestCase is the evaluate() method. This method takes the test component as an argument,
configures it to the test case condition, then performs the test evaluations on the component returning the

 116

results as a new TestResult record; this is where the testing occurs. That is, each TestCase record knows
how to perform the intended test evaluations using its pre-defined test conditions. In other applications, the
evaluate() method may accept additional parameters, such as additional components required to test the
target component and any additional test parameters and/or test data (e.g., raw time-series data). Note that
the TestCase record contains both test configuration and any “free parameters” for the test case, as described
in the Tool Suite section.
Finally, note that the TestCase record maintains an index field, which is available simply to identify each
test configuration in the processing and displaying of evaluation results. Note further that the TestCase
record type maintains a static INDEX attribute which is incremented upon each record creation. The updated
INDEX value is used to populate the index field of each new record.

The TestResult record contains fields particular to the evaluations being performed. The test record records
should always contain a status field and a field containing the original test case generating the result,
specifically in the diagram, the fields status and cfg, respectively. The status field, of type StatusResult,
contains the general test condition, success or failure, and contains any failure messages and exceptions if
warranted (note that type ResultStatus is a common resource in JAL representing a general status
condition). Generally, there is always a rate field identifying the overall data rate measured during the test
evaluation, and other fields such as the total duration of the test dur, the total memory allocation required
alloc. Recall that these fields are expected by many of the tools in the JAL Tool Suite shown in Figure 60.
It may also contain other fields not shown in the diagram, such as the number of data messages, the types
of data messages recovered, and the number of correlated data blocks produced.
In the center left-hand side of Figure 61 is the class TestSuiteGenerator. Generally, all JAL Application
Suite applications have such a class to generate a suite of test cases from the application command-line
arguments. In our case, TestSuiteGenerator takes the collection of application command-line arguments
and produces a corresponding collection TestCase records via the generate() method. The application then
invokes the evaluate() method of each TestCase record on its TestComponent instance collecting the
TestResult result records. Test suite creation is typically done in the application constructor while test
suite evaluations are performed in the run() method.
In the top right-hand side of Figure 61 we see that TestApplication employs the ConfigScorerBase and
ConfigScoreBase tools from the JAL Tool Suite. The classes TestScorer and TestScore are respectively
derived from these base classes to recognize the TestCase and TestResult records used in the TestComponent
evaluations; the template parameter bindings are shown explicitly in the diagram. Via its inheritance and
template bindings the TestScorer class will automatically score any collection of TestResult records
according to their data rate fields, that is the rate field. Generally, JAL Application suite applications use
additional criteria for scoring, which would be accommodated by adding support within the TestScore class.
 2.13. Data Simulator
A Data Simulator was developed for testing of the Data Platform Ingestion Service capabilities [39]. The
Data Simulator creates a variety of ingestion scenarios for the Data Platform, including that of multiple,
simultaneous Data Providers, and multiple heterogeneous data types. As its name suggests, the Data
Simulator creates simulated data of any type and of any configuration supported by the Data Platform. It
is configurable to supply data at arbitrary rates and arbitrary time intervals to test the ingestion capabilities
of the Data Platform. This tool is configurable to supply any combination of supported heterogeneous data
within the same ingestion frame, within multiple ingestion frames, within one data stream, or over multiple,
concurrent data streams. The Data Simulator is intended to test the overall performance and capacity of the
Data Platform, including full load testing to determine where the archiving system fails. Note that a data
simulator was previously developed for the MLDP prototype of the Phase I effort, however, it cannot fully
exploit all the features of the new Data Platform system, nor does it conform to the new interfaces.

 117

 Figure 62: Data Simulator architecture and relationships

A basic architectural diagram for the Data Simulator is shown in Figure 62. As seen in the right-hand side
of the diagram the Data Simulator relies on the Java API Library for communications with the Data Platform.
Thus, the measured throughput involves the entire stack of data transport. Within the Data Simulator the
focus of operation is the active class IngestionScenarioExecutor. This class initializes the scenario
according to a scenario definition file, then executes the scenario collecting the results upon completion.
Note that the ingestion scenario is completely defined within the scenario definition file scenario_def.yml.
This file defines the configuration of all ingestion frames (i.e., sizes, data types, etc.) as well as each Data
Provider used to deliver them.
As seen in Figure 62 the IngestionScenarioExecutor class manages a collection of DataProvider
components. Each component represents a separate Data Provider which independently, and
simultaneously, supplies simulated data to the Ingestion Service. Each DataProvider component is first
initialized, where it creates its ingestion frame payload (discussed below), connects to the Ingestion Service
using the Java Library API IIngestionStream interface, then waits for execution start by the
IngestionScenarioExecutor (all Data Providers can be started simultaneously or at separate times). Once
started a DataProvider component is completely autonomous and continues to supply data until its payload
is exhausted (normal operation) or an exception occurs (e.g., an ingestion failure). Each DataProvider
component contains its own internal clock which specifies the rate of frame transmission and any delays in
execution. After completing its execution, each DataProvider reports its ingestion results back to the
IngestionScenarioExecutor for final calculations. The scenario does not end until all DataProvider
components have completed, a terminating exception occurs (e.g., Ingestion Service failure, platform
failure, etc.), or a timeout condition is reached (optional). The IngestionScenarioExecutor collects,
computes, and records all results as an IngestionScenarioResults object. Note that the scenario will not

 118

necessarily terminate if a single DataProvider component fails; typically, the failure is simply recorded
within the scenario results along with the terminating exception and any partial results obtained from the
DataProvider instance before failure.

Note from Figure 62 that ingestion frames (of JAL type IngestionFrame discussed in Subsection 2.12) are
created by the class IngestionFrameFactory. Instances of this class are the source of all simulated data for
the ingestion scenario. Each IngestionFrameFactory instance is initialized to the data type and frame size
it produces, then simply creates simulated data henceforth (i.e. with the build() method). To prevent any
competition between platform computing resources all ingestion frames are created a priori, that is, before
the scenario executes (within the IngestionScenarioExecutor initialize() method). A specialized queue,
of type IngestionFrameQueue, is needed to hold the pre-built ingestion frame payloads. To prevent heap
overflow, this queue stores most of the ingestion frames to disk. A portion of the payload remains in an
active memory buffer for rapid access by DataProvider components; the buffer is continuously replenished
from disk storage by independent threads as ingestion frames are removed. As shown in Figure 62, each
DataProvider component contains an IngestionFrameQueue instance which houses its ingestion payload.
Each payload is created during initialization with the fill() method.
Not shown in Figure 62 is event logging by a DataProvider component. Event logging is an optional feature
which provides varying details (i.e., log levels) of component operation during execution. All components
can log events to the same file for post-scenario analysis. This feature can be valuable in diagnosis since
ingestion scenario execution is asynchronous where all DataProvider components are independent and
compete for Ingestion Service resources.
From the above description it is seen that a broad spectrum of ingestion scenarios is possible. It is primarily
intended for load testing of the Data Platform where large amounts of data are ingestion over a sustained
duration, thus stressing the archive capacity. Note that the initialization process for such scenarios with
large payloads can be lengthy, as all simulated data is created a priori. As implied in the right-hand-side of
Figure 62 the Data Simulator can also be used in performance tuning of the configuration parameters for
both the Data Platform and the Java Library API.
 2.14. Performance and Operations Evaluations
Throughout project duration comprehensive performance and operations evaluations were periodically
conducted on the Data Platform Core Services, often in conjunction with the companion Java API Library.
These evaluations, although requiring notable effort, provided important insight into additional
performance and operational improvements. At the time of this writing, we have conducted performance
evaluations of Data Platform Release 1.9.0 in conjunction with Java API Library Release 1.8.0. Here
maximum data rates are confirmed at 500 MBps for the Ingestion Service and 200 MBps for the Query
Service (i.e., for time-series data requests). Although incomplete, we see a preliminary 2x to 3x additional
performance gain over that of the previous releases. Once JAL Release 1.10.0 is available we expect to
confirm data rates of over 1 GBps for the Ingestion Service and 500 MBps for the Query Service. The
latter rates are projected from the benchmarking utilities of the Data Platform in conjunction with the
serialized data column feature in Data Platform Release 1.9.0. Thus, we expect a 30-fold improvement
over the original stated project goal of 32 MBps data transmission rates.
Several archive redesigns were developed throughout the project to achieve these outstanding performance
figures. The first major Data Platform archive redesign was motivated by the results of a battery of tests
for the Ingestion Service and described in ref. [20]. The archive schema was refactored to store time-series
data in compressed format, specifically, that of the Protocol Buffers messages used to transport the data
through gRPC. (The redesigned archive was included in Data Platform Release 1.5.0) Not only did the
new archive schema improve storage time, it decreased storage space and improved efficiency. The new
design also facilitated the additional performance improvements described in Release 1.9.0, that of client-
side data column serialization. Those improvements were motivated by the results seen in code profiling.
The results suggested that the Query Service was “process bound,” specifically, the gains when using data

 119

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

streaming appeared unusually slow (as compared to unary Remote Procedure Call). It was thus found that,
indeed, multiple serialization and deserialization operations could be eliminated with the new archive
design. Specifically, with compressed data storage the Data Platform no longer needed to unpack data for
inspection and storage.
The comprehensive set of evaluations for the Data Platform Ingestion Service, Query Service, and the Java
API Library is described in detail within Appendix I. Although the actual performance numbers are now
stale due to the additional performance improvements implemented in Data Platform Release 1.9.0 and
Java API Library Release 1.8.0, evaluations are described in detail within Appendix I as they guided the
strategy for both new implementations. The evaluations were designed to consider all factors to determine
optimal configuration and parameter values for maximum performance. In fact, many of the results in
Appendix I motivated the new design within Data Platform Release 1.9.0. All evaluations were performed
on a single MacMini development platform with Apple M2 Pro processor containing 10 cores, 16 Gbytes
memory, and ½ TByte SSD drive. The platform hosted the Data Platform Cores Services, the Java API
Library, and all test fixtures and test applications.
The comprehensive testing described in Appendix I was initially done by “brute force.” It became apparent
then that a more formalized approach for such testing was required. This became the motivation for the
JAL test framework which then morphed into the JAL Applications Framework.
