 2.16. MLDP Facility Installation and Deployment
Facility installation and deployment scoped in the original project requirements and represents a project
milestone. At the time of this writing 2 components of the MLDP, the Aggregator and the Data Platform,
are installed, deployed, and operating at 2 locations: 1) the SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory in Menlo
Park, California [60], and the NASA Artemis II Spacecraft Test Facility in Sandusky, Ohio [34]. The
MLDP with full throughput has not yet been deployed, but both Aggregator system and Data Platform
system are installed and operational at the SLAC facility (although not yet integrated).
Aggregator
At present the Aggregator is installed and operating at 2 facilities: 1) SLAC in support of LCLS-II [45]
operating at 1 kHz for ~1,000 signals, and 2) at NASA’s Neil Armstrong Test Facility in support of Artemis
II [46] operating at 250 kHz for 1,024. signals. There is potential that Arizona State University (ASU) will
use the Osprey 250k for their CXFEL accelerator [47] in crystallography Eiger detector data collection at
the ASU Biodesign Institute [48]; Osprey DCS is in current contract with ASU the for low-level timing and
acquisition system.

 120

The prototype Aggregator system was installed at the SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory in Menlo
Park, California [60]. The prototype was installed as the part of the Beam Synchronous Acquisition System
(BSAS) [61] then upgraded for support of the LCLS-II project [36]. The current system is fully software-
based and generates HDF5 files which are stored locally.
The Osprey 250k hardware component of the Aggregator system has been installed at the Neil Armstrong
Test Facility in Sandusky, Ohio [46]. The system performs the fast sampling, data acquisition, and
collection NASA’s Artemis II Orion spacecraft test platform. A bank of 32 chassis is stacked for front-end
acquisition of 1,024 signals at 250 kHz. The deployment here represents a significant accomplishment
considering the outstanding data rates achieved.
Data acquisition sampling rates were confirmed up to 1 kHz at Phase II start using the fully software-based
Aggregator (the BSAS system). As reported in a previous performance report, the maximum sampling of
the hardware-based Aggregator (the Osprey 250k) seen in facility operation is 250 kHz while the maximum
tested rate of a single chassis is 750 kHz [20].
Data Platform
The Data Platform was installed at the SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory in in Menlo Park in summer
2025. A summer intern student built an application reading the HDF5 files supplied by the Aggregator
BSAS system which are then loaded into the Data Platform via the Ingestion Service. The intent to build
an “EPICS Interface” for the Aggregator system where EPICS pvAccess NTTable instances are translated
into gRPC IngestionDataFrame instances that can be directly supplied to the Data Platform. The EPICS
Interface system is covered further in Chapter VI Subsection VI.2.
SLAC has a Machine Learning and Artificial Intelligence (ML/AI) group dedicated to application of ML/AI
techniques to accelerator [62]. The intent is to use the ML/AL capabilities of the MLDP in support of their
activities. Efforts are currently being pursued to integrate the MLDP into the SLAC control system (e.g.,
the EPICS Interface and a planned Python client library).

 4.4. Demonstration Application
The Data Platform Demonstration Application described in Subsection 2.9 was built expressly for
disseminating project results. It ships with the latest Data Platform release distribution (version 1.11.0), is
simple to run requiring only MongoDB, and simple to use featuring an intuitive Graphical User Interface
(GUI) for interactions with all Data Platform Core Services. Being a standalone desktop application, no
additional servers or services are required (other than MongoDB) which differs from the Web Application.
To further simplify matters the Data Platform installation includes a utility for populating a Dockers
container with the MongoDB data archive. The Demonstration Application covers most use cases
supported by the Data Platform.
Being a collection of collaborating services, Data Platform functionality was previously difficult to
demonstrate to prospective customers. The Demonstration Application now clearly establishes its advanced
support for data science, Machine Learning, and Artificial Intelligence applications and software
application development. Potential customers can download and install the Data Platform from the Data
Platform public repository [13], then launch the Demonstration Application on their local platform without
additional setup (the Core Services run inside the application). The Demonstration Application GUI
features tools for intuitive data archive searches and request building using archive metadata. Archive
annotation and annotation searches using request building is also demonstrated by the application.
 4.5. Web Application
The Web Application also offers tremendous potential in the dissemination of project results. Deployed
within a standard internet web browser, accessible via a public URL, and supported by an independent host
server, the Web Application requires only commonly available resources to demonstrate many of the
commercial features and standard use cases for the Data Platform. This capability was recognized early in
Phase I which motivated its development. By maintaining a test archive on a dedicated server with public
URL, remote users can access the URL with a web browser loading the Web Application and evaluating
many of the MLDP features.

 122

We note that deployment of the Web Application is more complex than that for the Demonstration
Application. The most practical use of the Web Application for demonstration is to deploy the Web
Application service on a public server, that is, rather than locally. Deployment has significant ecosystem
requirements such as the Envoy proxy server described in Subsection 2.11.
 4.6. Public Dissemination
Osprey DCS has provided general notification and announcements of Data Platform development and the
larger MLDP efforts to its current customer base and within the control system and accelerator community.
There are now several projects and institutions using, planning to use, or considering use of the MLDP,
including the Arizona State University (ASU) CXFEL project [47] (Osprey 250k only), the National
Aeronautics and Space Agency (NASA) Artemis II project [46] (Osprey 250k only), and Stanford Linear
Accelerator Complex (SLAC) LCLS-II project [45] (both Aggregator and Data Platform).

III. PRODUCTS
Primary Product
The final product of the Phase II project is the Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP). It is described
concisely in Chapter I, Section I.2, more fully in the Project Overview of Chapter II, Subsection II.1.3, by
components and subsystems within Subsection II.2, and listed as a product within Section 4 of this Chapter.
The MLDP is a “data-science ready” platform for data science, Machine Learning (ML), and Artificial
Intelligence (AI) applications in the diagnosis, modeling, control, and optimization of particle accelerator
and large experimental physics facilities. It presents data scientists and applications a consistent, data-
centric interface to archive data standardizing implementation and deployment of ML/AI algorithms to
different operations configurations within the same facility, or between facilities. The MLDP can also be
deployed at user facilities for experimental data collection, archiving and analysis. It is capable of
acquisition and archiving of heterogeneous data from experimental diagnostics along with system
configurations, control system process variables, and any metadata required for provenance. Thus, the
MLDP can manage experimental data through its entire lifecycle. Thus, the MLDP is a “facilitator” of data
science and ML/AI applications. Considering the outstanding performance now seen by the MLDP, and
the size and scope of accelerator and experimental physics facilities, the MLDP represents the next
generation of technologies for data management in data science applications.
The MLDP is composed of subsystems performing sub-tasks in full-stack support of data science and
ML/AI applications, such as the Aggregator front-end for high-speed data acquisition and collection and
the Data Platform for heterogeneous data archiving and management. These subsystems represent
independent standalone products, also have components and supporting tools. All major products of the
project are described in Section 4.

1. Publications
No journal publications have yet been produced. Journal publication(s) are anticipated as projects products
once real-world application data is available.

2. Intellectual Property

3. Technologies and Techniques
The topics of this section are the major technologies and techniques developed to satisfy project goals.
Note that Osprey DCS claims no property rights over these technologies and techniques. They are publicly
available and to be shared with the accelerator and experimental physics community through the open-
source code repositories and hardware documentation contained in Subsection 1.2.

 125

 3.1. Fast Data Acquisition and Alignment
Performance for the data acquisition and alignment process was significantly improved using novel
techniques developed during the project; the idea was to combine data acquisition and alignment, moving
the process upstream into the hardware level. Within the Osprey 250k, the processes of sampling,
acquisition, and data correlation are combined, being performed by Field Programmable Gate Arrays
(FPGAs) within the datastream. The innovation also required development of the Quartz board hosting the
Analog-to-Digital Converters (AD/Cs), the timing synchronization between stacked chassis, and the chassis
Input/Output (I/O) systems [49]. Developed also was the FPGA firmware performing the triggered
sampling, acquisition, and alignment [12]. All designs and firmware are available in the public repositories
listed in Subsection 1.2.
 3.2. Fast Heterogeneous Data Storage and Retrieval
The Data Platform system contains novel technology for the rapid archiving and recovery of heterogeneous
time-series data. Specifically, the Ingestion Service and the Query Service components contain novel
techniques for the fast storage and retrieval of heterogenous, time-series data and metadata necessary for
data science applications within a large particle accelerator facility or other large experimental facility
within the Department of Energy complex. This technology has continued to improve over the duration of
the project where data transmission and archiving rates are upwards of 1 GBps for a single development
platform. Time-series query data rates are expected at 500 MBps. The technology is embedded in the Data
Platform Core Services [15].
 3.3. Archive Annotation
A significant technology developed during the project is the archive annotation capability of the Data
Platform system supported by the Annotation Service. It offers the ability to augment archived data with
user notes, data associations, and calculations, or more generally “user annotations.” This technology
provides a “value-added” capability to the data archive created from client interaction. Any annotation
added to the archive is subsequently available to all other users of the MLDP. The annotation capability is
part of the Data Platform Core Services [15].
 3.4. Core Services Design Pattern
All services within the Data Platform share a common design pattern, or technique. The design facilitates
significant concurrency and flexibility using a general producer-consumer framework with multi-threading;
we feel that the technique is novel enough to warrant the extensive coverage included in Chapter II
Subsection II.2.7. The Common Core Services Framework provides a consistent technique for client-
service interaction, retrieving all client requests, processing request data, and dispatching the processed
responses. There is a common architecture for service initialization and finalization, transport of incoming
and outgoing data, and for data processing tasks. There is also a common framework for service
configuration.
 3.5. Fast Ingestion Stream Processing
The ingestion datastream processing available from the Data Platform is also novel. Clients have direct
access to the ingestion data needed for real-time applications, for example, those used in the control of
facility operations or real-time observation of experimental data. Real-time access to ingestion data is
provided by both the Ingestion Service and the Ingestionstream Service. Note that the algorithm plugin
framework was not implemented, it is intended for future development, likely within the Java API Library.
The plugin framework, if available, would be a significant addition to the novelty of ingestion stream
processing.

 126

4. Other Products
 4.1. Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP)
The Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP) is the definitive product of the project. It is the
amalgamation of all other products produced during the project duration. It is composed of both hardware
and software components.
The MLDP provides full-stack support for data science and ML/AI applications at particle accelerator and
large experimental physics facilities. It supports ML/AI applications from front-end, high-speed data
acquisition of heterogeneous, time-series data, through data archiving and management, to back-end
inspection and analysis. Considering the nature of machine learning and the scope and scale of typical
accelerator facilities, it represents the next generation of data acquisition, archiving, and data management
technologies for particle accelerator and large experimental physics systems.
The MLDP can also be deployed at user facilities for experimental data collection, archiving, and analysis.
It is capable of acquisition and archiving of heterogeneous data from experimental diagnostics such as
images, arrays, etc., along with system configurations in the form of scalars, tables, and structures, control
system process variables, and any metadata required for provenance. Thus, the MLDP can manage
experimental data through its entire lifecycle, from acquisition and archiving, through analysis and
investigation, to release and final publication. It has demonstrated unprecedented abilities in data collection
rates and data management, from acquisition through automation and publication.
     4.2.        Osprey 250k
The Osprey 250k is the first hardware product in the Osprey DCS product line [12]. It is the front end of
the MLDP providing the high-speed acquisition and correlation of data from facility hardware. It can be
connected directly to analog hardware output signals, requiring no device drivers or other supporting
software/firmware for communications.
One Osprey 250k chassis can simultaneously acquire, collect, and align 32 differential analog input signals
at sampling rates up to 250 kHz. Analog signals are digitized to 24-bit floating point values. Up to 32
chassis can be stacked with a single input trigger to concurrently process a maximum of 1,024 input signals.
Output signals are available from high-speed Ethernet ports, as User Datagram Protocol (UDP) packets
containing the digitized samples aligned with their common timestamps. Each packet typically contains
the output data for 4 sample triggers.
All sampling, alignment, and synchronization is done with a single Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA)
within an Osprey 250k chassis. Four 8-channel, 24-bit digitizers (AD/Cs) are located at the input of the
chassis which feed the FPGA. The chassis also includes a 50-Ohm input trigger signal and multiple fiber-
optic fanout signals for synchronization with additional chassis. The hardware setup is simple compared
to other acquisition systems containing CPUs, operating systems, and supporting software. Thus, site
installation and configuration of the Osprey 250k is relatively straightforward.
 4.3. Data Platform
The Data Platform is the archive management system for the MLDP [11]. It is responsible for the high-
speed ingestion of sampled, time-correlated, heterogeneous data, such as that provided by the MLDP
Aggregator system. It is also the back end of the MLDP providing users access and interaction with the
data archive. The Data Platform is composed of a collection of collaborating services communicating using
the Data Platform Communications Framework. All services within the Data Platform are built on top a
common framework, the Common Core Services Framework. Note that the Communications Framework
is an independent, gRPC-based system that facilitates communication between services, and with Data
Platform clients. The Common Core Services Framework functions in conjunction with the Data Platform
Communications Framework. Thus, the Data Platform features a modular, service-based design pattern
that facilities maintenance, upgradeability, and extensibility.

 127

The Data Platform is a fully independent, standalone, subsystem of the MLDP. It does not require an
EPICS environment, nor does it specifically require the Aggregator system for archive population; any
client conforming to the Communications Framework ingestion API can supply data to the Data Platform.
Moreover, the Data Platform has a mature installation and deployment system that is publicly available
[13]. All source code for the Data Platform Core Services is also publicly available [15]. The Data Platform
can easily be downloaded, installed, and deployed on local platforms with minimum configuration and
ecosystem requirements (Java, MongoDB, and setting a single environment variable).
The Data Platform installation and deployment system has been greatly improved since inception; it is
available in the “Releases” section [13] at the main repository site [11]. It is now a formal tool for
disseminating the Data Platform system, instructions, and documentation. The deployment system provides
local installation via an installer archive that is downloaded using a standard web browser. All release
distributions of the Data Platform are available on its GitHub repository for installation and deployment
[13]. There are instructions for configuring the installation to specific platform on the repository website.
The Ingestion Service, the Query Service, Annotation Service, an Ingestionstream Service, along with
companion benchmarking and validation utilities are all included in the current release [11]. The installer
has full functionality and can locally deploy current development releases of the Data Platform for
inspection, testing, evaluation, and operation.
 4.4. Web Application
The Web Application represents an additional product of the project in support of the MLDP [14]. It is tool
providing remote access and interaction with the MLDP data archive using a standard internet web browser
and a public URL. The Web Application interacts with the MLDP archive using the Data Platform
Communication Framework and Core Services. It offers a subset of Data Platform functionality to remote
users, that is, those features viable on a web browser platform. The Web Application can perform archive
searches and request building using archive metadata, perform time-series data queries, and annotate
datasets within the archive. Adding time-series data to the archive is not permitted.
 4.5. Demonstration Application
The Demonstration Application is a product explicitly intended for dissemination of project results,
specifically the capabilities of the Data Platform [55]. It is a standalone desktop application that
demonstrates most all the Data Platform functionality, including data ingestion and archive annotation, as
well as archive searches. The Demonstration Application ships with the latest Data Platform version,
Release 1.11.0. All Data Platform Core Services are built into and run inside the application, the only
requirement of the local platform being a MongoDB installation, or a Dockers container running MongoDB.
For further convenience, a utility for creating a MongoDB Dockers container is included with Data Platform
Release 1.11.0.
The Demonstration Application has a Graphical User Interface based upon JavaFX [44] featuring screens
for time-series data query and display, both tabular and charted. There are tools for archive searches and
request building using archive metadata. Annotations can also be searched, queried, and created. The
application includes a basic data simulator that can be used to populate the MLDP archive with simulated
time-series data and metadata which is then available for searches and request building. There are also
tools for Data Event monitoring of the simulated Process Variables during operation of the data simulator.
As a collection of collaborating services, it is difficult to demonstrate the Data Platform operation without
building data science and ML/AI applications supported by it. The Demonstration application was built to
fill this void, displaying the intended operation of the Data Platform, with all its collaborating Core Services
to perform common data science tasks. Currently it provides no function other than demonstration.
We are considering use of the same technologies and techniques of the Demonstration Application in the
development of a desktop application supporting remote interaction with the MLDP archive (i.e., replacing
or augmenting the Web Application). It is a straightforward task to connect the application to remote Data
Platform Core Services, rather than the internal services. All the screens and tools of the current application

 128

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

could be used for interaction with a remote archive. Additional screens could be built for connecting to
remote services, or remote URLs, and the history of such connections could be stored locally in history
and/or configuration files.

IV. PARTICIPANTS AND COLLABORATING ORGANIZATIONS
All design and development activities have been internal to Osprey DCS, although some development of
the Aggregator component was performed off-site, specifically the Osprey 250k (see below). Formal
collaboration concerning the full MLDP system is now possible as the APIs are now mature. Osprey DCS
installed the Data Platform at the SLAC accelerator facility in the summer of 2025. The Osprey 250k is
installed at the NASA Artemis II test facility in Sandusky, Ohio.
Other external organizations have expressed interest in utilizing MLDP components; the Data Platform is
particularly attractive since it is public domain software. Osprey DCS is currently contracting with Arizona
State University (ASU) for low-level support of timing systems for their X-Ray crystallography Eiger

some interest in using the Data Platform.

development support concerning their efforts on a current DOE project (SCORPIUS); the contract included
specific investigations of the MLDP Core Services as a system component, at that time the “Datastore”
component. Details are provided below in Section IV.2.

V. IMPACT

1. Impact on Principle Discipline
The Machine Learning Data Platform (MLDP) is a product providing full-stack support for data science,
Machine Learning, and Artificial Intelligence (ML/AI) applications at particle accelerator and large
experimental physics facilities. It supports ML/AI applications from front-end, high-speed acquisition of
heterogeneous, time-series data, through data archiving and management, to back-end inspection and
analysis. The MLDP represents a “data-science ready” platform for data analysis and ML/AI applications
in the diagnosis, modeling, control, and optimization of these facilities. It presents physicists, data scientists,
engineers, and applications a consistent, data-centric interface to archive data standardizing implementation
and deployment of ML/AI algorithms to different operations configurations within the same facility, or
between facilities. Considering the nature of machine learning and the scope and scale of typical accelerator
facilities, it represents a next generation of data acquisition, archiving, and data management technologies
for particle accelerator systems.
The MLDP can also be deployed at user facilities for experimental data collection, archiving and analysis.
It is capable of acquisition and archiving of heterogeneous data from experimental diagnostics (e.g., images,
arrays, etc.) along with system configurations (e.g., scalars, tables), control system process variables, and
any metadata required for provenance. Thus, the MLDP can manage experimental data through its entire
lifecycle, from acquisition and archiving, through analysis and investigation, to release and final publication.
It has demonstrated unprecedented abilities in data collection rates and data management, from acquisition
through automation and publication.
Being an open-source, public-domain project, the MLDP is designed to impact the broadest possible market
while increasing the accessibility by minimizing the required expertise for installation, deployment, and
operation of the MLDP and its components. It is a facilitator of data science, performing the overhead of
acquisition, collection, management, and analysis support allowing data scientist to focus on the task at
hand. It is anticipated that the availability of the MLDP will significantly enhance the applicability of
Machine Learning, Artificial Intelligence, and general data science techniques to the operation and
optimization of accelerator systems and other large experimental physics facilities within the Department
of Energy complex and industry.

2. Impact on Other Disciplines
The MLDP is a tool for fast data acquisition, archiving, and management that supports general machine
learning and data science techniques applied to correlated, heterogeneous, time-series data. The MLDP
also manages a broad spectrum of metadata supporting advanced archive searches, request building, and
data provenance. Although the Aggregator system is tailored to facilities using the EPICS control system,
the Data Platform data ingestion and archiving system is fully independent (i.e., standalone and EPICS
independent). Additionally, the Osprey 250k hardware product is fully independent, although generally
used within an EPICS environment. Any facility producing data conforming to the ingestion
communications protocol of the Data Platform can archive data within the MLDP and, thus, utilize all back-
end data science capabilities, including the Web Application for remote inspection and interaction. Due to
this generality, there is expectation of broader application beyond particle accelerator and large
experimental physics systems.

3. Impact on Development of Human Resources
The MLDP is a productivity tool for the data sciences and scientists; it is intended to significantly reduce
the overhead in data management. The MLDP facilitates data science activities and expedites the

 133

development of ML/AI applications allowing physicists, data scientists, and engineers to focus on facility
and/or experimental tasks rather than data acquisition and management.

4. Impact on Physical, Institutional, and Information Resources
The Machine Learning Data Platform is a tool for acquisition, collection, and dissemination of data and
information pertaining to facility operations and experimental data. Data availability is tailored to data
science personnel and data science application development. Additionally, the companion Web
Application provides general remote access and interaction with all archived data. The intent is to make
facility operations data and experimental data as widely available and accessible as possible. Note that the
MLDP supports full data management through its entire lifecycle.

5. Impact on Technology Transfer
No techniques, technologies, tools, or resources developed in this project are considered proprietary or
business sensitive. Consequently, all products are public domain and may be freely transferred throughout
the community.

6. Impact on Society Beyond Science and Technology
Unknown.

7. Foreign Spending
No project expenditures have been made in or to foreign countries.

 134

VI. CHANGES AND PROBLEMS
Slight modifications to the project approach and schedule were made during project duration. The
following list contains the notable changes:
 1. Performance modifications to the Aggregator and Data Platform Core Services continued
 throughout the entire project duration.
 2. Full integration of the Aggregator and Data Platform was only partially completed.
 3. The Data Platform distribution system was initiated early and now fully mature.
 4. The Annotation Service required additional development beyond that anticipated.
 5. A data exporting feature not originally scoped was added to the Annotation Service.
 6. The Java API Library (JAL) required additional development, but functionality was enhanced.
 7. Ingestion datastream processing was only partially completed. Additional processing is likely
 within the Java API Library (JAL).
 8. The Python language library development has been postponed.
Most of the above topics are natural in the process of research and development where unanticipated
opportunities and issues arise during the development of new products and technologies. They are covered
in further detail below.
Performance was a major focus within the project and opportunities for improvement were prioritized and
exploited whenever they appeared; these opportunities often arose mid-project during performance studies.
This focus typically postponed development of other features scoped within the project; however, this was
a deliberate decision. It was our intent to build the system core to its fullest potential before addressing
features grounded on core operation. The external interfaces to the MLDP must also be robust and mature
before becoming public.
The installation and deployment system for the Data Platform was also developed much earlier than
originally scheduled, as this significantly increased productivity within the development team. MLDP
documentation has proceeded in step with MLDP development, rather than using a focused effort at project
completion.
The Python API Library intended for the Data Platform was not developed, in conflict with the original
project scope. Development here was postponed, focusing instead on development of the ingestion
datastream processing of the Advanced Data Sciences portion of the project. The latter feature is central to
the MLDP real-time capabilities and of significant customer interest. The addition of a Python API Library
can be addressed at a later data as it is essentially self-contained.

1. Changes in Approach
 1.1. Aggregator System
The approach in Aggregator development changed from a strictly software-based implementation to that of
a hardware-based, front-end acquisition system. Within the original project narrative, this approach was
offered as a possible tactic for significant performance improvements. With additional support from the
NASA Artemis II project Osprey DCS was able to fully pursue this idea, culminating with the Osprey 250k
data acquisition system, our first hardware product. The Osprey 250k was described in detail in Chapter II,
Subsection II.2.4.
The idea is to combine sampling, acquisition, and data alignment, moving it upstream into the hardware
level. Within the Osprey 250k chassis, analog input signals are first digitized with Analog to Digital
Converters (AD/C) then sampled and aligned with Field Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs). An input

 135

trigger signal is used for the timed acquisition. Processed data is available as User Datagram Protocol
(UDP) packets fed to output high-speed Ethernet ports. The packets consist of rows of input signals aligned
to their common timestamps. Each Osprey 250k chassis support 32 differential input signals or 64
common-ground input signals. Chassis can be stacked with a common trigger to support simultaneous
acquisition of up to 1,024 signals. The maximum sampling rate for 32 chassis under simultaneous
acquisition is 250 kHz.
 1.2. Data Platform Core Service and Communication Framework
The Core Services of the original MLDP prototype have been redesigned, rebuilt, and refactored multiple
times during the project. In fact, the Core Services have had 3 full re-writes during the project. The initial
rebuilt was a possibility that was anticipated and addressed in the SBIR Phase II Project Narrative. The
details of the final design differ from preliminary designs offered in the original narrative, as they are the
result of exhaustive design and performance studies conducted at Phase II inception. The upgrades to the
Core Services continued within the project as opportunity warranted, typically motivated by the results of
performance and operational studies. Performance and robustness were key issues since project inception
and all efforts have been made to improve these areas whenever possible, even though it diverted resources
from other project activities. It was our intent to have the core systems of the MLDP functioning as robust
as possible before adding additional functionality. This is especially important for the Data Platform
Communications Framework which, once public, cannot be modified without significant effort and
customer disruptions.
 1.3. Data Platform Distribution
The Data Platform distribution system was initiated early in the project, as it assisted significantly in team
development. Developers were able to download, install, and deploy the latest version of the Data Platform
code base from a single, central repository, without the significant overhead of building, configuring, and
deploying from multiple repositories on the local development platform. Thus, it was natural to build out
the distribution system for public release. Substantial effort has been dedicated to the system as it now
service as a central repository for all Data Platform product distribution, including documentation,
instructions, and release notes. Since the distribution system was initiated early it is now fully mature,
complete with instructions, online documentation, and release notes for each version.
 1.4. Data Platform Annotation Service
Development for the Annotations Service was initiated according to the original schedule. However, due
to its sophistication we decided to “front-load” the process, as it is a complex system providing a critical
feature of the MLDP. Development in this area exceed original predictions. Additionally, the design of
the Annotation Service changed repeated during the project as the system matured. The Annotation Service
now boasts a “cleaner” and simpler API than earlier versions. We felt this additional efforted was warranted
due to the complexity of the archive annotation process and the need to present clients streamlined
interactions. In total, implementation of the Annotation Service required over a year of development
requiring approximately 6 man-months of effort; over twice that originally anticipated.
A data export feature was also added to the Annotation Service, which was not in the original project scope.
However, the export feature was a valuable addition to the MLDP requested by customers, and a natural
extension of the Annotation Service. Archive annotation requires the identification of datasets within the
MLDP archive, that is, the data to be annotated. A significant effort had been made developing the model
support datasets thus it was already available for data exporting. Additionally, the ability to export both
time-series data alongside calculation annotations was not originally scoped but added as a useful feature
for customers.
 1.5. Java Language Library
The Java API Library (JAL) was originally intended as a simple, high-level interface to the Data Platform
hiding clients from the details of gRPC communications in the Data Platform Communications Framework.
However, it became necessarily more complex to support performance capabilities of the Data Platform. It

 136

was decided early in the project to distribute some time-series processing to the client side to avoid process-
bound Data Platform Query Service operations. The Data Platform Core Services can simultaneously
service multiple clients; thus, it was reasonable to offload repeated data processing to the client whenever
possible, as the client is assumed to have a dedicated platform. The situation for the Query Service and
time-series data requests was described in detail within Subsection II.2.12. Thus, development effort and
scope for the client Java API Library was beyond that originally anticipated. We also note that an
application framework for rapid development of applications utilizing the JAL has been built and included
in the distribution; this feature was not originally scoped but is a significant addition.
 1.6. Ingestionstream Processing
A notable change in approach from the original Phase II Project Narrative concerns the algorithm plugin
feature of the MLDP. The algorithm plugin feature is part of the Advanced Data Science portion of Phase
II and was originally intended to be included within the Ingestionstream Service of the Data Platform. It
now likely to be moved to the Java API Library (JAL) supporting the Data Platform. It was determined
that a general plugin capability for the Data Platform was an overreach of its Communication Framework
architecture, which uses the gRPC library for the underlying communication operations. The Core Services
still contains an Ingestionstream Service that provides general “Data Event” capabilities, but supporting a
general algorithm plugin feature is beyond the scope of gRPC. Providing this feature within the high-level
programming framework of the Java API Library is more natural.
 1.7. Python Language Library
In compensation of the additional resources required for the Annotation Service it was decided to delay
development of the Python API Library so that development of the Ingestionstream Service could proceed
as planned. The Java API Library also required more development than anticipated. As such the creation
of a Python language library has been postponed indefinitely as initial Phase IIA project resources are
exhausted. Specifically, we chose development of the Advanced Data Sciences component of the project
over that of the Python library. Development of the Python library is essentially a self-contained task that
can proceed at any time now that the Data Platform APIs are mature.

2. Actual and/or Anticipated Problems
 2.1. Aggregator-Data Platform Integration
The original scope of the project includes full integration of the front-end Aggregator system with the Data
Platform archive management system of the MLDP. This activity was only partially completed due to
focus on the Osprey 250k and the Data Platform Communication Framework. Currently, the Aggregator
system does not directly communicate with the Ingestion Service using its gRPC interfaces. An
intermediate step is employed where the Aggregator creates formatted HDF5 files and stores them to disk,
rather than transmitting the collected data directly to the Ingestion Service. A separate standalone
application was built to read the HDF5 files, convert them to the Data Platform Communication Framework
format, then transmit the data to the Data Platform Ingestion Service.
The above application was built as an intermediate step toward integration but has independent merit, as it
can load legacy files into the Data Platform archive. It was built and installed at the SLAC facility where
the Data Platform is now deployed, and SLAC has significant amounts of legacy operations data stored
from several years of Aggregator operation. However, the intent now is to develop full integration in the
form of an EPICS Interface.

 137

 Figure 63: EPICS Interface integration of Aggregator and Data Platform

A preliminary design for the EPICS Interface is shown in Figure 63. The application, built in C++, would
naturally interface with EPICS using pvAccess and would use C++ gRPC for direct communications with
the Ingestion Service. The EPICS Interface depicted in Figure 63 is actually a general application for
translating pvAccess NTTable instances into the gRPC messages of the Ingestion Service, specifically, the
IngestDataRequest messages of the Data Platform Communications framework. Thus, any NTTable data
instance produced from any Data Provider within EPICS could be transmitted to the Ingestion Service, not
solely the Aggregator system. Additionally, ingestion metadata can be included in NTTable instances and
can also be supplied to the Data Platform archive along with time-series data. The EPICS Interface would
most likely be implemented as an EPICS application deployed within a facility EPICS environment [37].
However, it could also be deployed standalone within an EPICS Soft IOC [38], as depicted in Figure 63.
 2.2. Algorithm Plugin Framework
The SBIR Initial Phase II Project contains an Advanced Data Sciences component that includes real-time
access and fast processing of active ingestion datastreams. This feature is available to applications requiring
real-time operation (typically facility control and optimization algorithms). The original intent was to
develop a Core Service, the Ingestionstream Service, providing all ingestion datastream processing. An
“algorithm plugin” framework was also intended for the Ingestionstream Service where clients could fit
custom algorithms that would do fast processing of the ingestion stream data.
As mentioned above, the inability to include the algorithm plugin framework into the Ingestionstream
Service of the Data Platform was an unanticipated problem. During design studies at the start of the
Ingestionstream Service development, it was clear that support for the plugin framework within the Data
Platform Communications Framework was simply unfeasible. Providing a generalized framework for user
algorithm plugins is simply beyond the scope of gRPC communications used by the Data Platform
Communication Framework. The Ingestion Service does, however, provide direct access to live ingestion
datastream in the form of a Subscribe/Publish service. The Ingestionstream Service then uses Ingestion
Service ingestion datastream access to monitor it for client-defined “data events”, capturing and returning
any data associated with the event.
Early in Phase II, investigations were performed concerned the use of C++ and related techniques required
for fast ingestion stream processing. It is possible to development components in different languages, C++
offering the fastest processing but at the cost of added complexity. At the time we were considering
Ingestionstream Service “plugins” as a potential candidate for C++ implementation, which was carefully
documented [29]. Now we are also considering other technologies such as Emscripten [52] with
WebAssembly (Wasm) [53] and the Cling interpreter within the Root framework [54] for employing inline
compilation techniques for the Ingestionstream Service. However, the most plausible solution is to move
the algorithm plugin framework to a high-level environment, the most likely being the Java API Library
(JAL). We recognize that this is a significant project deviation, but the Java API Library is a more natural
setting for an algorithm plugin framework.

 138

 Figure 64: JAL algorithm plugin framework basic architecture

The JAL library contains an Application Framework for rapid development of JAL applications, and the
plugin framework is a natural extension of this application framework. The idea is to create a library of
exceptional algorithms, that is “plugins”, available to clients for real-time ingestion datastream processing.
A basic architecture for an algorithm plugin framework is shown in Figure 64, along with its relationship
to the Ingestionstream Service (compare this with Figure 22). Within Figure 64 we see a “plugin executor”
class, PluginExecutor, which is responsible for managing and executing user-defined algorithm plugins. A
real-time algorithm is represented by the class Plugin, which must implement the IPlugin interface for
recognition and execution by the PluginExecutor active class. The algorithm registers itself with the plugin
executor using the attach() method. Clients can subscribe to algorithm results by implementing the
interface IDataProd and using the subscribe() method. Note that multiple clients can subscribe to the data
product results of a single algorithm. Thus, multiple plugins can be executing with multiple clients receving
algorithm products.
 2.3. Load Testing and Horizontal Scaling
Although not specifically scoped within the project, load testing of the Data Platform is an important
activity yet to be performed. The objective here is to determine the maximum archive capacity of the Data
Platform; that is, under what conditions it “breaks.” Performance evaluations continued throughout the
project but did not stress the archive capacity. An advanced Data Simulator capable of producing a variety
of ingestion scenarios was completed and is available for load testing. It can be configured to supply
heterogeneous data from multiple, concurrent Data Providers at differing rates, different data types,
executed over extended time scales. However, as of this writing load testing has not begun.
Since the Data Platform is composed of collaborating service it is possible to launch multiple instances of
the same service supporting a “horizontal deployment.” The MongoDB database system can also be
deployed over multiple servers with archive “sharding” which both increases capacity and lookup speeds.
Thus, both Data Platform and MongoDB database system can be both be deployed over multiple servers
and specialized hardware thus supporting full horizontal deployment. As of this writing Osprey DCS has
just started investigation of horizontal deployment, specifically, for the Ingestion Service. Note that load
testing is the primary method of evaluating horizontal scaling of the Data Platform; and it would determine
optimal server configurations.

 139

 2.4. Python Language Library
The SBIR Initial Phase II Project specifies the development of a Python language library for high-level
interaction with the Data Platform. Python language support is important for data science applications as
most popular data science libraries are built in Python (e.g., see [3] [4] [5] [6]). However, as discussed in
the previous section the development of this library has been postponed indefinitely. During the project
resources were diverted to Data Platform performance improvements, unexpected effort required for the
Annotation Service, the data exporting feature, and additional efforts required for the Java API Library. In
the final project phase, it was decided to focus remaining resources toward development of the
Ingestionstream Service in support of the Advanced Data Sciences component. Although a recognizable
deviation from project scope, development of the Python library is a self-contained task and can be initiated
at any time when future resources are available.
A high-level Java language client library was developed and is available, the Java API Library (JAL). The
Java language library was implemented first because the Data Platform was also implemented in Java and
integration was the most straightforward; the gRPC-based Data Platform Communication Framework is
configured to build and install locally in the Java language. It was found during JAL development that
high-level language support for the Data Platform is non-trivial due to the gRPC communications
framework and the distributed processing between Data Platform services and the JAL client. For example,
the assembly of time-series request data from the Data Platform Query Service is a complex process which
is performed within the JAL Query Service API. Thus, the knowledge obtained from the Java API Library
development can be directly leveraged to create the Python library. The architecture and operation of the
JAL library was thus carefully documented in Chapter II, Subsection II.2.12. The Python library
architecture will likely follow that of the JAL library. Further, it was prudent to wait for the Data Platform
Communication Framework APIs to mature before developing an additional high-level client library.

3. Changes Requiring Significant Impact on Expenditure
No changes in project expenditures were incurred.

4. Significant Changes in Use of Human Subject, Animals, and/or
Biohazards
The project uses no animals or hazardous materials. Other than feedback and critiques from MLDP users,
there are no human subjects or experimentation.

5. Changes in Primary Performance Site Location
There were no changes in the primary site locations. However, some Aggregator development was
performed on site at the NASA Neil Armstrong Test Facility in Sandusky, Ohio.

6. Carryover Amount
The project is complete and there are no carryover amounts.

I. APPENDIX A: EVALUATIONS

  1. Technology Evaluations
  After Phase II it was determined that a complete redesign of the MLDP archiving system (i.e., the Data
  Platform) was necessary to achieve performance goals. The design studies initiated at the start of Phase II
  required systematic evaluations and benchmarking of existing technologies and methods. The focus of
  these efforts was based upon the following considerations for the archive design (in order of importance):
 1. Performance
 2. Modularity/dependency
 3. Development effort
 4. Ease of deployment
  A summary of the technology benchmarking results is shown in Table 5. The benchmarking contains 3
  categories distributed within the table rows:
 1) gRPC Network Transmission – Overall gRPC network rates without archiving using Java gRPC.
 2) Data Archiving – Time series data storage rates for specific archiving systems.
 3) Metadata Update –Archive metadata update rates.

 Data Rates
 Benchmark Description
 Double Values (vals/sec) Bytes (bytes/sec)
 gRPC network transmission (Java) 22M – 33M 176M – 264M
 Archiving, structured - HDF5 large 68M – 77M 544M – 616M
 Archiving, structured - JSON files 38M – 47M 304M – 376M
 Archiving, buckets - MongoDB 7M – 11M 56M – 88M
 Archiving, buckets - MariaDB 4.5M – 5.5M 36M – 44M
 Archiving, structured - HDF5 small 1.3M – 2.4M 10.4M – 19.2M

         Archiving, points - MongoDB                    360K – 410K                    2.88M – 3.28M
 Archiving, points - MariaDB 140K – 162K 1.12M – 1.3M
 Metadata updates - MongoDB 11K to 36K updates/sec -
 Table 5: Data Platform component benchmarking summary

  Table 5 is informative in that is isolates data archiving performance from data transmission performance.
  In particular, the archiving performance is reported for multiple data storage systems and techniques. Four
  disparate data archiving systems and techniques are considered:
 1) System File Archiving – Direct to disk archiving using JSON and HDF5 file formats.
 2) MongoDB – A formal NoSQL, document-based database system.

 -1-

 3) MariaDB – A formal relational database.
 4) InfluxDB – A formal database system tailored for time-series, measurement data.
From the table we see that file archiving with HDF5 format performs best overall when storing structured
data directly to disk.
In addition to the technology benchmarking a parallel effort was performed investigating the feasibility of
a Data Platform implementation in the C++ language. Such an approach yields executables in the host
platform’s native binary format. Presumably this condition yields the fastest performance available,
although at the cost of added development effort, host ecosystem, and deployment overhead. (The original
Datastore prototype was implemented in the Java language.)
For these evaluations a basic Ingestion Service was built in C++ utilizing gRPC as the communications
framework (as before). Figure 65 depicts a component-level schematic of the C++ testbed. The testbed
simulated the creation, transmission, and ingestion of large heterogeneous data tables over a local network.
The objective was to study a basic system focused solely on performance. A simple archiver scheme was
employed utilizing direct-to-disk binary storage, no formal database systems were employed. Several
configurations and transmission strategies were investigated.

 Figure 65: C++ Ingestion Service testbed

of data tables being ingested and for various transmission methods (e.g., synchronous, asynchronous, unary,
and streaming). Results are presented for both archiving and when archiving is turned off. The table
dimensions stress the carrying capacity of the data transport while the transmission methods are properties
of the gRPC system. When archiving is disabled the gRPC network transmission rates are isolated.

 Table 6: peak transmission and archiving rates in C++ implementation

Upon conclusion of the design studies a new course of action was decided. The Datastore system was to
be completely redesigned and rebuilt. Although operational, development of the previous prototype was to
be suspended. The new design is based upon the following systems:
 • Java – The Java programming language offers a reasonable tradeoff between performance,
 development effort, and ease of deployment. C++ provided the best performance but at a
 significant development increase, particularly so for gRPC development and installation. Java also
 provides a platform independent deployment solution.
 • gRPC – It was decided to maintain the Datastore as a standalone system independent of EPICS.
 The gRPC system provides a fast, platform-independent, and language neutral communication
 framework that is offers modularity and extensibility. The latter feature being particularly
 important for future Advanced Data Science efforts.
 • MongoDB – The MongoDB database system is now used for both time-series data storage and
 metadata storage. Previously it was utilized solely for metadata archiving due to its document-
 based storage structure. With redesign of the time-series data archive, the MongoDB system was
 found to have exceptional performance. Additionally, it is freeware and installs on most platforms.
 • HDF5 – The HDF5 library provided the fastest overall storage performance. Its “self-describing
 data file” format is compatible with the archiving of heterogenous, time-series data. However, it
 requires development overhead required for file management and organization.
Although C++ is not to be used for general Datastore development it was discovered that C++ gRPC
provides exceptional performance when utilizing asynchronous, streaming communications. Unfortunately,
this gRPC protocol is the most difficult to implement and deploy. However, due to the flexibility of a gRPC
communications framework it is possible to create specialized Data Platform components built in C++.
C++ and C++ gRPC are particularly attractive for the datastream processing addressed in Year 2; these are
real-time systems requiring exceptional performance characteristics. Thus, the implementation of

 -3-

asynchronous, streaming gRPC communications in C++ was carefully documented after the evaluations
[29].
We are not currently utilizing the HDF5 library but are reserving it as an option for specialized data storage.
Specifically, we are considering HDF5 as a companion storage system for legacy data. We anticipate that
the MongoDB database archive may eventually become overwhelmed when storing data over extended
periods. Thus, historical data can be moved to HDF5 disk storage when MongoDB nears capacity (such
size is yet unknown). If legacy data is queried by a Data Consumer it can then be “hot-swapped” back to
main storage.

2. Serialized Data Storage
All data going to and from the Data Platform requires serialization for data transport. We suspected that
archiving the serialized data directly might yield a significant performance benefit, as well as a significant
savings in storage space. As a further performance benefit archived data would not require re-serialization
for recovery by the Query Service. Note, however, that some unpacking of serialized data is always
required to identify sources, properties, and other metadata.
An investigation of serialized data storage within the MLDP archive was performed where the objective
was to partially unpack transmitted data only to the extend necessary to identify data sources, attributes,
and other metadata. The time-series data remains serialized and is archived in its serialized (and partially
compressed) form; specifically, as DataColumn gRPC messages. Result of the study are shown in Table 7.
The left column shows values for version 1.4 non-serialized archiving mechanism while the right column
shows results for the equivalent situation for version 1.5. All results are obtained from a Linux-based
development platform.
 Non-Serialized Data Archive Serialized Data Archive
 5,104,428.09 values/sec                               9,822,780.67 values/sec
 6,462,209.54 values/sec                               13,137,727.17 values/sec
 6,609,385.33 values/sec                               12,605,704.08 values/sec
 6,456,472.61 values/sec                               13,378,672.17 values/sec
 6,300,535.55 values/sec                               13,658,869.73 values/sec
 storage size: 738 MB storage size: 448 MB
 documents: 240 K documents: 240 K
 avg. document size: 13.3 kB avg. document size: 11.4 kB
 index size: 3.6 MB index size: 3.47 MB
 Table 7: serialized data storage preliminary results

The first 5 rows of Table 7 (the upper block) contains ingestion archiving rates for double values. They are
the results for the same archiving test performed 5 times. These results suggest an additional performance
factor 2 can be achieved with the new archive strategy, which was realized. The last 4 rows of Table 7 (the
lower block) contain MongoDB storage sizes for the two cases. We see a noteworthy savings in overall
storage space with serialized archiving, approximately 40% (from 738 MB to 448 MB).
Incorporating serialized data archiving also makes storage heterogeneous data considerably simpler. Since
the time-series data is never unpacked no specialized processing (according to data type) is required.
Confirmations for complex data types (e.g., images, arrays, structures) have been demonstrated where
archived data is successfully recovered by the Query Service.
 2.1. Serialized Archiving and Multiple, Concurrent Ingestion Streams
Performance of the Data Platform Ingestion Service was evaluated against various configurations of gRPC
data streams connecting the redesigned Java API Library to the Ingestion Service. The gRPC streaming
transport for the new Java API library Ingestion Interface is based upon a thread pool where each thread

 -4-

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

maintains a separate gRPC data stream for transmission. Each thread task runs concurrently, continuously
polling a payload message buffer for available IngestDataRequest gRPC messages and transmitting them
when acquired. When no more messages are available the thread tasks complete shutting itself down.
The tests described below are essentially an evaluation of the IngestionChannel component within Figure
54. All evaluations were performed on a single MacMini platform with Apple M2 Pro processor containing
10 cores, 16 Gbytes memory, and ½ TByte SSD drive. The platform hosted both the Ingestion Service and
all test fixtures and services.
Test Description
A standard payload of IngestDataRequest messages was transmitted to the Ingestion Service using differing
configurations of gRPC data streams. The payload configuration is shown in Table 8 where the allocation
refers to the Protocol Buffers serialized memory allocation. The message payload was stored in an
autonomous message buffer and available to message consumers (i.e., thread tasks) as fast as they requested.

 Message Count 60
 Avg. Message Allocation 2.75 Mbytes
 Total Payload Allocation 165 Mbytes
 Table 8: IngestDataRequest transmission payload configuration

All proceeding data streaming results use the standard payload within Table 8. Note that all messages
within each test are unique so that they are not rejected by the Ingestion Service.
During the testing there were from 1 to 8 concurrent ingestion streams transmitting messages to the
Ingestion Service and 2 possible gRPC data stream types: 1) a unidirectional forward stream from the Java
API library and 2) a bidirectional data stream where the client library transmits data and receives a response
for each data message1. In all cases a single gRPC Channel object supported all ingestion data streams.
Note that the tabulated results below contain multiple cases run for the same configuration to demonstrate
any variability.
The same performance tests were performed for two release versions of the Data Platform, version 1.3 and
version 1.5. The data archiving approach and technique was fundamentally redesigned in version 1.5. This
action led to significant performance improvements as seen below.
In the later release version, data is archived in compressed “data buckets” and special database indexes are
maintained for data retrieval. Thus, ingested data is never unpacked from its transmitted format (i.e.,
Protocol Buffers) and the Ingestion Service simply uses attributes within transmitted messages to identify
the contents for indexing.
Transmission rates are computed simply by dividing the total payload size (165 Mbytes) by the duration
required to transmit all messages. This duration is bracketed by the instant at which the thread pool activates
and the completion instant where all streaming thread tasks complete and the thread pool terminates.
Test Results
Table 9 presents the results from the evaluations of the Ingestion Service version 1.32. The table contains
results from 4 separate evaluations using the same testbed, the columns labeled “Case n” in the table. This
is done to demonstrate any inherent performance variability within the system.

Like version 1.3, from Table 10 we noticeable variation in transmission rates between cases. In some
situations, this variation is significant, up to a factor 2 (see Unidirectional streaming for 7 and 8 streams).
It is currently unknown why such significant variations now occur.
More importantly the new archiving technique of version 1.5 shows considerable performance
improvements. Comparing with version 1.3 we see a factor 2 for a single ingestion stream to over a factor
5 for multiple streams. It is now possible to support data ingestion rates of over 500 Mbps, well beyond
our original project goal (i.e., 32 Mbps).
Also demonstrated in Table 10 is that using 3 or more concurrent ingestion streams offers increased
performance (although with significant variability). This is unlike version 1.3 where there is little benefit
from using more than 2 ingestion streams. Using 4 bidirectional ingestion streams typically yields
transmission rates of 700 Mbps or greater while using 5 unidirectional ingestion streams can provide rates
up to 800 Mbps (although with significant variability).
 2.2. Conclusions
The redesign of the archiving system within the Data Platform version 1.5 provides a significant
performance enhance beyond the original. The Data Platform is now capable of data ingestion at rates more
than 500 Mbps. However, to achieve the maximum performance gains additional concurrent data stream
are required, at least 4. Beyond 5 concurrent data streams, either unidirectional or bidirectional, there seems
to be no additional performance gains in version 1.5. At this time, it is unknown if the additional gRPC
resource requirements will affect overall performance; such consequences will likely appear in the load
testing to be performed soon.
For Data Platform version 1.3 performance typically falls off as the number of concurrent data streams
increases beyond 2. There is an interesting anomaly where at 5 concurrent data streams the performance
again reaches that of 2 data streams. This may be a harmonic condition of the test platform which has 10
cores.
It is worth noting that the loading of data transmission between the competing streaming thread tasks is
relatively equal. This condition indicates that there is no resource capture within the library and operation
is likely optimized.

3. Data Platform Ingestion Service Evaluations
Performance evaluations were performed on the redesigned Java API Library (JAL) Ingestion API in
concert with the Data Platform Ingestion Service. Evaluations were performed for both release 1.4 and
release 1.5 and greater.
The tests are essentially an evaluation of the Java API Library IngestionFrameProcessor component
depicted in Figure 54. All evaluations were performed on a single MacMini platform with Apple M2 Pro
processor containing 10 cores, 16 Gbytes memory, and ½ TByte SSD drive. The platform hosted both the
Ingestion Service and all test fixtures and services.
 3.1. IngestionFrame Processing
The Java API Library uses IngestionFrame instances as the unit of ingestion. An IngestionFrame is a library
class analogous to an EPICS NTTable. It contains heterogeneous data and various attributes and metadata
available for later query by clients. They are also unbounded in memory allocation. All data ingestion
within the client library is through IngestionFrame instances.
The gRPC framework defines a maximum message size that can be transmitted between a client and a
service. The default size is 4 Mbytes (.i.e., 222), however, larger messages sizes can be configured for
client-server connections3.
For data within an IngestionFrame to be transmitted to the Ingestion Service, the IngestionFrame must have
memory allocation less than the maximum gRPC limit or, if not, must be decomposed into composite
frames each satisfying the requirement. Thus, IngestionFrame processing is the following two-step process:
 1. Decomposition into IngestionFrame instances meeting gRPC limitation
 2. Conversion of IngestionFrame (data) into IngestDataRequest gRPC messages.
Note that this process occurs strictly within the Java Client API library; it is independent of Ingestion
Service operation or performance.
The processing of IngestionFrame instances within the client library can be performed either on a single
thread (i.e., the main thread) or concurrently on multiple threads. With multi-threaded processing 2
independent thread pools are maintained: 1) one for decomposing IngestionFrame instances (if required),
and 2) one for converting IngestionFrame instances into IngestDataRequest gRPC messages for
transmission to the Ingestion Service. Each thread pool contains multiple, independent threads tasks to
perform its designated operation (e.g., decomposition or conversion).
A queue buffer is available for clients to submit IngestionFrame instances without blocking.
Decomposition thread tasks acquire these instances, process them, then deposit the allocation-bound frames
into an intermediate queue buffer available to the conversion thread tasks. Conversion thread tasks convert
the processed IngestionFrame instance (with the required memory allocation) into IngestDataRequest
messages, depositing the final products into a staging buffer for transmission to the Ingestion Service.
We note that within the Java client API library there is an option to provide ingestion throttling, or “back
pressure”, to the client. This optional can be used by clients to do their own performance tuning, or it can
be used to protect the Ingestion Service from an overactive Data Provider. The ingestion throttling is
implemented through an optional capacity-limited staging buffer. When the memory allocation within the
staging buffer grows beyond a given limiting value, the ingestion API refuses to accept additional
IngestionFrame instances until the staging buffer drops below capacity (i.e., IngestionFrame submission
blocks).

 3
 It was found earlier that gRPC performance tends to fall off for message sizes greater than 16 Mbytes.

 -8-

 3.2. Test Description
The point of this exercise is to determine optimal configurations for the IngestionFrame processing
operations. The following objectives are immediate:
 • Confirm that IngestionFrame processing is not the performance bottleneck with the client data
 stream to the Ingestion Service.
 o Ensure IngestionFrame instances are converted to IngestDataRequest messages faster than
 the transmission rate to the Ingestion Service.
 • Use as few resources as possible to achieve the above condition.
 o Ensure that thread resources used by IngestionFrame processing do not encroach on those
 needed by gRPC for optimal performance.
A standard IngestionFrame payload was used for all tests. The IngestionFrame instances contained time-
correlated tables of double-valued columns (all simulated data). Table 11 contains the payload parameters
for all evaluations performed in the sequel. The allocations within the table refer to memory allocation
after Java serialization.
 IngestionFrame Count 15
 Row count 1,000
 Column count 1,000
 Avg. frame allocation 14.4 Mbytes
 Total payload allocation 216.4 Mbytes
 Table 11: IngestionFrame processing payload configuration

We note that the processing of the above payload produced the IngestDataRequest payload described in
Table 8.
 3.3. Test Results
The results of the IngestionFrame processing evaluations are shown in Table 12. The results are grouped
into the following 3 general categories:
1. IngestionFrame allocation limitations: 0.4 Mbytes, 1 Mbytes, and 4 Mbytes
2. The number of processing threads: 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, and 12.
3. Processing rates: front end (IngestionFrame) and back-end (IngestDataRequest messages).
Notes:
1. The maximum allocations of 0.4 and 1 Mbytes are somewhat artificial in that gRPC has the default
 value of 4 Mbytes. However, these values are included as they demonstrate results for the additional
 processing.
2. The total number of thread task is the sum of the decomposition thread tasks and the frame-to-message
 conversion tasks, except for cases where all processing is done on the main thread (no concurrency).
 Note that the same number of processing threads are used for message decomposition and message
 conversion, this is an artifact of the Java client API and the test fixture.
3. Two different processing rates are provided to show performance at the front end where IngestionFrame
 instances are entering the Java client API, and at the back end where IngestDataRequest message are
 exiting the Java client API. An IngestDataRequest message created from a source IngestionFrame

The payload of 15 IngestionFrame instances, each with 14.4 Mbytes memory allocation, produces 60
IngestDataRequest messages after processing. Thus, each single incoming frame produces 4 gRPC
messages for this case. Note from Table 8 that each resulting IngestDataRequest message has serialized
allocation of 2.75 Mbytes, significantly less than the maximum allocation of the original IngestionFrame
and the gRPC limitation of 4 Mbytes. This “inefficiency” is difficult to avoid since it is not possible to
determine the allocation of the final IngestDataRequest message a priori (i.e., before processing), and
decomposition is done in integral fashion. The best we can do is to enforce size limitations on the incoming
IngestionFrame instances then optimize their decomposition (e.g., according to shape, size, data types, etc.).
For completeness, note that the maximum frame allocation of 0.4 Mbytes (i.e., 400 Kbytes) requires 540

 - 10 -

IngestDataRequest message to contain the entire payload, whereas a maximum frame allocation of 1 Mbyte
requires 225 messages.
 3.4. Conclusions
From Table 9 we can assume that the maximum ingestion rate for the Ingestion Service is around 150 Mbps
for version 1.3 and from Table 10 over 500 Mbps for version 1.5. Thus, to prevent data ingestion
bottlenecking from the Java client API we must process the incoming data faster than this rate, specifically,
as seen from the back end. Referring to Table 12, the use of 2 independent threads would suffice in all
cases (i.e., 1 thread for decomposition and 1 thread for conversion). However, the use of 4 independent
threads would provide a comfortable margin, processing the incoming data at roughly twice the rate at
which it is maximally transmitted. There the total thread count within the Java API library would be 7, 2
for the gRPC stream tasks, 4 for the processing tasks, and 1 for the main thread. Of course, Java and gRPC
would require additional threads for their own independent processing (e.g., thread pool management,
channel management, etc.). Determination of the final thread configurations will require additional testing
with full data throughput.

4. Data Platform Query Service Evaluations
 4.1. Test Description
The performance evaluations for the Data Platform Query Service and Java API Library were specific to
the recovery of time-series data requests only. No metadata or annotation requests are considered. The
time-series data were all double-valued scalars contained in the Data Platform test archive (described
below). The focus was raw query data operations where results sets are delivered as unprocessed “data
buckets”; these are gRPC streaming operations where recovered request data are not restricted by the gRPC
maximum message size limit (the default being 4 Mbyte).
Performance was evaluated against various configurations of gRPC data streams connecting the Java API
Library to the Query Service and various post processing techniques for raw recovery data (i.e., for
correlating the result set). Regarding the latter, the QueryResultProcessor class has a variety of
configuration options that can be set for processing of recovered data, discussed below. (The replacement
class QueryResponseAssembler of Release 1.8.0 retains all configuration parameters, plus additional
parameters for super-domain processing and sampled aggregate creation.)
Test Archive
The Data Platform deployment ships with the utility app-run-test-data-generator which is used to create
the Data Platform test data archive. Assuming an initially empty archive, after running this utility the Data
Platform archive will contain simulated, time-series data for 4,000 process variables all with names of the
form ‘dpTest_n’ where n is an index running from 1 to 4,000. The sample rate for the data is 1 kHz while
the total simulated acquisition time is 60 seconds. Thus, there are 240 million samples in the test archive,
all double valued. Thus, the test archive contains about 2 GBytes of raw data.
Test Requests
In the test cases below we consider the first 20 seconds of the test archive only. This was done to avoid
heap space errors and to reduce overall test suite execution times, which were on the order of 1/2 to 1 hour
due to the large data requests and the extensive case load.
Two time-series data requests were used in all performance evaluations. Each query requests half of the
reduced, 20 second test archive, although not the same half. Table 13 lists the test requests used in the
evaluations, along with their name used throughout the narrative.
 Request Name Data Sources Time Range Size (Mbytes)
 HALF_SRC              {dpTest_1, …, dpTest_2000}           [0, 19.999]                  444.098

 - 11 -

 HALF_RNG              {dpTest_1, …, dpTest_4000}           [0, 9.9995]                  444.109
 Table 13: time-series data requests used in evaluations

From Table 13 we see that the size of each request is over 100 times the size of the default gRPC message
size limit of 4 Mbytes, which was enforced in the evaluations. Thus, we expect at least 100 responses from
the Query Service for each request.
Test Fixture
The JAL Query API test fixture provided means for measuring request data recovery time, raw data
correlation time, and full data request recovery and assembly. The test fixture also provided performance
metrics for the Data Platform Query Service operation under differing operation conditions. Consequently,
the evaluations yield performance information regarding the various configuration parameters of the Java
API Library. We hope to therefore identify an optimal set for these library parameters.
The data transmission rates from the Query Service to the Java API Library were measured for each test
request using from 1 to 5 concurrent data streams and both unidirectional (BACKWARD) and bidirectional
(BIDIRECTION) gRPC data streams. Correlation data rates were also measured for each of the above
cases. Specifically, we compare data correlation rates against the data recovery configuration, specifically,
the request decomposition type and number of data streams.
There are 4 configurations for data correlation using the QueryResultProcessor component, that is, beyond
the use of multiple data streams. Correlation can be performed after request data recovery, during request
data recovery, and with or without the use of multiple processing threads in correlation operations.
Specifically, the RawDataCorrelator part within the QueryResultProcessor can be single-threaded or multi-
threaded; and it can have access to the common message recovery buffer after all data is recovered, or
during data recovery.

 4.2. Evaluations
It is important to note that all data rates presented below result from a single execution for a test fixture
configuration. When running the same configuration multiple times, we generally see different results,
sometimes better, sometimes worse. In general, the best results are seen for a “hot platform,” that is, when
the gRPC library has been active for some time. Thus, sometimes we see better data rates for what may be
considered more expensive operations where gRPC has been active for a longer duration.
Throughout the evaluation we have identified 200 MBps (Mega bytes per second) as a target rate for data
transmission. Result values exceeding that rate are highlighted in red within the summary tables.
