Upon inspection of Table 15 and Table 16 one conclusion is immediately evident, unidirectional gRPC data
streaming (i.e., the BACKWARD case) typically outperforms bidirectional streaming for the Query
Service; specifically, for all cases but the 2 and 3 streams VERTICAL decomposition cases this is true.
Thus, we would conclude that unidirectional streaming should be the default method of time-series request
recovery.
Regarding the request decomposition, the horizontal composition strategy generally outperforms the
vertical strategy for unidirectional gRPC data streams. When using bidirectional data streams vertical
decomposition is slightly better for most cases. Thus, if unidirectional streaming is the default horizontal
request decomposition should be used in conjunction.
Specific conclusions about the number of gRPC data streams used in request recovery is less deterministic.
It is apparent that multiple streams perform better than a single stream, but not always significantly.
Additionally, there is not a proportional increase in performance with the number of data streams. This
could be platform dependent; the current test platform having 10 CPU cores. Using between 3 and 4 data
streams seems to facilitate good transmission rates for most cases; say in the 150 Mbps range. Interestingly,
the best transmission rate, 247 Mbps, is for 2 streams using HORIZONTAL decomposition of the
HALF_RNG request; there are only 4 cases where transmission exceeds 200 Mbps. We shall see this
situation in later results. The worst rates, approximately 84 Mbps, always occur for single streams.
Another interesting feature of the data is that the type of request affects transmission rates. The
HALF_RNG request typically outperformance the HALF_SRC request. The HALF_RNG request has

 - 13 -

twice as many data sources (i.e., Process Variables) but only half the time range. Thus, queries over large
time ranges would seem to be more expensive than those with more data sources, even when the amount of
data is equivalent. These data do not consider correlation, so this factor is a property of the Query Service.
We also wish to point out that the above data rates appear low. We shall see faster rates when correlation
is combined with data streaming. This is likely due to the test fixture creating a “hot” gRPC library in those
cases.
For the sake of completeness, we also considered the recovery of an equivalent amount of data using unary
gRPC data transmission, which is size limited. Due to the availability of the time-series data request
decomposition utility DataRequestDecomposer it is possible to perform large requests using unary gRPC
even though their total recovered memory allocation is well beyond the maximum gRPC message size
restriction of unary gRPC (the default is 4 Mbytes). Thus, it is possible to measure the total recovery
duration for a large, but decomposed, test request and compare the overall transmission rate to that for
gRPC data streams.
 Request Request Count Data Rate (Mbps)
 HALF_RNG                 200                                          107.76
 Table 17: request data recovery transmission rates for unary gRPC operation

Table 17 presents the equivalent data transmission rate when decomposing the HALF_RNG test request
into 200 composite requests, the decomposition was done horizontally. Due to an unknown issue with the
Query Service and/or JAL Query API, it was not possible to recover the HALF_SRC request using a similar
scheme, the Query Service rejected the composite requests with a “message too large” exception (note that
composite requests should have a result allocation of approximately 2 Mbytes). This exception has since
been identified and corrected since these evaluations; it concerned the time interval boundary conditions of
composite requests.
Surprisingly the overall transmission rate for the 200 composite messages is comparable to the transmission
rates for a single gRPC data stream request recovery. This condition, considering the previous results,
indicated that recovery rates could be limited by query processing within the Query Service rather than
actual data transmission. Motivated by these findings, it was discovered that the Data Platform was
performing serialization and deserialization operations in gRPC data streams that could be eliminated with
the new archive design. The current Data Platform Release 1.9.0 now outperforms the above results by a
factor 2x to 3x.
Request Message Recovery
It is interesting to note the number of Protocol Buffers response messages returned (i.e., QueryDataResponse
messages) by the Query Service for each of the above test cases, for they are not equal. This inequality is
due to the composite request domain boundaries after the original request is decomposed; there are query
redundancies along the domain boundaries and the Query Service may return the same data buckets multiple
times (i.e., in different data streams for different composite request). This condition does not affect request
reconstruction as the redundant data are simply discarded in the correlation process. However, data
redundancy can affect performance.

that required to deliver all request data when all gRPC message sizes are at capacity. From Table 13 we
see the test requests have an allocation of 444 Mbytes. The (default) maximum gRPC message size used
in the evaluations is 4 Mbytes. Thus, it takes 111 response messages to deliver all request data when at
capacity and Table 18 shows tight variations around that value. (The values less than 111 are likely due to
the efficient serialization of Protocol Buffers.) The conclusion drawn from Table 18 is that data transfer
for horizontal request decomposition is extremely efficient.
 Request Stream Type Protocol Buffers Message Recovered
 1 stream 2 streams 3 streams 4 streams 5 streams
                   BACKWARD               109(444)       110(444)      120(489)        112(444)      110(444)
 HALF_SRC
                   BIDIRECTION            109(444)       110(444)      120(489)        112(444)      110(444)
                   BACKWARD               109(444)       110(444)      132(533)        132(444)      110(444)
 HALF_RNG
                   BIDIRECTION            109(444)       110(444)      132(533)        132(444)      110(444)
 Table 19: request messages (and Mbyte allocation) for VERTICAL decomposition

Table 19 lists the number of Protocol Buffers messages returned by the Query Service when vertical request
decomposition is applied (i.e., decomposition by time range). Here the results shown a significant increase
in the 3 and 4 gRPC data stream cases where 23 additional messages are recovered and up to 89 Mbytes of
additional data as compared to the single-stream case; this is a 21% increase in message count and memory
allocation. Referring to the transmission rates in Table 16, the additional data transmission will likely
represent a 20% overall degradation. This redundant data transmission is a likely explanation for the
reduced data rates seen in Table 16 for vertical decomposition (i.e., as compared to horizontal
decomposition). Transmission rates would be closer to the horizontal decomposition rates seen in Table
15 if the 20% degradation was not present.
The additional redundancy for the vertical decomposition is easily explained. In horizontal decomposition
each Process Variable should appear only once amongst the composite requests. However, in vertical
decomposition the time ranges are divided into closed intervals of the form [𝑡! , 𝑡" ], [𝑡" , 𝑡# ], … , [𝑡$%! , 𝑡$ ]
where adjacent intervals contain the same endpoint. All vertical composite requests contain the full set of
Process Variables. Thus, it is possible for data buckets containing common endpoints to be returned on
multiple data streams, and for all Process Variables.

 - 15 -

Request Data Correlation
We wish to determine data processing performance of the raw data recovered by a time-series data request;
specifically, we measure the performance of the RawDataCorrelator class for the data recovered in all test
cases. In the results of this subsection, data correlation is isolated from the request data recovery; that is,
the request data is recovered first, then correlated. The correlation processing times for each test case are
then presented.
The RawDataCorrelator class can concurrently process incoming data using multi-threading. It is possible
to align the time-series data within independent blocks (as RawCorrelatedData objects) managed by multiple
processing threads. The blocks are assembled as each response message is added to the correlator.
Processing is continuous and a fully correlated set is always available after each response message is added
(the processor blocks until the response message is fully processed). Thus, we present both situations,
where correlation concurrency is disabled and when enabled.
We stress that correlation must be done in an atomic fashion; the correlator blocks until a
QueryDataResponse.QueryData data message is fully processed, even though the correlation of message data
within the message can be done using multiple threads. Thus, even though response messages can be
recovered with multiple, concurrent gRPC data streams (a common data buffer being the point of collection),
the response messages are always correlated serially. Consequently, variation in the correlation rates see
below can be due to the order in which the response data is presented, as well as the type of request
performed. Note that when multiple gRPC data streams are employed the response message ordering is
subject to change upon each execution.
Single-Threaded Correlation
We first consider single-threaded correlation. When concurrency is disabled within a RawDataCorrelator
object, all processing is single threaded; that is, all aspects of data correlation are serial and done on the
main thread. As described above, in this situation for any given request the performance variation is strictly
due to the order of data presentation, which is in turn due to the decomposition of the original request.
 Request Stream Type Correlation Rate (Mbps)
 1 stream 2 streams 3 streams 4 streams 5 streams
                  BACKWARD              777.12        813.67        845.36        861.13         858.87
 HALF_SRC
                  BIDIRECTION           789.51        841.28        822.37        849.02         882.53
                  BACKWARD              446.34        433.78        431.96        409.37         423.31
 HALF_RNG
                  BIDIRECTION           431.36        437.52        440.10        413.09         443.85
 Table 20: request data single-thread correlation rates for HORIZONTAL decomposition

                  BACKWARD              438.11        667.37        587.68        504.69         716.90

 Table 21: request data single-thread correlation for VERTICAL decomposition

 - 16 -

Table 20 shows the correlation processing rates for horizontal decompositions of the time-series data
request while Table 21 shows that for vertical decompositions. Upon inspection we see that the correlator
strongly prefers vertical decomposition. That is, the correlator is better at correlating many data sources
over smaller time ranges rather than correlating smaller sets of data sources over large time ranges.
In contradiction to the above observation, the correlator prefers the HALF_SRC data request over the
HALF_RNG request. The HALF_SRC request has half as many data sources (~2,000) but with twice the
time range (~20 seconds). This could be a collective effect where smaller sets of data sources decomposed
by time range are more easily correlated into the data blocks. But again, the order of presentation is
significant here and this may be an artifact of how the Query Service returns the request data.
As expected, we see little difference in the method of data recovery, either by a unidirectional gRPC data
stream or a bidirectional data stream. In either case the request data is already available for processing. It
is also assumed to be returned in the same order and within QueryDataResponse messages containing the
same data, regardless of the data stream.
Referring to the previous section, the data recovery process seems to prefer horizontal decomposition while,
here, correlation appears to prefer vertical decomposition. Thus, when these two operations are combined,
we may see an averaging effect.
Multi-threaded Correlation
When using multiple threads, the correlator assigns a thread task to each data bucket within a response
messages. The maximum number of thread tasks is a parameter of the Java API Library and for all cases
we used 20, which would seem an absolute maximum choice on a 10-core platform. Each task attempts to
align the sampled data within the bucket to an existing data block. If the task fails it is assumed that a new
correlated data block is required and, thus, one is requested. Thus, there can be some inefficiency if the
same block is requested multiple times, which is not allowed. Additionally, all data bucket insertion tasks
that fail must be re-run after creating the new correlated data blocks. However, as seen in the measurements
presented below, the overall result is a performance gain.
 Request Stream Type Correlation Rate (Mbps)
 1 stream 2 streams 3 streams 4 streams 5 streams
                  BACKWARD              1,209.8       1,422.8      1,234.4       1,522.6        208.99
 HALF_SRC
                  BIDIRECTION           1,470.2       1,351.8      1,299.2       1,434.2        1,435.8
                  BACKWARD              1,044.9       865.74       1,012.9       880.99         995.93
 HALF_RNG
                  BIDIRECTION           1,014.2       997.01       986.52        961.48         964.21

 Table 23: request data multi-threaded correlation rates for VERTICAL decomposition

 - 17 -

Table 22 presents the correlation rates for horizontal decomposition while Table 23 presents these rates for
vertical decomposition. Unlike the single-threaded correlation, here we see a slight preference for
horizontal decomposition in general. However, the most notable observation is that concurrent processing
improves performance for most cases. Correlations rates are generally on the order of 1 Gbps and higher.
There are cases for vertical decomposition where performance dropped significantly for 3 or more gRPC
data streams, here rates are comparable to the singled-threaded cases (see interpretation below). Recall
from Table 19 that there was significant data redundancy in recovery for these cases. However, it appears
that employing multi-threaded correlation will create nothing but a benefit, we will not lose any
performance.
Since the rates for multi-threaded correlation are generally around 1 Gbps we do not expect any significant
processing cost within the full time-series data query operations, as compared to request recovery. Request
data recover rates in Table 15and Table 16 average around 150 Mbps, about an order of magnitude that for
correlation. It is important to note that all data was correlated after it was recovered; thus, there was no
competition for computing resources. We also wish to measure performance when correlation and
streaming is performed concurrently to see if any additional performance gains, or losses, are possible.
Identified in the debugging event logs were many “data bucket insertion task failure” events during some
correlation operations, presumably for the cases where performance dropped significantly (although it is
hard to identify exactly due to the test fixture). The “data bucket insertion tasks” are the concurrent tasks
where sampled data from the Query Service raw data buckets is aligned within correlated data blocks. If
such a task fails it must be repeated against multiple data blocks, creating additional blocks if necessary.
Again, it is assumed that these repeated task failures caused the correlation rates to drop significantly below
1 Gbps; this condition is likely due to the data redundancy seen in vertical decomposition and, perhaps, to
the order in which the recovered data is presented to the correlator (a general aspect of multi-threaded
processing). No data bucket insertion tasks failed during single-threaded correlation.
Recovery and Correlation
Here we consider total data rates for both request data recover and correlation processing. The Java API
Library facilitates concurrent request data recovery and correlation processing of the incoming data. When
considering both data request recovery and correlation there are additional configurations to consider. We
can enable/disable concurrency within the correlator itself, and we can enable/disable concurrency of
correlation with data recovery.
First, we consider the cases where data recovery and correlation are separate processes, that is, correlation
of incoming data is disabled during recovery. We would expect these cases to result in a combination of
those in Table 15, Table 16, Table 20, and Table 21 for singled-threaded correlation, then Table 22 and
Table 23 when correlation is multi-thread. Generally, this is true although recall that all test results are
simply test fixture execution samples that experience variation from run to run.
The unique situations within this section are when the correlation is allowed to commence before all request
data is recovered. There we wish to see if there is significant resource competition between the gRPC
library and the correlation process which is allowed to spawn up to 20 separate execution threads.
No Concurrency
First consider the case where no concurrency is utilized, other than potential use of concurrent gRPC data
streams for request data recovery. The correlation processing is done on a single thread and does not begin
until all request data is recovered. Specifically, all request data is first streamed back to the common
message buffer then correlated into time-aligned data sets. The results are shown in Table 24 for horizontal
decomposition and Table 25 for vertical decomposition.

is present. Although it is hard to identify the exact cause of this anomaly without extensive investigation,
it is most likely due to variations in the gRPC library performance. Note further that no data rates exceed
the target value of 200 MBps.

The results of Table 30 and Table 31 provide some intriguing observations. We see that the HALF_RNG
request performs better here. Additionally, the bidirectional gRPC data stream is generally performing
better. Thus, it is hard to draw absolute conclusions when comparing single-threaded and multi-threaded
processing of incoming request data; it is difficult to determine the better configuration. It is likely that
there is competition for computer resources between the gRPC library and the correlation processor here,
which could explain the increased performance in bidirectional streaming where each response message
requires a client acknowledgment. Again, the cases of between 2 to 4 unidirectional gRPC data streams
perform well, however, this time for the HALF_RNG request. The results here are not consistently over
200 Mbps as was for the single-threaded correlation cases.
Future studies would warrant limiting the number of allowable processing threads within the correlator.
All the cases treated here have a maximum allowable thread count of 20, however, the actual number of
active threads was not measured. If resource competition were an issue, limiting the correlator thread count
might improve performance for this situation.
Full Request Recovery
We would like to determine an automated strategy for recovering time-series data requests for the best
possible performance. To do so we attempt to identify a set of optimal configuration parameters for the
Java API Library, for the request decomposition technique, for the number of gRPC data streams, and for
the configuration of the correlation processor. A test fixture was designed to evaluate all possible parameter
combinations for each time-series data requests under consideration.
The Java API Library allows for automated request decomposition by maximum allowable data source
count, say PVSmax, and by maximum allowable time range duration, say Tmax. The values of PVSmax and
Tmax are configuration parameters specified in the Java API Library configuration file. (These values can
also be set directly as configuration parameters of the QueryRequestProcessor component.) This approach
is suitable whenever the client is cognizant of their typical time-series data requests. Clearly, however, the
method will not work for all cases. Thus, we incorporate the parameters PVSmax and Tmax into a more general
approach; the general automation strategy is based upon the maximum number of allowable gRPC data
streams, say parameter Nstr.

 - 22 -

The original data request is decomposed according to the maximum number of allowable gRPC data
streams Nstr. The strategy is essentially threefold, first attempting a decomposition using parameters PVSmax
and Tmax, then defaulting to a configuration where the number of gRPC data streams is less than Nstr.
 1. Assuming multiple data streams are allowed (i.e., Nstr>1) we first attempt to decompose the request
 according to a maximum number of allowable data sources PVSmax and maximum time range Tmax
 for each composite request. Thus, the total number of composite requests, say Ncmp, is determined
 by the parameters PVSmax and Tmax. The automation here prefers a HORIZONTAL decomposition
 but will utilize any format that satisfies the parameters PVSmax and Tmax, including a grid
 decomposition. The idea is to attempt an initial decomposition assuming that the typical client time-
 series data request is known and, thus, can be optimized in the large.
 2. If the above number of composite requests Ncmp is larger than the maximum number of allowable
 gRPC data streams Nstr then the automation attempts a HORIZONTAL request decomposition
 according to the number of gRPC streams. Specifically, the number of data sources within each
 composite request will be no larger than PVStot/Nstr, where PVStot is the total number of data sources
 within the original request. The time range in each composite request is the same as the original
 request. This decomposition succeeds if the number of data sources in the original request is greater
 than or equal to the maximum number of allowable data streams, that is PVStot ³ Nstr.
 3. If the above HORIZONTAL decomposition fails, then the default is a VERTICAL decomposition
 of the original request. Each composite request has the same data sources as the original request,
 but with a time range divided equally by the number of data streams.
With the original request decomposed according to the above, we must then determine the concurrency
configuration for the request processor: internal multi-threaded correlation enabled/disabled, and
concurrent correlation and streaming enabled/disabled. To determine an optimal set of parameters we
performed full recovery of the test requests with the following parameter sets:
 • Maximum number of data streams: {1, 2, 3, 4, 5}
 • Maximum composite request data source count: {500, 1,000, 2,000, 4,000}
 • Maximum composite request time range duration: {1s, 2s, 3s, 5s}
 • Multi-threaded correlation enabled: {false, true}
 • Correlation while streaming enabled: {false, true}
The above parameter values provide 320 configurations for each test request HALF_SRC and HALF_RNG,
for a total of 640 test cases. In all cases a unidirectional gRPC data stream was used.

                 true          true      216      201      244      16       9                7

 4

                 true          true      215      206      231      6        3                3

                 false         true      226      208      244      5        4                1
 5

                 true          true      225      204      282      9        3                6
 Table 37: configuration results for data rates > 200 Mbps, best run

Table 37 displays the cases only where the data rates are greater than 200 Mbps. The clustering is narrowed
to the 2-stream situation where multi-stream processing during data recovery has the best performance, as
expected. This suggests the default configuration for the correlation processor within the Java API Library
configuration file.
 4.3. Conclusions
The best achievable query data rates for the Java API Library Release 1.6.0 used in conjunction with Data
Platform Release 1.8.0 appear to over 200 MBps.
From the above results we can make some general recommendations concerning configurations and
parameter settings for time-series data requests. The following generalization concern both the Data
Platform Query Service and the Java API Library configuration (default parameter settings within the
configuration file):

 - 28 -

 • Favor unidirectional (backward) gRPC data streams (both DP and JAL).
 • Favor horizontal request decomposition by data column (JAL).
 • Use between 2 and 3 gRPC data streams for request data recovery (both DP and JAL).
 • Correlate recovered data as it is being streamed, i.e., during recovery (JAL).
 • Multi-threaded request data correlation usually increases performance, but not conclusive (JAL).
The use of multi-threaded correlation processing can yield the best performance. However, it was seen that
this is not consistent. It may be that the maximum thread count used for correlation processing, 20, was
too large. In future evaluations it would be useful to evaluate the multi-threaded correlator performance
against the maximum number of allowable threads or at least measure the number active threads during
correlation.
In addition, it is recommended that clients try to minimize the time range of the data request when possible;
generally, equivalently sized requests with smaller time ranges outperform those with large time ranges.
The correlator prefers horizontal decomposition. That is, it is better at processing multiple requests where
the data source count is high but the time range durations within the request are smaller.
In contradiction to the above observation, the correlator prefers the HALF_SRC data request over the
HALF_RNG request. The HALF_SRC request has half as many data sources (~2,000) but with twice the
time range (~20 seconds). This could be a collective effect where smaller sets of data sources decomposed
by time range are more easily correlated into the data blocks. But again, the order of presentation may be
significant here and could be an artifact of how the Query Service returns the request data. Additionally,
the correlation operation is typically an order of magnitude faster than the recovery operation; thus, focus
should generally be on data recovery rather than correlation, at least for the test cases considered.
From the above results it was suspected that internal query request processing within the Query Service
may have a limiting effect upon request data recovery, that is, beyond that of gRPC transmission. This was
suggested by the results obtained from the unary transmission case. Using multiple gRPC data streams
improves performance but seemingly not as fast as expected; the effect could be simply due to concurrent
processing of query requests with the Query Service, rather than concurrent transmission of the results.
Regarding Data Platform Release 1.9.0 it was found that this was indeed the case. A significant portion of
that release addressed the performance issue after which a factor 2x to 3x was seen using the bench marking
utilities for both the Ingestion Service and the Query Service. Comprehensive testing for the new Data
Platform release, such as that described in this subsection for the Query Service, has yet to be performed.
Multi-threaded correlation performed at least as well and, in most cases, significantly better than single-
threaded correlation. The correlated processed recovered request data at an average rate of ~1 Gbps, this
is compared to the average request data transmission rate of ~150 Mbps. Thus, correlated the request data
using concurrent processing post recovery appears the most promising, as it is safe and fast.
There was an anomaly in the Query Service unary time-series query request mechanism. The Query Service
performs an internal estimate of the result set size for requests, rejecting them if the estimated size is greater
than the maximum gRPC message size limit. We were unable to perform the HALF_RNG request with
either a horizontal or vertical decomposition of 200 composite requests which would limit the result size to
2 MBytes in both cases. This anomaly has since been identified and corrected; it concerned boundary
conditions for the time ranges of data requests.

 - 29 -

II. APPENDIX B: GRPC AND PROTOCOL BUFFERS
This appendix presents an overview of the gRPC Remote Procedure Call (RPC) network communications
framework and its preferred interface definition support technology Protocol Buffers. This is not a
comprehensive description; the focus is on the specific gRPC features employed by the Data Platform
communications framework. However, the coverage is broad enough to obtain a basic understanding of
the gRPC framework and use of the Protocol Buffers meta language. That is, readers should obtain a basic
understanding of direct gRPC communications with the Data Platform.
The gRPC framework is essentially platform independent, supports most popular programming languages,
and uses HTTP/2 as the underlying transport mechanism. Protocol Buffers, or “protobuf”, is the default
Interface Definition Language (IDL) used by gRPC. Both the remote procedure call interface and the data
messages that they exchange are defined using the protobuf “proto” meta-language. Structured data to be
transported by gRPC is defined in the proto language and standard programming language realizations are
generated using the protobuf compiler protoc. The proto meta-language is also capable of defining the
gRPC interfaces for both clients and services.
The gRPC online documentation contains further explanations [66]. A quick start guide for locally
installing gRPC and Protocol Buffers is provided here [67] (the link is specifically for the C++ language
but installs for all supported languages). Code examples can be found on Github [68], while Java language
code examples are found in a separate repository [69]. Additionally, the Java quick start guide is here [70].
For a more complete description of Protocol Buffers see the online documentation [71]. The online
documentation covers the proto meta language for defining communications messages and their service
interfaces [72]. The Protocol Buffers compiler, protoc, is available on Github [73] where it can be
downloaded, built, and installed (or by following the instructions in the gRPC quick start [67]). Note that
Java gRPC plugin for service interface code generation is not provided int the build distribution but can be
acquired separately here [74]. (Java builds can be performed by project management utilities that internally
manage code libraries which generally do not require local protobuf installations.) Protocol Buffers can
also be installed through various system environment management utilities such as homebrew or invoked
through standard project management utilities such as Maven [75] or Gradle [76].
Note that the Data Platform is implemented in the Java language, correspondingly all programming
language implementations presented in the main body are specifically for Java. However, this appendix
provides examples for the Python and C++ languages to cover Data Platform gRPC clients built in alternate
languages. As to be seen, gRPC realizations can vary greatly between languages, they are generally quite
different.

1. Protocol Buffers gRPC Implementation
From the interface and message definitions specified in the IDL “proto” files, Protocol Buffers generates
high-level language support for the gRPC library. This support generally appears as classes and resources
for both the client communication and the service communication; the exact form depends upon the high-
level programming language selected. However, we can identify the structural commonality of the
generated Protocol Buffers code, which is covered in this section.
 1.1. Clients
Protocol Buffers generates “communication stubs” for clients. The communication stubs contain the remote
procedure calls available for the service. There are different stub types supporting different
communications models. For example, a “blocking stub” implements all remote procedure calls as blocking
operations where explicit data message instances are exchanged. These stubs are generally used to
implement synchronous communication with the service. A “non-blocking” stub supports “streaming”
remote procedure calls, that is, those procedure with the stream keyword in their definitions. All operations
within a non-blocking stub are typically gRPC streaming operations where clients can send and/or received

 - 30 -

multiple messages during a single operation. That is, rather than direct message exchange, the operations
establish data streams between client and service. Although more complicated, non-blocking
communication stubs can be used to implement asynchronous, multi-threaded communications with the
service.
Clients implementing synchronous communications obtain a blocking communication stub. The
communication stub contains all the non-streaming remote procedure calls (i.e., those not containing the
stream Protocol Buffers keyword). Analogously, clients implementing asynchronous communications will
acquire a non-blocking communication stub (i.e., those containing the stream Protocol Buffers keyword).
The non-blocking stub contains all the streaming remote procedure calls. Details on communication stub
acquisition are covered later.
 1.2. Services
On the service side, Protocol Buffers provides a base class for receiving incoming messages and returning
the corresponding responses. The base class, referred to as the service implementation base, provides
function hooks for the service endpoints. Services override these functions to manage all incoming and
outgoing RPC messages. Thus, service implements a child of this base class for both synchronous and
asynchronous communications (C++ differs in asynchronous communications). Note that a single core
service may support multiple client connections.
In the Python gRPC framework only one implementation base is available, the service implements
asynchronous communication using the asyncio Python library. For Java gRPC implementation, again
only one implementation base is available. The implementation base is always configured for streaming
operations, and the service may choose an asynchronous implementation using multi-threading support
offered in the Java language SDK. The C++ gRPC framework, however, provides direct support for
asynchronous communication, two distinct implementation bases are available, one for each paradigm.
Here the asynchronous class is not typically used for inheritance, although it can be.
Client requests are passed to the stub, transmitted across the network through the gRPC framework, then
arrive at the implementation base where the message is recovered. The corresponding service response
follows a complementary path where it is delivered to the stub and recovered by the client as a remote
procedure call result. For the non-streaming case, the remote procedure call consists of a single request
followed by a single response; the call does not release until the full operation is completed. For the
streaming case, a data stream is opened between the client and the service. Data streams may be
bidirectional or unidirectional. Multiple requests and responses may be exchanged until the stream is
explicitly terminated. However, there are gRPC stream management protocols that must be respected,
along with the additional data stream requirements defined by the core service and the specific remote
procedure being invoked.
 1.3. gRPC Resources
The gRPC framework contains library resources required of both clients and services. Many resources are
language specific, for example, builder classes, interfaces for implementing data streams, and execution
queues for asynchronous communications. However, in all implementations clients require gRPC channels
for network connections and services require gRPC servers to host them.
To obtain a communication stub, a channel connection must be created by the client specifying the desired
service URL and port number. Each communication stub maintains a channel connection to the service;
the service may support multiple connections to multiple clients. Channels need not be unique among
communication stubs. Additionally, a single channel will support multiple data streams between a single
client stub and the connected service.
Finally, each service must be hosted within a gRPC server. Server instances are created on the host platform
and can be configured to support both synchronous and asynchronous communication. Individual service

 - 31 -

implementations attach to specific server ports on the host platform. The service provider can launch
services through the hosting server, then managing service start and shutdown operations.
 1.4. Streaming and Non-Streaming Operations
The gRPC framework provides support for both standard remote procedure calls and streaming remote
procedure calls. In standard remote procedure call, the procedure is invoked and the operation blocks until
completed, after which the response is returned. For a streaming gRPC operation, a remote procedure call
establishes a data stream between client and service, the operation does not block. The stream remains
open allowing client and service to transmit messages until explicitly terminated.
Within the proto meta language, streaming operations are explicitly identified with the stream modifier
within a remote procedure call definition. Either the argument, the response, or both may be marked with
the stream keyword. If both are marked, the remote procedure call establishes a bidirectional data stream
between client and service, otherwise a unidirectional stream is created.
Both non-streaming and streaming communications often use the same protobuf messages. However, the
implementations are quite different. Non-streaming (blocking) communication is straightforward and
simple in gRPC, with a direct exchange of protobuf messages. Streaming communication typically involves
the exchange of programming interfaces or iterators, which are used to establish the data stream. Streaming
communications can be significantly faster, especially when using multi-threading, albeit at added
complexity.
In most gRPC implementations, asynchronous communication is implemented with data streaming. That
is, gRPC generally provides direct support for multi-threading in the streaming case, discussed more below.
Thus, in gRPC, asynchronous communication implies streaming operations, although the converse is not
required (i.e., streams can be single threaded). Additionally, gRPC typically offers clients the ability to
assign separate execution thread to a blocking (non-streaming) remote procedure call, a hybrid type of
communication.
 1.5. Synchronous and Asynchronous Communication
The gRPC framework supports both synchronous and asynchronous communications between client and
service. Synchronous operations are essentially standard remote procedure calls supported by a single
execution thread. Asynchronous gRPC communication facilitate multi-threading of streaming remote
procedure calls. Threads can be assigned to client requests and service responses over a single data stream.
Thus, both client and service can spawn processing threads for message handling. Additionally, multiple
streams can be established with repeated procedure calls.
In the Java gRPC implementation synchronous communications are realized in the ServiceBlockingStub
communications stub. The ServiceFutureStub also implements synchronous communications; however,
the operations are non-blocking. Operations there allow clients to assign execution threads to the non-
blocking response (i.e., to be spawned when completed). The ServiceStub class supports full, asynchronous
communications. There, multiple execution threads would run independently and must be synchronized
externally by the client.
Full multi-threaded, asynchronous communications are available only for streaming remote procedure calls
in gRPC (those defined using the stream keyword). Thus, we often equivocate the two paradigms when
the context is clear. In Java gRPC, the service implementation base class is the asynchronous service
implementation, services choose to realize the communications however they wish. In C++ gRPC, there
are two separate service implementation base classes, one for each situation. In Python, the asyncio library
must be applied to the single service implementation base class for asynchronous communication.

2. The Procol Buffers Language
A gRPC communication framework can be defined in the Protocol Buffers proto meta programming
language [72]. It is a language and platform neutral system of specifying the supported remote procedure

 - 32 -

calls and their associated data messages. Here we demonstrate the basic syntax and grammar, while
focusing on the language features used by the Datastore communications framework. The explanation
proceeds by way of an example carried throughout the narrative.
Consider the proto code example shown in Figure 66; assume that the code is contained in file
CommonMessages.proto. The file contains the definition of a protobuf message, GenericMessage, containing
a named data set. The data set is realized as an aggregation of additional protobuf messages typed
GenericData. The data represented within a GenericData message consists of an alarm condition and a data
value. The data value may be typed as either Boolean, integer, long integer, or character string and is stored
as a union using the oneof proto keyword. The alarm condition has type GenericAlarm, which is an
enumeration of the constants NONE, MINOR, or MAJOR. Finally, the file also defines a response message
GenericResponse consisting of a single Boolean field acknowledge, indicating whether the data set has been
properly received and processed.
Referring to Figure 66 we see that protobuf messages are defined using the message protobuf keyword and
 syntax = "proto3";
 option java_multiple_files = true;
 option java_package = "com.ospreydcs.generic";

 /**
 * A generic gRPC data message sent by a client. Contains a named data set.
 * @param name name of dataset
 * @param data the message data
 */
 message GenericMessage {
 string name = 1; // data set name
 repeated GenericData data = 2; // data set
 }
 /** A data value instance within the message dataset. */
 message GenericData {
 GenericAlarm alarm = 10; // any alarm conditions
 oneof value { // defined union
 bool boolValue = 1; // Boolean value
 uint32 intValue = 2; // integer value
 uint64 longValue = 3; // long integer value
 string stringValue = 4; // string value
 }
 }
 /** Enumeration of all possible alarm conditions. */
 enum GenericAlarm {
 NONE = 0; // no alarms
 MINOR = 1; // minor alarm condition
 MAJOR = 2; // major alarm condition
 }
 /**
 * A generic response to the generic message.
 */
 message GenericResponse {
 bool  acknowledge = 1; // true if message received and acknowledged
 }

 Figure 66: proto example - CommonMessages.proto message and response definitions

enumerations are defined with the enum keyword. Note that every message field is defined by a name, a
type, and an integer identifier. Many proto native types are identified: bool, uint32, uint64, and string.
Fields representing aggregation (i.e., containing multiple instances) are defined with the repeated keyword
modifier. Note that there are also compiler directives in the file header, namely syntax and option. The
syntax specifier dictates that version 3 of the proto language is to be used. The option keyword supports a

 - 33 -

set of compiler options: the java_multiple_files option determines whether the compiler creates a unique
file for each protobuf message, while the java_package option allows one to specify the Java package name
containing all the defined message and enumerations. Finally, note that every field within a message is
assigned an unsigned integer index. Thus, the general syntax for message field definition is
 datatype fieldname = index;

where index is the field index value. The index value is used internally by protobuf when serializing the
message before transport.
 Figure 67 contains another code example defining a gRPC service interface, GenericService. Assume the
source code is contained in the file GenericService.proto. The code example contains compiler options
 syntax = “proto3”;
 option java_package = “com.ospreydcs.generic”;
 import “CommonMessages.proto”;

 /**
 * A service that accepts data sets in the form of GenericMessage’s and returns
 * responses as GenericResponse messages which acknowledge receipt.
 *
 * Data may be transmitted individually using the sendMessage() operations,
 * or it may be streamed using the streamMessage() RPC method.
 */
 service GenericService {
 rpc sendMessage(GenericMessage) returns (GenericResponse);
 rpc streamMessage(stream GenericMessage) returns (stream GenericResponse);
 }

 Figure 67: proto example - GenericService.proto service definition

seen in the previous example plus the additional directive include instructing the compiler to include the
contents of CommonMessages.proto. That is, the service interface GenericService requires all the messages
defined in CommonMessages.proto. Although the situation is somewhat artificial (the example is small
enough to be contained in a single file), it does illustrate the modularization of proto source files.
The example in Figure 67 defines a gRPC interface for a service which receives data sets contained within
GenericMessage protobuf messages. The interface contains two operations (i.e., remote procedure calls)
identified with the rpc keyword, sendMessage() and streamMessage(). Both operations take a
GenericMessage argument containing the data set and return a GenericResponse result containing the

 - 34 -

acknowledgement. However, operations are supported very differently within gRPC. The sendMessage()
operation is implemented as a standard remote procedure call accepting a single argument and returning a
single response. The streamMessage() operation establishes a forward stream of GenericMessage messages
as the request and a backward stream of GenericResponse messages as the response. The streaming
operation maintains a bidirectional data stream where multiple data sets may be transmitted until the stream
is explicitly terminated. As to be seen, the data stream is typically implemented with an exchange of
programming interfaces or iterators (i.e., depending upon the language support).
The entire example presented above (i.e., both source files) can be represented concisely with the single
UML diagram of Figure 68. The diagram also explicitly indicates the relationships between the protobuf
messages and the gRPC service interface. For reference, the diagram also contains notes describing the
proto language and the specific UML representation.

 Figure 68: proto example - UML schematic

3. Protocol Buffers Implementation
Once the “Generic Service” gRPC communications framework is defined in the proto language, the
Protocol Buffer compiler protoc is used to generate the programming language source code implementing
the framework. The specific programming language is identified using protoc compiler options. The

 - 35 -

protobuf compiler is generally invoked twice, once to generate the protobuf message source file(s), then
again to generate the gRPC service interface source file(s). The protoc generated source files then provide
endpoints for both client and service applications. All protobuf generated source code uses the gRPC
framework for message transport across computer networks.
 3.1. Command-Line Compiler
Protobuf compilation is a two-stage process when run from the command line. The protoc compiler must
be invoked to generate the protobuf messages, then again to generate the client and service gRPC interfaces.
Each invocation requires different compiler options and targets.
For protobuf message generation, the basic syntax for the command line Protocol Buffers compiler is given
by the following:
 protoc [OPTIONS] PROTO_FILES
where PROTO_FILES is a collection of the proto source file locations and the [OPTION] field is a list of
compiler options. The target language is specified as a compiler option. The following are the currently
supported language options:
 --cpp_out=OUT_DIR Generate C++ header and source.
 --csharp_out=OUT_DIR Generate C# source file.
 --java_out=OUT_DIR Generate Java source file.
 --kotlin_out=OUT_DIR Generate Kotlin file.
 --objc_out=OUT_DIR Generate Objective-C header and source.
 --php_out=OUT_DIR Generate PHP source file.
 --pyi_out=OUT_DIR Generate python pyi stub.
 --python_out=OUT_DIR Generate Python source file.
 --ruby_out=OUT_DIR Generate Ruby source file.

The parameter OUT_DIR is the directory receiving the generated language files, this directory must exist prior
to compilation (although any package subdirectories will be created). It is important to note that using one
of the above options will generate only the Protocol Buffers messages contained within the given proto
files collection (i.e., the data message identified with the message keyword). No gRPC interfaces are
generated (i.e., the gRPC resources for remote procedure calls defined with the service keyword).
The client and service gRPC interfaces are generated using the –-grpc_out=OUT_DIR compiler option; this
option must be used in conjunction with the --plugin=protoc-gen-grpc=PLUGIN_LOCATION option. Note
that in the latter option both the plugin name (i.e., protoc-gen-grpc) and its location are specified within
the option parameters. Thus, to generate the gRPC framework, the basic syntax for the protobuf compiler
is given as follows:
 protoc --grpc_out=OUT_DIR --plugin=protoc-gen-grpc=PLUGIN_LOCATION

 - 36 -

The executable gRPC plugin found at PLUGIN_LOCATION specifies the programming language used in the
generated source files. The parameter OUT_DIR is used as before.
To demonstrate the Protocol Buffers language
options, we compile the example code into three
different programming languages: Python, Java, and
C++. Assume first that our directory structure is that
as shown in Figure 69 where the proto source files
CommonMessages.proto and GenericService.proto
are contained in the directory Protobuf/proto.
Assume second that the gRPC system is installed
locally and the Protocol Buffers compiler and gRPC
plugins are on the local path (again, see [67] for local
gRPC installation instructions). As shown above,
compiling is a two-stage process; the protobuf
messages and gRPC interface resources must be
compiled separately. Figure 69: proto example - directory structure
 3.2. Python Language Compilation
To generate a Python implementation for the Protocol Buffers example, execute the following two
commands from the Protobuf/proto directory of Figure 69:
 protoc -I . --python_out=../python/src CommonMessages.proto
 protoc -I . --grpc_out=../python/src \
 --plugin=protoc-gen-grpc=`which grpc_python_plugin` \
 GenericService.proto
Note that the full path to the gRPC plugin is obtained using the UNIX command `which
grpc_python_plugin`; the full path specification is generally required to ensure proper operation. The -I .
option simply instructs the protoc compiler to look in the current directory for any referenced files, for
example, targes of include directives within source files. Once completed, the above commands will have
generated the following Python files:
 • CommonMessage_pb2.py: All protobuf message implementations (from CommonMessages.proto)

 • GenericService_pb2_grpc.py: The GenericService gRPC interface implementations.

Both files are in the Protobuf/python/src directory as instructed. The GenericService_pb2_grpc.py source
file contains the interface implementations and resources for both clients and services. The “pb2” seen in
the file names refers to the Python version of Protocol Buffers, not the protobuf languages version.
 3.3. Java Language Compilation
To generate the Java implementation for the Protocol Buffers example, execute the following commands
from the Protobuf/proto directory of Figure 69:
 protoc -I . --java_out=../java/src ./CommonMessages.proto
 protoc -I . --grpc-java_out=../java/src \
 --plugin=protoc-gen-grpc-java=`which protoc-gen-grpc-java` \
 GenericService.proto
Note that the Java language case is slightly different from the general case. Specifically, when generating
the gRPC interface resources, one must explicitly identify the Java language in the output option (--grpc-
java_out) and the gRPC plugin name identifier (protoc-gen-grpc-java).

The generated files are under the parent directory Protobuf/java/src as instructed in the command line.
The compiler creates new subdirectories within the parent for output, as dictated by the Java naming

 - 37 -

standard for class packages and directory structures. Recall that we explicitly defined a package enclosure
for all Java implementations using the proto compiler directive “option java_package =
com.ospreydcs.generic”. Thus, the generated files will be contained in the package directory
Protobuf/java/src/com/ospreydcs/generic.

The compiler directive “option java_multiple_files = true” is also included in the CommonMessages.proto
file. This command instructs the compiler to generate separate source files for each protobuf message.
Thus, the following files are contained in the Protobuf/java/src/com/ospreydcs/generic package
directory:
 • Protobuf message files:
 o message classes: GenericMessage.java, GenericData.java, GenericAlarm.java,
 GenericResponse.java

 o interface classes:  GenericMessageOrBuilder.java, GenericDataOrBuilder.java,
 GenericAlarmOrBuilder.java, GenericResponseOrBuilder.java

 o protobuf resources: CommonMessages.java
 • gRPC interface files:
 o GenericServiceGrpc.java

Here, the protobuf compiler generates two files for each protobuf message: a message class and a message
interface. The interfaces are required by the Java protobuf implementation and are discussed below.
Protobuf also generates an additional file, CommonMessages.java, containing protobuf resources associated
with the original source file. The gRPC interface implementation for GenericService are contained in
GenericServiceGrpc.java, the file contains resources required by both clients and services.

Note that the Protobuf/java directory within Figure 69 contains the file pom.xml and the additional directory
target. These entities are used for building the example with the Maven utility. In that case, the pom.xml
file contains the protobuf compiler settings and options, while the target directory will contain the
generated Java source files. Details of a Maven project build are beyond the scope.
 3.4. C++ Language Compilation
To implement the gRPC communications framework in C++, execute the following commands from the
Protobuf/proto directory:
 protoc -I . --cpp_out=../cpp/src CommonMessages.proto
 protoc -I . --grpc_out=../cpp/src \
 --plugin=protoc-gen-grpc=`which grpc_cpp_plugin` \
 GenericService.proto
Notice that the commands follow the same general format as for the Python case.
The first command generates the protobuf messages. The following two C++ files are generated:
 • CommonMessages.pb.h: The C++ header file containing message class definitions.

 • CommonMessages.pb.cc: The corresponding C++ source file containing message implementations.

Both files are contained in the Protobuf/cpp/src directory. As is standard for the C++ language, the class
and type definitions for the protobuf messages are contained in the *.h extension file, while the
implementations are in the *.cc extension file.
The second command generates the gRPC implementation of the GenericService interface. It requires the
local path availability of the Protocol Buffers plugin grpc_cpp_plugin for generating C++ gRPC interface

 - 38 -

implementations. Again, the UNIX command `which grpc_cpp_plugin` is used to obtain the full path
of the plugin.
The C++ gRPC interfaces are generated within the following files:
 • GenericService.grpc.pb.h: The C++ header file containing gRPC interface definitions.

 • GenericService.grpc.pb.cc: The corresponding C++ source file containing interface
 implementions.
These files are also located in the Protobuf/cpp/src directory. The file contain resources for both clients
and services.

 - 39 -

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

4. Python Language Implementation
 4.1. Messages
The Python implementation of the protobuf messages within Figure 66 is shown in Figure 70. The diagram
is overly complicated because it includes the method by which the message classes are created. The
message classes are listed at the top.

 Figure 70: proto example - CommonMessages.proto Python implementation

 The implemented message classes are contained within the CommonMessages_pb2 namespace, however,
there is no explicit code defining them. Clients using the Python implementation may proceed without
knowledge of the actual Python implementation, simply by treating the message classes as if they are
standard Python classes with attributes defined in Figure 68. Readers uninterested in the message class
implementation can skip to the next subsection.

 - 40 -

Upon inspection of the CommonMessages_pb2.py file one would find that there is no code definition of the
protobuf messages; that is, there are no Python class statements for the common messages. The protobuf
messages GenericMessage, GenericData, GenericAlarm, and GenericResponse are created using Python meta
classes and class builders. The protobuf messages appear as like-named Python classes within the
CommonMessages_pb2 module namespace.

The diagram attempts to capture the mechanism by which this is done. Within the CommonMessages.py file
one finds 2 global objects: 1) _sym_db (type google.protobuf.SymbolDatabase), the global database of
protobuf meta classes, and 2) DESCRIPTOR, a file descriptor (type
google.protobuf.descriptor.FileDescriptor) containing a serialized description of the
CommonMessages.proto file. As indicated in Figure 70, there are then several lines of code. One line creates
the DESCRIPTOR object. The subsequent lines instruct the Protocol Buffers internal builders to create the
protobuf message classes from the DESCRIPTOR object. Note that the protobuf message classes are created
in both the global namespace and the CommonMessages_pb2 module namespace.
 4.2. Clients
The protobuf generated client resources are depicted in Figure 71. The gRPC client communications stub
and service base class are contained in file GenericService_pb2.py. The communications stub is realized
with the GenericServiceStub class and provides the client endpoints for the GenericService interface. It
contains all remote procedure calls contained within the GenericService protobuf definition.
Referring to Figure 71, the communication stub contains two procedures, sendMessage() and
streamMessage(). The operations are actually implemented as attributes of types UnaryUnaryMultiCallable
and StreamStreamMultiCallable, respectively; due to the loose typing in Python the distinction is essentially
without a difference. Clients can refer to these attributes just as if they were operations in a formal interface.
Both can be invoked either synchronously or asynchronously as to be discussed below.
Communications stubs are available with from the GenericServiceStub constructor. Note that the
constructor requires an argument of type Channel. Thus, it is necessary to first construct an appropriate
channel object to create the network connection to the service. The type of communications, either
synchronous or asynchronous, is determined by the choice of channel object. Also, any security credentials
required by the service are included within the Channel object. Channel creation is discussed more below.
 4.3. Services
The service endpoints are contained in the base class GenericServiceServicer, shown in Figure 71. Service
implementations override this class to acquire the service endpoints and provide operations. To launch an
implemented service, a gRPC server instance is required (i.e., an grpc.Server object). Python Server
instances are created using the Python gRPC resources described below. An implemented service instance
is then assigned to the server using the static function add_GenericServiceServicer_to_server() contained
in the GenericService_pb2.py module.

 - 41 -

The service must inherit from the GenericServiceServicer base class and override the two procedures,

 Figure 71: proto example - GenericService.proto Python implementation

sendMessage() and streamMessage(). Note that the sendMessage() function takes a single GenericMessage
argument while the streamMessage() function takes an iterator of GenericMessage arguments. Respectively,
a single object of type GenericResponse is returned by operation sendMessage() and an iterator of
GenericMessage objects is returned by operation streamMessage(). This is the essential difference between
non-streaming and streaming remote procedure calls in Python. Note that each procedure also requires an
argument of type ServerContext which contains process parameters for the hosting server instance, these
are optional parameters offered to the server by the service.
To launch a service, an service instance must be created then registered with a gRPC server object (of base
type grpc.Server), as described above. As shown in Figure 71, the Server abstract class has methods for
assigning the service to a specific port with or without security credentials. The class also has methods for
starting and stopping the service.

 - 42 -

 4.4. gRPC Resources
Figure 72 is a diagram showing the additional gRPC resources required for channel and server creation in
Python. As shown in the figure, the Python package grpc contains the resources needed for synchronous
communications while package grpc.aio contains the analogous resources for asynchronous
communications.
Synchronous Communications
The package initialization file grpc/__init__.py (and, thus, in the grpc namespace) contains functions for
creating both secure and insecure synchronous channels and synchronous server instances. Channel
credentials, of type ChannelCredentials, are required to create channel objects. Recall that server
credentials are assigned by port using ServerCredentials instances. Both credential classes have
constructors for creating instances, the arguments passed to the constructors depend upon the type of
credentials being used. For all server creation a thread pool executor is required, all other arguments are
optional.
Asynchronous Communications
For asynchronous communications, analogous methods for channel and server creation are contained in the
grpc.aio namespace, although their location is distributed within the modules grpc/aio/_channel.py and
grpc/aio/_server.py, respectively. Again, credentials are required for channel creation. Note that all
channel and server objects have the same class identifier, Channel and Server, respectively. Thus, to switch
from synchronous to asynchronous communications clients and services import the desired namespace.
Additionally, for asynchronous communication one must also import the Python asyncio library and follow

 - 43 -

DE-SC0022583 (SBIR) PHASE IIA FINAL REPORT

the async Python syntax and convention. See the gRPC Python code examples for specifics of each
communications mechanism [68].

 Figure 72: proto example - Python gRPC channel and server resources
