5. Java Language Implementation
Compiling the example CommonMessages.proto in Java produces the implementation for the protobuf
messages shown in Figure 73. The diagram also includes relationships and associations between resources
to help identify roles and responsibilities. Additionally, notes are included to further identify resources and
their functions.
 5.1. Messages
Unlike the Python implementation, the protobuf messages are realized as concrete Java classes. The
diagram includes dependencies contained in the external package library com.google.protobuf to
demonstrate the protobuf mechanism. All protobuf generated message classes inherit from a common base
class GeneratedMessageV3, used by the internal gRPC mechanism to recognize message classes.
One Java class and one Java interface is generated for each protobuf message (i.e., a protobuf message data
structure). Seen in the diagram is the additional class CommonMessages which provides a static method for
obtaining the gRPC file descriptor. It is interesting to comment that if we had set the compiler option
java_multiple_files = false all resources defined in CommonMessages.proto would be implemented as
enclosed static classes of CommonMessages. Also differing from the Python case is that Java message classes
are immutable; once they are created, they cannot be modified. The message fields are implemented as
private attributes of the message class, which provides no setter methods. Thus, the requirement for
message builder classes in the Java implementation, as seen in Figure 73.
Message Builders
The immutability of the Java message classes necessitates the use of builder classes seen in Figure 73. Each
message class contains an enclosed class Builder. Builder instances can be generated with the newBuilder()
static methods within the enclosing message class. Message class attributes (protobuf message fields) are
configured with the builder setter methods. A message class instance is the created in toto by invoking the
build() method of the builder. Note that the setter methods for builders all return the builder this reference.
This is a programming convenience so that setter calls may be chained.
Message Interfaces
For each message class a Java interface is also created. The interface is always named by the protobuf
message identifier suffixed with “OrBuilder”; for example, GenericMessageOrBuilder and
GenericResponseOrBuilder are the interfaces for protobuf messages GenericMessage and GenericResponse,
respectively. The interface defines all the attribute getter methods for the message. Thus, the interface is
used to unpack a message class after transmission.
Both the protobuf message class and its corresponding builder expose the interface. Thus, message builders
can be inspected before message instance creation.

 - 45 -

 Figure 73: proto example - CommonMessages.proto Java implementation

Message Creation
From the GenericMessage class we see that aggregated message fields are implemented as ordered lists of
type java.util.List<>. The lists containing instances of the field type, in this case GenericData instances.

 - 46 -

In our example, the protobuf message data field is defined as the class attribute data_ with type
java.util.List<GenericData>.

From the builder class GenericMessage.Builder we see that individual GenericData instances may be
appended to the list using the addData() method or set by index using the setData() method. Alternatively,
the entire data set may be assigned atomically with the method addAllData() which accepts an iterator of
GenericData objects, of type Iterable<GenericData>. From the GenericMessageOrBuilder interface we see
that the data field elements may be retrieved individually by index with the getData() operation or
collectively using the getDataList() operation. The same basic mechanism is used for all aggregated
message fields in the Java gRPC implementation.
For union message fields (defined using the oneof keyword), the message field value is implemented as a
Java Object type. This condition is seen for with the attribute value_ of the GenericData class. Note that
Protocol Buffers creates the enclosed enumeration GenericData.ValueCase which lists all the fields of the
union. Here, the enumeration constants identify attribute types. The message class attribute type is
available through the interface operation GenericDataOrBuilder.getValueCase(). The attribute value is
assigned using a typed setter method of the builder class.
Finally, note that the com.google.protobuf package contains the message class Empty, which is used for
protobuf remote procedure call operations defined without arguments. In the Java protobuf implementation,
remote procedure calls always require arguments; instances of the Empty class can be used to explicitly
indicate the no argument condition. Note that the static method Empty.getDefaultInstance() can be used
to obtain the default Empty instance; this instance may be used for all zero-argument remote procedure calls.
 5.2. Clients
The Java implementation of GenericService.proto is shown in Figure 74. The diagram illustrates that the
class GenericServiceGrpc contains the entire gRPC interface, for both clients and services. This class
contains four enclosed static classes. The three classes GenericServiceBlockingStub,
GenericServiceFuturesStub, and GenericServiceStub are the gRPC communications stubs for clients. The
first communications stub provides clients blocking communications while the latter two offer non-
blocking client communications, although GenericServiceFuturesStub is somewhat of a hybrid. As seen
in the diagram all communications stubs are part of an inheritance hierarchy originating in package io.grpc.
The abstract class GenericServiceImplBase is provided for services to attach their endpoint operations,
discussed below.
Communications stubs of the desired type are available from the GenericServiceGrpc static methods
newBlockingStub(), newFutureStub(), and newStub(). Each method requires an instance of a Channel object
which establishes the network connection. Creation of Channel instances requires the use of a channel
builder object, in this case a ManagedChannelBuilder object as shown in Figure 74. A
ManagedChannelBuilder instance is available from the class static methods forAddress() and forTarget().
These methods provide builders for insecure channel connections (see Section 0 for information on builders
for secure channel). The channel builder also contains methods for channel configuration, such as timeout
limits and maximum message sizes (compression options are set on the Channel instance). Note that
channel instances should be released when no longer needed, using the shutdown() method. This operation
frees gRPC resources attached to the channel..

 - 47 -

 Figure 74: proto example - GenericService.proto Java implementation

Synchronous Communication
Standard (non-streaming) RPC operations within gRPC are implemented as blocking, synchronous
operations. Note that the streamMessage() operation of our example is available only within the streaming
communication stub GenericService.GenericServiceStub. The synchronous communications stub
GenericServiceBlockingStub contains only one method, sendMessage(). Its use is straightforward; it takes
a GenericMessage argument and returns a GenericResponse instance, blocking until complete. Non-
streaming, synchronous communications is quite simple in Java.
Callback Communication
The GenericServiceFuture communication stub may be used to implement callback communication. From
Figure 74 we see that the stub contains only the non-streaming sendMessage() operation. However, here
the remote procedure call does not block but, instead, returns a ListenableFuture<GenericResponse>
interface. This condition allows clients to directly wait for completion, set up a completion notification

 - 48 -

thread, or set up a result processing thread. Note that callback communications can be considered a hybrid
between synchronous communications and asynchronous communications.
The ListenableFuture<> interface is part of the Google concurrency library in the
com.google.common.util.concurrent package. The interface allows clients to either block on the get()
operation, or register an execution thread to spawn whenever the operation is completed. For the latter case,
the ListenableFuture<> interface provides the operation addListener(Runnable, Executor>.
Remote procedure call responses can be recovered from the ListenableFuture<>.get() blocking operation.
The client can implement independent response threads using the addListener() operation. Thus, the
callback communications stub offers a limited form of asynchronous communication.
Asynchronous Communication
Streaming operations are available in the GenericServiceStub communication stub. In fact, all operations
within the GenericService interface are available within this stub. However, here even the non-streaming
operation requires the creation of a backward data stream from service to the client.
The Java gRPC implementation offers clients asynchronous communications only for streaming operations.
Streaming communications always involve the exchange of StreamObserver<> interfaces to establish the
data stream. Streaming remote procedure call invocations do not block, they exchange interfaces. Data is
transmitted only a non-streaming argument.
Referring to the GenericServiceStub class, even the non-streaming operation sendMessage() returns a
StreamObserver<> interface which is used to obtain the GenericResponse message response. For the
streaming operation streamMessage(), the client must implement a class exposing the
StreamObserver<GenericResponse> interface, used as the argument. The interface implementation must
properly realize the onNext(GenericResponse) and onCompleted() operations shown in the Figure 74.
In response to the streaming remote procedure call streamData(), the client receives a
StreamObserver<GenericMessage> interface, which is used to send data messages to the service with the
onNext(GenericMessage) operation. Once all messages have been transmitted, the client signals completion
by invoking the onCompleted() operation on the returned interface. The service should then respond in kind
by invoking onCompleted() on the client’s StreamObserver<GenericResponse> interface. This terminates
the data stream.
As shown in Figure 74 the package io.grpc.stub contains class implementations of the StreamObserver<>
interface offering additional stream flow control. Clients may choose to implement their stream interfaces
inheriting from a base class (e.g., CallStreamObserver<> or ClientCallStreamObserver<>). Clients can also
cast StreamObserver<> interfaces received from the service to an appropriate type (e.g.,
CallStreamObserver<> or ServerCallStreamObserver<>). Note that these base classes offer the ability to set
event handlers (i.e., Java Runnable instances) to be invoked whenever the service signals a ready event.
This is a convenience when implementing asynchronous, multi-threaded communication.
 5.3. Services
Within Figure 74, the GenericServiceImplBase is the service base implementation class. It provides the
remote procedure call endpoints required by service implementations. In the Java gRPC realization there
is only one type of service base implementation, it supports all service endpoints as streaming operations.
Internally, gRPC provides an asynchronous implementation. Services may choose to implement their
operations synchronously or asynchronous. However, the communications model is essentially dictated by
the client when accessing the communications stub.
Referring to GenericServiceImplBase, there are 2 services endpoints, 1) sendMessage() for the non-
streaming RPC operation, and 2) streamMessage() the streaming RPC operation. In the non-streaming
case, the sendMessage() accepts two arguments, the message itself (of type GenericMessage), and the
interface StreamObserver<GenericResponse>. The latter argument is used to send responses to the client.

 - 49 -

In the streaming operation streamMessage(), a StreamObserver<GenericResponse> is passed in and the
operation returns an interface StreamObserver<GenericMessage>. The procedure argument is, again, the
client interface to send service responses. The StreamObserver<GenericMessage> interface returned by the
service must be properly implemented to accept GenericMessage instances using the
onNext(GenericMessage) operation. The client and service coordinate the stream completion using their
respective onCompleted() operations within the exchanged interfaces. Any errors encountered during the
data streaming on either side can be signaled using the onError() operation on either interface.
Note that Figure 75 provides a more detailed description of the GenericServiceImplBase base class for
service implementation. In actuality, the base class method requirements are dictated by the enclosed
interface AsyncService. (As previously mentioned, all service implementations are essentially
asynchronous in the Java gRPC implementation.) The base class is also required to implement the
io.grpc.BindableService interface, which is done within the generated Java code. The latter interface
identifies the service implementation as a gRPC service which can be launched with gRPC server instances,
to be discussed below.
 5.4. gRPC Resources
Figure 75 lists some common gRPC resources required for both clients and services. The package io.grpc
contains resources and methods for creating gRPC channels, gRPC servers, and security credentials. The
utility class io.grpc.Grpc contains static methods that create builders for secure channels and servers.
Security credentials are identified by class type and created with class constructors.
Channels
Network channel objects are created using channel builders. Thus, clients must first obtain a channel
builder, which configures and then creates a channel. In Java gRPC, object of type ManagedChannel are
used. Channel builders of type ManagedChannelBuilder are required to create these channels.
Channel builders producing insecure network channels are available from the ManagedChannelBuilder class.
The class has static methods forAddress() and forTarget() which create ManagedChannelBuilder instances;
the methods are identified in Figure 74. However, builder instances created by these methods will generate
only insecure network channels.
To create secure network channels, a ManagedChannelBuilder instance must be obtained from the
io.grpc.Grpc utility class. Channel builder objectsare created with the Grpc.newChannelBuilder() methods.
They will be attached to the host address provided in the arguments. A ChannelCredentials object must
also be provided, it establishes the security credentials for all subsequently created channels. The
ChannelCredentials class is subtyped to identify the specific credentials used. For example, the type
TlsSecurityCredentials is explicitly shown in Figure 75 to identify Transport Layer Security (TLS)
credentials. Once a secure ManagedChannelBuilder instance is obtained it functions as before, but produces
only secure channels with the credentials provided.

 - 50 -

 Figure 75: proto example - GenericService.proto required gRPC resources

Servers
Figure 75 lists essential resources required for service launch in the Java gRPC implementation. A gRPC
server instance is required to launch a service. Server instances, of type io.grpc.Server, are obtained by
server builder objects available in the io.gprc Java package. Once a Server object is configured and created,
the service is assigned to a specific port address. The Server class provides methods for starting and
stopping the attached service. The process is clarified with the help of Figure 75.
Server builder objects, of type io.grpc.ServerBuilder, are created with the
Grpc.newServerBuilderForPort() static method. The method requires both a port address and security
credentials for the servers to be build, which may be of type InsecureServerCredentials (not shown)
indicating no security. Note that the ServerBuilder class also contains a static method for creating insecure

 - 51 -

ServerBuilder instances, analogous to the ManagedChannelBuilder class. After the ServerBuilder instance
is obtained additional server configuration is possible, including timeout limits, server interceptors, and
message size limits. The service instance must be attached to the server builder before the gRPC server is
created. Once the service instance is attached to the builder, a hosting Server object may be instantiated
with the build() method.
Credentials
All security credentials for channels and servers inherit from the abstract base classes ChannelCredentials
and ServerCredentials, respectively. The specific types of credentials are determined by subclasses. For
example, the Transport Layer Security (TLS) protocol for a channel is realized with the class
TlsChannelCredentials, as indicated in the Figure 75.

Security credential classes generally require builders to instantiate credential instances. Builders are
implemented as enclosed static classes within the credentials class. Then builder instances are available
from static methods within the credentials class.
Whenever security is applied to communications, both channel and server must have compatible credentials.

6. C++ Language Implementation
The C++ gRPC implementations for the example in CommonMessages.proto and GenericService.proto are
shown in the UML diagrams of Figure 76, Figure 77, and Figure 78. The diagrams are not exhaustive, or
precise. The C++ gRPC implementation is complex due to the low-level nature of C++ many of the precise
language details are not presented in the diagrams, only the essential aspects are given. For example, most
times variable types are precisely listed, but their character is not, specifically, whether the variable is a
pointer, a reference, a pointer reference, etc. Classified variables are also often neglected, such as those
defined in the C++ standard library std (e.g., std::unique_ptr<>, std::shared_ptr<>, etc.). Many times,
the variable character can be inferred by context, however, it is best to consult the C++ source code. The
diagrams are meant to convey the basic features and essential requirements within the C++ implementation.
Use of C++ gRPC and Protocol Buffers is significantly more complex than that for Java and Python.
Additionally, the local ecosystem support is complex, requiring the installation of multiple dependencies
which are typically managed with the pkg-config utility when building C++ gRPC systems. This section
covers only the basics of C++ gRPC and Protocol Buffers. For a more comprehensive treatment see ref.
[29].
 6.1. Messages
The C++ implementation of the CommonMessages.proto example of Figure 66 is contained in files
CommonMessages.pb.h and CommonMessages.pb.cc. The classes and types contained in these files (primarily
the header file) are shown in Figure 76. Note that all protobuf messages are implemented as C++ classes
with the same name, and all inherit from the common base class google::protobuf::Message. The
GenericAlarm enumeration is implemented as a C++ enumeration but inherits from the primitive type int
and, thus, can be treated as an integer value.
The field attributes of message classes are private. As seen in Figure 76, their implementation is not
straightforward, however, attribute values are generally accessed using class “getter” and “setter” functions.
The attributes of a message class are always implemented as single enclosed data structure of type Impl_
and name _impl_ (specifically, an anonymous union named _impl_). The Impl_ data structure contains the
required message fields plus one additional parameter, the size of the message data which is convenient
when monitoring transmission. The _impl_ attribute is private but the enclosing class supplies accessor
methods for getting and setting the attribute values.
Aggregated message fields, such as the field data in GenericMessage, are implemented with the
google::protobuf::RepeatedPtrField<Element> template class. (Note that string fields are implemented
with google::protobuf::internal::ArenaStringPointer class rather than C++ string pointers.) The

 - 52 -

RepeatedPtrField<> class provides functions for adding message instances to the field, in this case adding
GenericData instances to the data field. It also provides functions to retrieve field values from populated
message instances.
Referring to the message GenericData, note that message field unions (i.e., fields defined with the oneof

 Figure 76: proto example - CommonMessages.proto C++ implementation

modifier) are implemented as unions within the Impl_ data structure. The GenericData class also contains
an enclosed enumeration ValueUnion which enumerates all union fields, in this case the possible types of
the value_ attribute. The type of the value_ attribute is available with the value_case() function.

 - 53 -

All message classes contain constructors, including a default constructor, that may be used to instantiate
message instances. For tighter control of memory resources, messages may also be constructed within a
memory heap “arena,” of type google::protobuf::Arena. Instances of RepeatedPtrField may also be
created with an arena.
 6.2. Clients
Client resources for the C++ gRPC implementation are shown in Figure 77. Client gRPC resources are
defined in header file GenericService.pb.grpc.h and implemented in source file
GenericService.pb.grpc.cc. The diagram also lists many of the gRPC resources generally required by
clients to establish network connections and perform RPC operations.
In Figure 77 we see the protobuf generated class GenericService realizes the gRPC interface defined in
GenericService.proto. Highlighted in the diagram is the single static function NewStub() which creates the
client communications stub. The stub itself is defined as the enclosed static class GenericService::Stub.
The class GenericService also encloses the implementation base classes for services (shown in Figure 78).
The rest of the diagram contains the gRPC resources necessary to support the communications stub
connection and operations.
Unlike the Java gRPC implementation, the C++ implementation has only one type of communication stub,
GenericService::Stub. Figure 77 uses a short-hand notation assigning template parameters W and R to the
Stub class where, in fact, there are none. This is done simply for brevity. The template parameters are
expanded with the bindings W -> GenericMessage and R -> GenericResponse shown in the diagram.
Template parameter W indicates message types written by the client and template parameter R indicates
message types read by the client.
The communications stub contains four operations, two for synchronous communications with the service,
and two for asynchronous communications. Unlike the previous implementations, both the non-streaming
operation sendMessage() and the streaming operation streamMessage() are explicitly available for both
synchronous and asynchronous communications. The asynchronous operations are always identified with
the prefix Async prepended to their original identifier, in this case AsyncsendMessage and
AsyncstreamMessage.

All functions within the Stub class require an argument of type grpc::ClientContext. A ClientContext
instance simply allows the client to assign additional connection parameters to a given operation, such as
timeout limits, credential, and/or compression. ClientContext instances can be created from class
constructors, including a default constructor. If no special connection requirements are necessary, an empty
ClientContext instance may be offered (i.e., one from the default constructor). ClientContext instances
may be used repeatedly.
Channels
To create an instance of the communication stub a client must first create the channel connection,
represented by the class grpc::Channel. As shown in the Figure 77, static functions for creating channel
instances are identified in the C++ header file create_channel.h within the grpc directory. The file contains
multiple functions for creating secure channels with various types of security credentials, only the Secure
Socket Layer (SSL) credentialing is explicitly shown in Figure 77. Functions for creating the various
supported channel credential instances, of base type ChannelCredentials, are found in file credentials.h.
Note that the grpc namespace contains the ChannelArguments class which can be used to set various channel
properties, such as compression, timeout limits, and maximum message sizes.

 - 54 -

 Figure 77: proto example - GenericService.proto client C++ implementation

Synchronous Communication
The Stub class contains both the non-streaming sendMessage() operation and the streaming
streamMessage() operation. Note that a ClientContext instance is always passed to any stub operation; this
argument is used to add any optional conditions to the specific procedure call (e.g., compression, credentials,
timeouts, etc.).
For the non-streaming synchronous remote procedure call sendMessage(), a GenericMessage argument (i.e.,
a C++ reference) is passed in along with a C++ pointer reference for the GenericResponse message to be
received. The operation blocks and does not return until the response pointer is set. Once completed the
operation returns a grpc::Status instance containing the condition of the result (e.g., OK, aborted, failed,
etc.).
Synchronous bi-directional streaming is provided by the streamMessage() operation. The operation
requires only a ClientContext instance as the single argument. The operation does not block, it simply
establishes the data stream. To support the data stream, a grpc::ClientReaderWriter<W,R> template class
instance is returned (specifically a pointer). The ClientReaderWriter<W,R> class functions as an interface
to the service facilitating the data stream. It implements the grpc::ClientReaderWriterInterface<W,R>
interface bound by W -> GenericMessage and R -> GenericResponse. Thus, it allows the client to transmit

 - 55 -

(write) GenericMessage instances to the service while simultaneously receiving (reading) GenericResponse
instances as responses. Referring to Figure 77, clients perform the message passing with interface
operations Write(W) and Read(R*), respectively. From the diagram, note that these operations are exposed
in separate internal sub-interfaces using C++ multiple inheritance. The data stream is terminated when
sending the final message with the WriteLast() operation then invoking Finish() once the response is
received.
Asynchronous Communication
Asynchronous communications are essentially analogous to the synchronous communications described
above, but with some added complexity. From the diagram we see that asynchronous operations require
the addition of a grpc::CompletionQueue instance, which is used to monitor to the state of the asynchronous
connection. Note that CompletionQueue instances may be created as needed using class constructors. They
are passed as arguments to the asynchronous operations.
As before, the non-streaming AsyncsendMessage() operation requires an explicit GenericMessage instance
as an argument (along with a ClientContext and a CompletionQueue). In this case, however, the operation
does not block. Rather than setting a GenericResponse message pointer as with synchronous
communication, instead the operation immediately returns a special grpc::ClientResponseReader<R>
instance. Again, template parameter R is bound to GenericResponse. The client initiates the response stream
by calling the StartCall() operation. The Finish() operation is then used to acquire the actual
GenericResponse instance response. The client is free to implement a separate execution thread to recover
the result.
The streaming AsyncstreamMessage() operation requires no explicit message argument, only a
ClientContext, a CompletionQueue, and the special “tag” argument. Tags are used in C++ data streams to
identify messages, in this case the first response of the service. The operation does not block, it simply
establishes the bidirectional data stream. Accordingly, a grpc::AsyncClientReaderWriter<W,R>() instance
is returned, which is used to manage the asynchronous data stream in conjunction with the CompletionQueue
instance. The ClientAsyncReaderWriter<W,R> class operates analogously to the ClientReaderWriter<W,R>
class, having similar functions. Note, however, that functions within the ClientAsyncReaderWriter<W,R>
class contain the additional tag argument. As previously mentioned, this is a bookkeeping parameter used
to identify messages within the data stream and their corresponding response; typically, an integer value is
assigned.
Callback Communications
C++ gRPC callback communication resources are generally referred to as reactors. As the name implies,
they react to RPC actions and events, without blocking. Here, RPC activities are signaled to reactor virtual
functions, which implementations then override to capture the events. This condition contrasts with the
synchronous and asynchronous APIs where communications resources are used as provided.
Thus, development is different for callback communications, requiring direct inheritance from the reactor
communications resources. This holds for both client and service implementation. Recall that virtual
functions (abstract methods) are identified using italicized font within UML. Reactors have a simpler
external structure as compared with their synchronous and asynchronous counterparts. Internally, however,
they are more complex. Knowledge of reactor internal structure is not needed for development, however.
See ref. [29] for details on reactors.
 6.3. Services
The service side C++ gRPC implementation for the GenericService.proto example is shown in Figure 78.
The GenericService class contains the two enclosed classes: 1) GenericService::Service and 2)
GenericService::AsyncService. The first is an abstract class which must be subclassed to implement a
synchronous gRPC service, while the second is a concrete class implementing the asynchronous gRPC
service. The diagram explicitly indicates that the synchronous service is implemented with the service
subclass SyncServiceImpl; this is provided simply to demonstrate the requirement as noted in the diagram.

 - 56 -

Both Service and AsyncService are represented as template classes in the diagram, with template
parameters W and R. Again, this is notational short-hand as with the GenericService::Stub enclosed class.
However, in these cases the template roles are reversed. Template parameters are expanded with the
bindings W -> GenericResponse and R -> GenericMessage, as shown in Figure 78. That is, services are
writing GenericResponse instances and reading GenericMessage instances.
The service side implementation for gRPC communications is directly analogous to the client side, with the
message roles reversed. Note that all service operations require a grpc::ServerContext argument that can
be used to pass optional instructions to the grpc::Server instance hosting the service (the Server class is
described below). ServerContext instance can be created from class constructors and instances can be used
repeatedly within service operations.
Synchronous Communication
Synchronous communications are implemented by inheriting from the base class GenericService:Service.
Subclass are required to override the functions sendMessage() and streamMessage().
Referring to the GenericService::Service base class, the non-streaming remote procedure call
sendMessage() accepts a GenericMessage input and then sets the pointer to the resultant GenericResponse
instance. The operation blocks until the pointer is set. A grpc::Status instance is returned indicating the
condition of the operation. The streaming operation, streamMessage(), returns an instance of
gprc:ServerReaderWriter<W,R> (specifically an instance pointer), which is an implementation of the
ServerReaderWriterInterface<W,R> interface and is used by the service to manage the data stream. It
allows the service to read GenericMessage instances and write GenericResponse instances as responses.
Asynchronous Communication
Services must inherit from base class GenericService::AsyncService to implement asynchronous
communication. Note from Figure 78 that AsyncService inherits from Service, the synchronous base class
which includes the synchronous operations sendMessage() and streamMessage(). However, the
AsyncService class override these operations replacing them with exception throwing implementations by
default. AsyncService child classes can implement alternative behavior.
Seen in Figure 78, asynchronous service operations are always prefixed with the Request label prepended
to the original remote procedure call identifier. In our case the asynchronous operations are identified by
RequestsendMessage() and RequeststreamMessage() functions within the GenericService::AsyncService
class. Asynchronous service communications requires the use of grpc::ServerCompletionQueue instances,
which are a subclasses of the grpc::CompletionQueue class. The same ServerCompletionQueue instance can
be used for both the CompletionQueue and ServerCompletionQueue typed arguments. As analogous to the
client side, the ServerCompletionQueue is used to manage the data stream represented by
ServerAsyncReaderWriter<W,R> instance retrieved from the RequeststreamMessage() operation, and also the
ServerAsyncResponseWriter<W> instance retrieved from the RequestsendMessage() operation. Again, the
asynchronous streaming service communications involve the use of tags to keep track of individual
messages within the data stream.
Servers
Launching a gRPC service, either synchronous or asynchronous, requires the creation of a grpc::Server
instance. Additionally, a grpc::ServerBuilder instance is required to create a Server instance. The
ServerBuilder class is defined in header file grpcpp/server_builder.h. Instances of ServerBuilder can
be created with the available default constructor. The server builder facilitates the configuration of various
server parameters, such as compression, maximum message size, and timeout limits. It also attaches the
service to the hosting server with the RegisterService() function, where it is assigned to a port address.
Server security credentials are required to register a service with a ServerBuilder instance. As with client
credentials, security credentials are identified by type and credential classes inherit from the base class
grpc::ServerCredentials. The file grpccp/security/server_credentials.h contains functions for

 - 57 -

creating server credentials, as indicated in Figure 78. The diagram explicitly shows the function
SslServerCredentials() for creating Secure Socket Layer (SSL) credentials. Note that this function
requires and argument of type SslServerCredentialsOptions for specifying SSL parameters. Other
credential types follow a similar implementation. Insecure servers can be created using credentials from
the InsecureServerCredentials() function.

 Figure 78: proto example - GenericService.proto service C++ implementation

As mentioned, the listening port for the service is also assigned by the builder. The builder can also create
grpc::ServerCompletionQueue instances, which are required to implement asynchronous communication
within the registered service. Note that most configuration functions within the ServerBuilder class return
its this pointer. Thus, configuration operations can be chained.
Once the server is configured by the builder, the service is launched with the BuildAndStart() operation.
This operation returns a pointer to the hosting server instance. The service can be explicitly shut down
using the Server class Shutdown() function.

 - 58 -

7. Summary
From the above examples we see that gRPC implementations can vary greatly depending upon the choice
of programming language. The Python realization appears simplest. This is due to the high-level nature of
the language and the loose typing. Since Python is scripted, resources are bound at run time and do not
need to be explicitly identified. The Java realization has mid-level complexity. Java is a strongly typed,
compiled language, but has many high-level features. Most gRPC resources are accessible in Java, however,
at added complexity. All resources must be explicitly identified at compile time. The C++ implementation
exhibits the most complexity and requires significant gRPC familiarity for both clients and services. This
is especially obvious when directly inspecting the protobuf generated source code, the diagrams presented
above only capture the minimum required for application. Note that although C++ is object-oriented, it is
relatively low-level, providing the closest proximity to the supporting platform. Thus, although complex,
C++ provides the tightest control over the underlying gRPC processes and resources.
It should be again noted that gRPC implementations are platform and language independent. For example,
a service implemented in C++ and hosted on a Unix platform is available to any client, on any platform,
implemented in any language. Thus, for performance, control, and maintainability, one may wish to
implement a service in C++, which is compiled into native executable binary. However, a client
unconcerned with performance, may choose to build the corresponding gRPC interface in Python, offering
the greatest ease in implementation.
